<div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-40"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="40" data-folio="1" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n39_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 1">[p. 1]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1">THE ENGLISH FACTORIES IN INDIA</h2><h3 class="sec-h2">1622–23</h3><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Henry Crosby’s</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-001" href="#sec-04-fn-001" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-001" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <span class="sc">Journal of the Cruise against the Portuguese, 1621–23</span> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xxix. p. 17).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1621, <em>October</em> 8. The fleet left Batavia. <em>November</em> 12. Captain Bennet died. <em>November</em> 20 (?). Anchored at the Nassau Islands.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-002" href="#sec-04-fn-002" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-002" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> <em>December</em> 5. Sailed again. The Dutch sent back to Batavia the <em>Enkhuisen</em>, ‘being of no force and did go badly; and the <em>Morning Stare</em> they had sent awaye before from the ilands at the wester end of Java, being ould and leake, and not able to performe the voidage.’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1622, <em>February</em> 6. General consultation. The Dutch resolved to send two of their ships (the <em>Arms of Zealand</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-003" href="#sec-04-fn-003" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-003" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> and the <em>North Holland</em>) to the Red Sea, in spite of a protest from Capt. Fitzherbert. <em>February</em> 17. Death of Capt. Fitzherbert. <em>February</em> 18. He was buried at sea. Michael Green elected to succeed him. <em>February</em> 19. Green’s appointment being disputed by Goodall, the question was referred to the Dutch admiral, who decided for the former. <em>February</em> 27. Anchored at Mohilla, in the Comoro Islands, near the Queen’s town. ‘Her house is like a cloystar, and the othear houses of lyme and stoane biult like to the Porttinggayles biulding.’ <em>February</em> 28. Boats were sent ashore, but they could not trade, as the people demanded calicoes, of which their ship had none, the stock being on board the <em>Exchange</em> and <em>Anne</em>. These two vessels and the <em>South Holland</em> had failed to get into the bay, and so had been obliged to go for Mayotta. ‘At the Queens towne they wear content to take cloth or ryals, but not bells nor paper, but for palme wine or oringges or lymes they would pase; <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-41" data-page="41" data-folio="2" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n40_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 2">[p. 2]</button> so our boates and the <em>Exchanges</em> barge did go to the Queens towne ... and ... did bring beefs aboard of us.' <em>March</em> 1. The anchor came home and the ship drifted out of the bay. <em>March</em> 3. They got into the road of Mohilla. <em>March</em> 10. The King came down and was presented with china plates and pots, spices, wine, rackapee, gunpowder, a sword, and some paper painted red on one side. 'The shreeve<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-004" href="#sec-04-fn-004" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-004" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> that was with him had some of the spice and the chinna trenchers. The King and the shreeve and the Kings two sonns stayed; and every daye he must have wine ashoare.' <em>March</em> 27. Letters sent on land for the King to deliver to any ship arriving from England. <em>April</em> 2. They sailed. <em>April</em> 14. Joined their consorts at Mayotta. <em>June</em> 5. The fleet sailed. <em>June</em> 12. A small ship chased into a harbour to the north of Mozambique, where she was captured and her goods shared. <em>June</em> 16. The Dutch ship <em>Hart</em>, having run ashore in the chase, and being very old and leaky, was stripped and burnt, as also the prize. <em>June</em> 18. They anchored at the entrance to Mozambique. <em>June</em> 23. Sailed southwards to look for some Portuguese carracks that were expected. <em>July</em> 13. 'We did spie the carricks, being three, and one ship.' <em>July</em> 14. 'We did fight with theam, and that night the vizamberall did rune in neare the shoare, and thear they did more by two ankers; and the next morning all thear masts wear overboard.' <em>July</em> 15. 'We did follow the othear two carricks and the ship; which the one carrick was the amberall and the othear the rare amberall, as we did judge; and in the afternoune we did fight with them and spoiled the rare amberall very much and the amberall likewise, tell they wear at the Ilands of Saint Jackes.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-005" href="#sec-04-fn-005" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-005" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> The amberall did goe ashoare on the iner iland and the rare amberall on the point within.' <em>July</em> 16. 'We did gett up to the vice amberall to se what we could gett, and as some of the ships came neare the cavaleres had a boat readie with 40 chests of ryalls in, and so went awaye close by the shoare to Mozambeque, and left all the rest aboard the ship allmost sunk, ryding in 8 fatham water; and as soone as I came aboard I did gett a Duchman to me and did cut the cables and lett her drive aground, or else <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-42" data-page="42" data-folio="3" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n41_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 3">[p. 3]</button> we had not saved the money that was saved. But the same daye we did se two sayle, the one being a carrick, the othear a ship ’; these, however, made for Madagascar and escaped. <em>July</em> 17. The <em>Exchange</em> was sent to the wreck of the Portuguese vice-admiral, ‘but thear was nothing to be seene of hir.’ <em>July</em> 23. ‘We saved som cloth and wine and oyle that was in the amberall.’ <em>July</em> 24. The fleet sailed for the Comoro Islands. <em>July</em> 27. Anchored at Mohilla. <em>August</em> 10. Captured an Arab junk laden with sandalwood. <em>August</em> 15. The fleet sailed to the NNE. <em>September</em> 15. Reached Chaul, having lost the company of the <em>Fortune</em> and the <em>South Holland</em> on the way. <em>September</em> 22. Started for Dābhol, look for the rest of the fleet, but were stopped by unfavourable winds. <em>September</em> 27. The <em>Diamond</em> captured a small junk laden with coco-nuts. <em>October</em> 2. They got some oxen, &amp;c., near ‘Rogpoore’ [Rājpuri]. ‘It is some 4 or 5 leages to the southward of Choule. It hath a little round iland in the middle going in [Janjīrā], with a castell uppon it and some peeces of ordinance in it.’ <em>October</em> 3. The <em>Diamond</em> sent to Dābhol to look for the Dutch ships. <em>October</em> 12. On her reporting that they were not at that port, the fleet sailed for Surat. <em>October</em> 18. Off Bassein they met the Dutch squadron of three vessels (<em>Fortune</em>, <em>South Holland</em>, and <em>Arms of Zealand</em>) and a pinnace. They had been at Surat, but as they, as well as the English, were short of provisions, it was decided to make for that port. <em>October</em> 25. Anchored at Swally, where they found the <em>Blessing</em>, <em>Discovery</em>, and <em>Reformation</em>, newly arrived from England. <em>November</em> 22. Sailed again to the southwards. <em>December</em> 1. Arrived at the entrance to Goa. ‘On the south syde of the going into Goa the northear land is called Bardes, whearon is a fort alowe by the water syde, which the carricks do ryde under commaund of. It is bilt of some red earth or stoane doth make it sheaw red ; and to the southward of the cloyster theare is Ould Gooa trenteth up.’ <em>December</em> 3. Chased a ship ashore and sent men to plunder her. The Portuguese blew her up by means of a train of gunpowder, but only one man was hurt. <em>December</em> 4. Joined the rest of the fleet, who were waiting for a carrack that was riding under a strong fort. <em>December</em> 7. ‘The Viceroye that we had fougt with at Mozambeque, that was in the carricke we did fight with, came from Quchcheen [Cochin] to Gooa with gallies <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-43" data-page="43" data-folio="4" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n42_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 4">[p. 4]</button> and galliots and friggets som 160 or 170 sayles.'<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-006" href="#sec-04-fn-006" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-006" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <em>December 19.</em> The <em>Discovery</em>, <em>Diamond</em>, and <em>Rose</em> arrived from Surat, and brought letters from two Dutch ships that had recently come from Batavia. <em>December 26.</em> The <em>Discovery</em> sailed for Batavia. <em>December 29.</em> The <em>Rose</em> went to Surat to fetch provisions.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1623, <em>January 3.</em> The <em>Diamond</em> and <em>North Holland</em> went to a place six or seven leagues north of Goa, called Bandey, 'to the northwards of the [Vengurla?] rocks that ly some 9 leages to the northwards of Gooa,' but failed to get provisions owing to threats used by the Portuguese to the natives. The latter sent messengers offering, if the fleet would help them by sea, to attack Goa by land, and also promised to obtain the release of four Dutchmen imprisoned at Dābhol. <em>February 2.</em> A messenger arrived from Cannanore requesting passes for two vessels to go to Surat, and offering provisions, if the fleet would repair to their port. 'But the black sayde the Portingall had a castell with 40 peece of ordinaunce.' <em>February 14.</em> 'The caffealo [<em>see previous volume</em>, <em>p. 31, note</em>] of friggets came out of Camebaye to Gooa, which went awaye about the 12 of Jannuary and passed by Gooa and did goe to the southward.' <em>March 9.</em> 'We came awaye from Gooa. And while we did ryde theare we had boats come ofe for the clearing of the Portingalls we had in the fleet; but they had but 6 Dutch, which the amberall did offer 24 or 26 of the Portingalls for them. . . . They came no more ofe, for I did think they had hanged them up, as we did heare by the blacks.'<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-007" href="#sec-04-fn-007" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-007" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> At night reached an island called 'Deaw' [Anjidīv], sixteen leagues from Goa, in lat. 14° 45′. <em>March 20.</em> Left the island, after obtaining water there, and provisions from the mainland. At night were off 'Onnoore'.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-008" href="#sec-04-fn-008" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-008" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> 'The Portingalls have a smale fort uppon the mayne.' <em>March 21.</em> Passed 'Battacelley' [Bhatkal]. <em>March 24.</em> Anchored off 'Mountadillye' [Mount Delly or d'Ely]. 'Thear is a road that is good for junks and frigets to ryde in, and a great towne ashore.' <em>March 25.</em> Arrived at Cannanore, and obtained some provisions. <em>March 27.</em> 'We did se a fleet of frigets, and the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-44" data-page="44" data-folio="5" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n43_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 5">[p. 5]</button> <em>North Holland</em> and the <em>Diamond</em> did go to meet with theam, but could doe no good.' <em>March</em> 28. Sailed again. <em>March</em> 29. Anchored near Calicut. <em>April 1</em>. Went to 'Panneare' [Ponānī]. <em>April</em> 6. Passed Cape Comorin. <em>April 10</em>. Rounded Ceylon. <em>May</em> 27. 'We heard such a rumbling as though the ship had rubed hir biulge agaynst some corall banke, and presently was gon agayne'; as they could find no trace of ground, they concluded it to be the effects of an earthquake. <em>May 31</em>. Saw 'Anganya' [Engano, on the south-west side of Sumatra]. <em>June</em> 9. Reached Pulo Panjang, off Bantam, and were met by the Dutch <em>Wezel</em>. <em>June</em> 10. Reached Batavia. (27 <em>pp. The rest of the journal is occupied with the homeward voyage of the Exchange, December, 1623–June, 1624.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">ANOTHER ACCOUNT OF THE SAME CRUISE<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-009" href="#sec-04-fn-009" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-009" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xxxi).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1621, <em>October 8</em>. Sailed from Batavia. <em>November 20</em>. Anchored at the Nassau Islands, where they remained till December 5, and then sailed for 'Moroseses [Mauritius] or the Englands Forrest '.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-010" href="#sec-04-fn-010" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-010" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1622, <em>February 22</em>. Saw two ships, but could not fetch them up. <em>February 27</em>. Part of the fleet got into Mohilla, but the <em>Exchange</em>, the <em>Anne</em>, and the <em>Holland</em> failed to do so, and had to make for 'the Mayotes' [Mayotta], where they anchored two days later. <em>June 5</em>. Sailed for the mainland. <em>June 11</em>. Chased a ship but lost her. <em>June 12</em>. Found that the ship had run into a harbour, where the crew deserted her, leaving nine children on board. The fleet went in and took possession of her; in so doing the <em>Hart</em> ran ashore. The harbour was called 'Sama Juell ',<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-011" href="#sec-04-fn-011" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-011" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> and lies about eight leagues north of Mozambique. <em>June 18</em>. Saw Mozambique Castle. <em>June 19</em>. Anchored in the Roads. <em>June 23</em>. Sailed again. <em>July 13</em>.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-45"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="45" data-folio="6" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n44_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 6">[p. 6]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Met three carracks and a galleon, and fought with them from ‘daybrighte’ till dark. ‘Spoyled’ the Portuguese vice-admiral. ‘At midnight the rere-ambrall laid us abord, but did us not much hurt but killed us one man.’ <em>July</em> 14. Saw the vice-admiral at anchor near the shore, with all her masts overboard. Chased the other three into Mozambique and then stood to the southwards ‘to get the vise-admirall’. <em>July</em> 15. Anchored near the latter and found her a wreck, having received thirty shot below water. Some of the crew got ashore on rafts or in boats. At four in the afternoon saw a carrack and a galleon. Left the <em>Diamond</em> by the wreck and chased the newcomers, but lost them in the night. <em>July</em> 17. The bulk of the fleet went towards Mozambique. The <em>Exchange</em> visited the place where the vice-admiral was left, but found her sunk. <em>July</em> 19. Sailed for Mozambique, in company with the <em>Diamond</em>. <em>July</em> 20. At Mozambique found the two other carracks, one sunk on the eastern end of the island, the other ashore near the castle. In the whole of the fighting the English lost but five or six men. <em>July</em> 23. The fleet set sail. <em>July</em> 26. Anchored at Mohilla, finding there the <em>Claw</em>. <em>August</em> 10. Made prize of a small junk that came in, laden with sandalwood and slaves. The English took fifty-four of the latter, the Dutch the same, and the King the rest, with the junk. <em>August</em> 15. Sailed again. <em>August</em> 27. Saw the African coast. <em>August</em> 31. The <em>Claw</em> was abandoned, being too leaky to continue the voyage. The same night the <em>Exchange</em> lost the rest of the fleet, with the exception of the <em>Anne</em>. <em>September</em> 1. The <em>Diamond</em> joined them. <em>September</em> 15. Reached the coast of India near Chaul. <em>September</em> 18. A ‘Portingall’ came aboard and told them of the capture of Ormus. <em>September</em> 23. Overhauled a junk belonging to Socotra, but released her. <em>October</em> 3. The <em>Diamond</em> was sent to Dābhol. <em>October</em> 18. Met the Dutch off Bassein. <em>October</em> 21. The English sailed for Surat, the Dutch staying to send a pinnace to Batavia. <em>October</em> 24. Went over the bar at Swally, and found there ten English ships. <em>November</em> 19. Went to Surat Road. <em>November</em> 22. Anchored off Surat River. <em>November</em> 23. Set sail. <em>November</em> 24. Dogged by twenty Portuguese frigates from Damān. <em>December</em> 1. Arrived off Goa. <em>December</em> 3. Chased a Portuguese ship ashore, but her crew fired her. <em>December</em> 20. The <em>Discovery</em>, <em>Diamond</em>, and <em>Rose</em> arrived <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-46" data-page="46" data-folio="7" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n45_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 7">[p. 7]</button> from Surat. <em>December</em> 23. The <em>Hope</em> and a pinnace joined. <em>December</em> 28. The <em>Discovery</em> sailed for Batavia. <em>December</em> 29. The <em>Rose</em> was dispatched to Surat for stores. 1623, <em>February</em> 25. The <em>Rose</em> returned with provisions, &amp;c. <em>February</em> 28. The <em>North Holland</em> and <em>Diamond</em> went to ‘Vangerlee’ with letters to ‘the Kinge of the countrye’. <em>March</em> 2. They returned with a present from the Governor. <em>March</em> 7. The Portuguese dismantled their carrack and towed her in over the bar. <em>March</em> 9. The fleet sailed from Goa. <em>March</em> 10. Anchored at the island of ‘Dew’ [<em>see</em> p. 4]. <em>March</em> 18. Captured two boats, one laden with ‘paddie’. <em>March</em> 20. Sailed. <em>March</em> 25. Anchored at Cannanore. <em>March</em> 28. Departed. <em>March</em> 29. Anchored near Calicut, where they remained three days. <em>May</em> 27. A great earthquake felt at midnight. <em>May</em> 31. Saw Engano. <em>June</em> 10. Reached Batavia. (52 <em>pp. In bad condition. The journal contains also the ship's outward and homeward voyages.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Richard Swanley's</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-012" href="#sec-04-fn-012" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-012" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <span class="sc">Journal of the Voyage to Persia</span> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xxxiv. p. 31).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1621, <em>November</em> 7. The <em>Jonas</em> and <em>Dolphin</em> quitted Port Swally, and anchored outside. <em>November</em> 20. ‘Ther came by us the Portugall caffela, being bound for Cambaya, being to the number of 100 friggatts with other boatts. Soe the <em>Lyon</em>, <em>Sampson</em>, and <em>Richard</em> wayed and gave chase after them. So when they came within shott of them, the Duchman sent two boatts off from his shipp, making accounte to have taken some of them; but falling fowle of two or three which wer waiters to the fleett, [they?] tooke one of his boats with ten of his men, one of them being the marchant of his shipp.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-013" href="#sec-04-fn-013" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-013" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> <em>November</em> 21. The whole fleet anchored off the Bar of Surat. <em>November</em> 25. The President and other merchants were set ashore, and the fleet sailed. <em>November</em> 27. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-47" data-page="47" data-folio="8" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n46_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 8">[p. 8]</button> The Dutch <em>Sampson</em> departed for the southwards, and the <em>Hart</em> and <em>Roebuck</em> for England. <em>December</em> 1. Death of Mr. Jeffries aboard the <em>Whale</em>. <em>December 14</em>. Anchored in the Road of Jask. <em>December 16</em>. Sailed for Kuhistak. <em>December 23</em>. Anchored at that place.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1622, <em>January</em> 19. Set sail from Kuhistak, and anchored about four leagues from Ormus. <em>January</em> 22. Stood near the castle, and saw five great galleons there, with two small ships, some galliots, frigates and galleys. <em>January</em> 23. As the Portuguese made no signs of coming out, the fleet stood over to Kishm Castle and anchored. <em>February</em> 4. It was decided to send the <em>Lion</em>, <em>Rose</em>, and <em>Richard</em> to Surat. (6 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes and Robert Hutchinson at</span> ‘NAWPEE’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-014" href="#sec-04-fn-014" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-014" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <span class="sc">to the Surat Factory, January</span> 2, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 69).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">· Have sold two dubbas<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-015" href="#sec-04-fn-015" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-015" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> of coral to certain banians at Rs. 9½ the seer of 14 pice, ready money, and have nearly concluded a bargain for the rest at the same price; the matter has however been delayed by the sickness of ‘Emipaul Singur’ [Amīpāl Singar], the principal merchant concerned. Have also got rid of their stock of elephants’ teeth at Rs. 32 per maund of 43 seer. ‘Rampaxad’ [Ramprasād] promises to pay the amount due from his father, but claims to set off certain payments alleged to have been made in Agra; the matter has been left for Bangham to settle. After making over charge to the latter, on December 31 they started from Burhānpur for Surat, in company with Biddulph, who had arrived three days before, bringing news of the dispatch from Agra of a caravan of 194 camels under the charge of John Young. Thomas Hawkridge has been ordered to meet the latter. When the caravan arrives at Burhānpur, Young will be sent back to Agra, while Hawkridge and ‘Scander’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-016" href="#sec-04-fn-016" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-016" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> will accompany the goods to Surat. Offers for their amber. Hope to be at Surat within fifteen days. (<em>Copy. 1¾ pp. Received January 10.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-48"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="48" data-folio="9" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n47_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 9">[p. 9]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Same, January</span> 2, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 40).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Martin and the other factors deputed to Baroda found Rustam Khān still there, and so were able to deliver Is-hāq Beg’s letter to him. After viewing the present they had brought, he questioned them about the stay of their goods at Ahmadābād, and finally caused his ‘monsee’ [<em>munshī</em>] to write a parwāna [order] to Safī Khān to release them. This document the factors at once sent to Ahmadābād. Of the money brought by Robert Tottle, some of the mahmūdīs will not pass and are therefore returned; also the ‘abraims’ [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 351] are deficient in weight by 2½ ‘gadians.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-017" href="#sec-04-fn-017" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-017" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Have heard nothing yet about the indigo. To-day they delivered the Surat letter to the Governor and begged his permission to buy narrow baftas of the sorts he does not want (‘for that hee hath forbidden the buying of any untill his torne bee served’); but he would give no immediate answer. (<em>Copy. 2 pp. Received January</em> 3.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes and John Parker at Agra to the Same, January</span> 3, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 63).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Particulars of goods and accounts forwarded. They send for information several samples of cloth usually bought by native merchants for the Persian market. Recovery of certain debts. ‘Mr. Yonge and his assistant is arrived in Samana [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. xxi] and adviseth off good hoopes for a sodaine dispatch in those this yeares insuinge investments, and for 10,000 rup[ees] more to bee remitted them. But wee cannot gett takers for soe manny; soe have remitted them 4,000 rup[ees] and advised them to charge us from thence, where by there owne reports they can take itt upp att as easie rates as wee can heare.’ As regards the camelmen who ran away when the English caravan under their charge was plundered near Māndū, they have already recovered 1,260 rupees from them, and hope shortly to receive a further sum, ‘haveinge gotten more off them into our prison and made them acknowledge there debt’. Have sold the best of the silk from Patna at 5¼ rupees the seer, which is 15 per cent more than it <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-49" data-page="49" data-folio="10" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n48_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 10">[p. 10]</button> cost. Request a supply of broadcloth, &amp;c. <em>Enclosed.</em> A list of cattle belonging to the Agra Factory and its dependencies, viz. five horses, one mare, and two teams of oxen for their two coaches. (<em>Copy.</em> 2¾ <em>pp. Received January 21.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">JAMES BICKFORD, NATHANIEL HALSTEAD, WILLIAM HILL, AND JOHN CLARK AT AHMADĀBĀD TO THE SURAT FACTORY, JANUARY 4, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 46).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last on December 29, ‘sence haveinge receaved one from Mr. Martin from Brodera, togeather with Rustom Cauns<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-018" href="#sec-04-fn-018" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-018" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> perwanna to Saffy Caun for lycence to lade our goods. Wee perceave you ordered him to followe our infernall foe, the mischeefous Governor of this place, with a piskesh [Pers. <em>pīsh-kash</em>, a present to a great man] to that effect, the yssue whereof hath showed the cleane contrary; for after wee had vizitted him in his retorne hither betweenc Battaa and Conniz<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-019" href="#sec-04-fn-019" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-019" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, and followed them both (being togeather uppon one eliphant) into the towne, they both retired themselves to there privacy, soe that wee could not then speake with them; but this morninge delivered Saffy Caun the writtinge of this Governor, for pirwan wee cannot call it, beinge onely a complymentall letter, feigned in our favour but in conclusion therof reeferrs it wholy to the will and pleasure of our oppositt to doe as hee list, acknowledge[ing ?] it to be his busines, wherin hee himselfe knewe best what hee had to doe. This courteous favour of our Governor receaved itts wellcome with as slight regard as were the contents of value, and wee his peremtory answere that if wee brought him twenty such perwannore more wee should not expect to lade any of our goods hence or from Cambaia till hee were satisfied his larres [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 227]. Thus you see whereunto wee are driven, and wee much feare our goods will hardly com tyme enough to bee dispeeded this yeare for England; which will cost our maisters more then 23,000 larrees will satisfy. Wee cannot writ you more then alreadie wee have done, Saffy Caun <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-50" data-page="50" data-folio="11" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n49_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 11">[p. 11]</button> and this late retourned extortinge curr beinge al one.' They trust the Surat Council will take speedy measures for compounding with the Governor. <em>P.S.</em>—The late unexpected rains have not damaged their cotton cloths as much as was feared. They have been whited afresh and repacked. (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp. Received January 7.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, January</span> 5, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 50).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Think that Safī Khān will insist on satisfaction. Rustam Khān sent for them and inquired why they did not pay the amount; they denied any knowledge of the matter, and again requested leave to lade their goods. His 'short and absolute answare was (to the noe little content of our adversary, Coja Nessan [Khwāja Nizām ?] there present) that wee should not expect to lade one fardle of our goods hence till that monny were satisfied.' This is the fruit of the present sent to him at Baroda. Request a supply of money; they are in debt and unable to send any to Lancaster. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received January 17.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes and John Parker at Agra to the Surat Factory, January</span> 5, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 66).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">'This morninge contrarye to expectacion came into our howse the Governor and the Califas<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-020" href="#sec-04-fn-020" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-020" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> officers of this place, who brought a mandate or perwanna from the Nabob Assaf Conn, writen in two coppyes, to the Governour of this place and his owne vekile [Arabic <em>wakīl</em>, 'agent'], the coppyes whereof wee send you here inclosed and importes as followeth, viz. to make seazure of whatsoever goods, redie monnyes, moveables, writings, &amp;c., and to take them into there possesion; which mandate of Asuff Cons the Governor and officers of this place have most officiouslye executed, and farther have made seasure off our persons, which is as farr as the Nabobs perwanna importes, and is the ultimo of there and our owne expectacions. The reasons which moved the Kinge, or rather Asuf Con, hereto are (soe farr as wee cann perceave) the Sindiemens exclamacions against us for there losses sustaned by us; but the reports are later busines of exclamations lately made att court by sundry bannians off Cambaya, whoe pretendes that <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-51" data-page="51" data-folio="12" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n50_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 12">[p. 12]</button> wee have this yeare robed them off noe lesse then fourscore lacks off rupp[ee]s; ROW_BREAK whereof ourselves are alltogeather ignorant, as not havinge hard from you since the first arrivall of the fleete. Yt should seem that this mandate rests not here with us only, but proceeds through all the partes off India; which causes us expressly to advise you hereof, that you may remeade both yourselves and us accordinglye, as not havinge any reamidie here att present; and the best and exquisite at present (as not havinge English att lesker <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-021" href="#sec-04-fn-021" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-021" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>) is in our opinions to put upp our petition unto Asuf Caun, importinge the hard measure delte us by him on noe groundes (as wee perceive and maye gather it), which tomorrow wee will put in practice; and if noe redrese thereby, wee must expect it from you, wee beinge att instant prizoned with our masters goods and knowe not justly from whence to seeke redresse unlesse from patience and sufferance, which according to our masters instructions wee shall bare. Howe these trobles wilbee pasified wee refer unto you to conjecture on, and the detraction in our future affairs, ourselves being resolved to stand it out to the utmost and will not com unto any agreement until order and approbation from you, ourselves and creditors being bound to suffer the utmost of there tyranny.' Have warned the factors at Samāna and Burhānpur. (<em>Copy, in bad condition. 1½ pp. Received January 21.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same</span>, January 6, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 98).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Yesterday sent two expresses, by way of Burhānpur and Ahmadābād respectively, with the following letter [<em>already given</em>]. 'Wee have made meanes to the Governor of this place to grant our lycence to repayre to the lesker, their to be ready to answeare what alligacions maye trewly be layd to our charge, but we ar absolutely denied to goe for courte untill further order from thence; and these officers alreadye have advised thether how far they have proceeded with us, and abide further hackum [Hind. <em>hukm</em>, 'an order'], untill when they say wee must have patience. Otheir reportes [    ] have risen upp, and is that Melickamber should wright to the Prince in Brampore against us for taking his shipp this yeare, that the Prince should send advise to the Kinge theirof <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-52" data-page="52" data-folio="13" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n51_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 13">[p. 13]</button> and for his approbacion for the banishinge us his countrye. How certayne this is we are ignorante, and if any such motione have passed att Brampore noe doubte but our frinds their and yourselves are beter acquented theirwith then us ; and if they should all prove but reportes, wee hould it not amisse that you procure testiment under the Governors and officers hands of Surratt, Cambaya, &amp;c., for our better redresse att courte ; as also it behoves you to thinke on som course for the sattisfyeinge these clamyrouse Syndamen, for nowe they have wherwithall in their owne possesions to sattisfye themselves.’ <em>P.S.</em>—Have just heard by a . ‘gelopdare’ [Pers. <em>jilaudār</em>, ‘a servant who leads a horse’] that the caravan has safely arrived at ‘Surungee’ [Sironj]. (<em>Copy. 2 pp. Received February</em> 1.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, January</span> 11, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 100).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Now haveinge this otherr convayance proffered, wee may not omitt to write you, although wee have yet found noe alteracion in our disturbance ; only have procured (upon suertys) lybertye for our persons, our goods restinge in the same order as formerly. As determyned, wee have put upp our arzdasht [petition] to courte, the coppie wherof wee send you heerinclosed [<em>not entered</em>]. This barer is the Dutches expresse, who two dayes sence arryved heere from Surratt, and brought them newes (which they reporte to us) of your stopeinge the Princes shipp this yeare ; which, as it seemed from them, is agayne released.’ Hope to hear soon from them. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received February</em> 4.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Agreement between the English and the Khān of Shīrāz for the Capture of Ormus, January,</span> 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Java</em>, vol. iii. pt. i. p. 301).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-022" href="#sec-04-fn-022" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-022" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘The condition of this writinge is such as followeth, and dated <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-53" data-page="53" data-folio="14" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n52_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 14">[p. 14]</button> the 8th of the month Rebel-awall, in the yeare of Mahomett 1031 <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-023" href="#sec-04-fn-023" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-023" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, agreed and concluded betwene our deputies and the English Company conserninge the affayres of Jeroone <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-024" href="#sec-04-fn-024" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-024" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and the ports, accordinge to the underwritten articles, which are on both sides to be dulie kept and performed without defaulte. First, that when by the power of God the cuntrie of Jeroone shalbe possessed by the subjects of His Majestie, whatsoever monyes, goods, ettc., shalbe taken in citty, castle, ships and howses, the one haulfe shalbe ours and the other halfe the English Companys; and on neither part shall thear be any thinge kept secrett; and if on ether part any suspition that ought be hiden or kept backe, theare shalbe an oath administred to each one accordinge to theare religion. And the proffitt of the customes and shippinge shall likewise be eaqually devided, provided that the English Company doe without defaulte ayde in takeinge of the castells and citties. 2. That if it please God that Jeroone be conquered, that a governour of ours and an other governour of the English remaine in the castle, and shall joyntly ayde and asist on the other in all accurranses and accidents, smale or great; and without consent on both sides ought nott [<em>sic</em>] to be performed. And unles I for the said Company sake doe make request unto His Majestie that the castle be delivered to the English; and if His Majestie grant my request and give them the castle, accordinge to His Hightnes command it shalbe delivered unto them; elce nott. 3. That whatt profitt shall arrise from the ships of the said Company, what soe ever appertayneth unto them our deputies shall nott demand any thinge, ether by pretence of customes or other duties; and what shipinge shalbe imployed of ours, what profitt shall arise to us shalbe our owne, and they by pretence of customes ettc. shall nott demand ought. And for other goods of marchants and traffikers, wheather in theare ships or ours, what profitt shall arrise theareby shalbe equally devided on both parts. And for other ships of the sea or of the ports of India,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-025" href="#sec-04-fn-025" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-025" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> what shall proceed from them by proffitt or pillage, the same likewise as the former shalbe equallie devided betweene both.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-54"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="54" data-folio="15" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n53_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 15">[p. 15]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">4. And conserninge the captives, for such as be Musslemen, wheather they be prisoners of that Company or our captives, the English shall nott meadle with them, butt deliver them to us; butt if Christians they shall accordinglie be theares. And conserning Captain Moore <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-026" href="#sec-04-fn-026" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-026" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and the Captain of Jeroone, it is agreed if the[y] happen to be the captives of the English, our deputies shall nott medle thearein, butt if they become our captives and in our pocession, advice of them shalbe given to His Majestie, and what he commands shalbe performed, and the English shall nott meddle about theare libertie. 5. Touchinge any the ports of India or the ports of Schaure <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-027" href="#sec-04-fn-027" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-027" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and Musscatt ettc., any that heareafter be taken betwene us and the Company of English shalbe equall partners thearein. And if the English shall goe with intention to take any port of India, and give notice theareof to our deputies, what the[y] desire (ayde off men and other helpe) the[y] shalbe assisted by Gods permission to accomplish theare victory. And when conquered, if the governour theareoff be a Christian and have a castle, accordinge to agreement aforsaid is to be equally devided; butt if theare be a castle in the suburbs or withoutt, apart the cittie, itt shalbe wholie to the deputies of theese parts and the said Company shall nott have to doe thearewith. Butt if the English conquor any place and nott acquainte our deputies thearewith according to agreement, the said place or cuntrie shalbe theare owne; butt if acquainted us thearewith and have ayde from hence, the same shalbe half in halfe betweene. And in what place which shalbe taken the prayers and cerimonyes of His Majestie of Persia shalbe celibrated, and his stampe uppon the coyne. 6. That soe longe as the English be imployed or excersized in the takinge of Jeroone, whatt soever shalbe theare expence, viz. of powder and shott, the charge shalbe equally devided betwene us and the English; butt after victorie the expence of theare ships shalbe wholly borne by themselves and nott conserne our deputies. And provitions of victualls for the said Company in the time of war shalbe borne on both parts, viz. of what is necessarie of beeves and sheep and hens <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-55" data-page="55" data-folio="16" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n54_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 16">[p. 16]</button> and butter and meale ettc. ; which ended, each [such ?] perticoulur expences shalbe borne onely by themselves. 7. And after the victory of Jeroone soe many or such ships as shall remayne to secure the castle and the passages and mayntaine peace and good order in the sea, what shalbe the said ships expences shalbe borne equallie on both parts. 8. Thatt the Christians doe nott force nor wronge unto the Mahamettans ; and if the men slaves and weomen slaves shall goe to the English, they shall nott make them Christians, butt restore them to their ownors. And if Christians shall come or flie tow Mussellmen, they shall accordinglie restore them to the English. And in the frendlie assistance each of the other they shall nott faile.'<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-028" href="#sec-04-fn-028" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-028" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (<em>Copy. 2½ pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-56"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="56" data-folio="17" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n55_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 17">[p. 17]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2">PRESIDENT FURSLAND AND COUNCIL AT BATAVIA TO THE COMPANY, JANUARY 11, 1622 (<em>O.C.</em> 1028).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-029" href="#sec-04-fn-029" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-029" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>‘. . . The 14th of the laste mounthe arrived the Duches pynnace the <em>Cleine Anchuisen</em> from the Goa fleete, beinge such a lewarde bauble that shee could not keepe the seae. Shee lefte the fleete the 4th of December at the Islands of Nasau [<em>see</em> p. 1]; and by letters receaved from Captaine Fitzharbert wee perceave they have loste bothe theire monsone and voyage, and that throughe the wilfullnes (or unskillfullnes) of the Dutche Admirall, as appeares by Captaines Phitzharberts relatione. Yet the saide Admirall persistes in his oppinione to proceede uppon the voyadge, thoughe litle reasone can bee alleadged by hime for takeinge of such a course, and small hope there is of effectinge anie thinge accordinge to our purposes in followinge that course resolved uppon by him and his adherents. Captaine Fitzharbert opposed the resolutione, but the Admiralls double voice carried the stroke.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-030" href="#sec-04-fn-030" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-030" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> And wee doe not wonder at theise proceedinges, for, havinge more shipps then they knowe howe to imploie, it is better for them to proceede then to retorne and lye heere in the roade ; for nowe they make accompte you shall paie parte of theire charges, which they will bee suer to make lardge enoughe. Wee wishe (since it is soe fallen out) that the fleete had retorned, for wee could have gevene our shippes better imployment, espetially the <em>Whale</em> and <em>Tryall</em> (soe longe expected) not beinge yet arrived ; which bringes us into an unlooked for stoppe in our proceedinges.’ Send copies of Fitzherbert’s letter and the consultation [<em>missing</em>], and deny the assertion of the Dutch that the delay in starting was due to them. ‘Watter Bennett, master of the <em>Anne</em>, is deceased, and fourteen men more out of our shippes since theire departure, and manie</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">beecome Christians, that they entertaine them not. And that if any English shall come unto us to become Mores, that wee shall returne them. For the love of freindshipp lett us performe these articles.’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For a later copy preserved at the Public Record Office see the <em>Calendar of State Papers, E. Indies</em>, 1622-4, p. 5. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-57" data-page="57" data-folio="18" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n56_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 18">[p. 18]</button> more fell downe sicke dailye. The Duche have not lost lesse men then wee; neither are the rest in better case then ours.’ . . . ‘By letters which the Duche have receaved from Masulapatnam they have advertisement that theire two shippes sent for the Red Seae had taken two riche shippes of Dabull; but theire people which were put aboarde beinge druncke, the Mores fell uppon them, cutt all theire throates, and soe escaped with theire shippes. The Englishe <em>Sampsone</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-031" href="#sec-04-fn-031" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-031" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> was arrived at Surratte soe weakely manned that they could hardly bringe her in. The other shippe they tell us not what is become of her. By theise theire proceedinges those places where you nowe have free trade in will allsoe bee shutt againste us; which is theire purpose, as farre as wee can conjecture, strivinge to make us as odious to all the worlde as themselves are, which (wee haveinge the name of beinge conjoyned with them) maie easely bee effected. They are growne a moste cruell and bloudy people, and have lately in theise partes committed such unhumaine acts in murtheringe all they take, and takeinge all they meete abroade, as well freindes as foes, that it is abhominable before God and man; and wee hope Your Worships will seeke to cleare your selves from the conjunctione of such unhumaine people, for soe longe as you contynnue to goe alonge with them in theire bloudie exploites (allthoughe wee have noe hand in them) yet you cannot expect that your buissines should prosper. The Gennerall reportes that the <em>London</em> and the other shippes got not the Red Seae, but dispersed themselves uppon the coaste of India, where they tooke three prizes worthe 80,000 r[ial]s, and a Chaule shippe, which they offer to restore if they maie have satisfactione for your loste caphila. The two shippes for England departed from Surratte in November, and fower greate shippes and two pynnaces are arrived at Surratt from Englande. The Portugalls keepe sixe gallioones before Ormus to expect our shippes which goes for Jasques. Thus muche wee have by reporte from the Gennerall, which wee thought fitt to incerte. . . .’ (1½ <em>pp. The rest of the letter deals with affairs at Batavia. Endorsed as received, by way of Holland, September</em> 23, 1622.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-58"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="58" data-folio="19" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n57_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 19">[p. 19]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">James Bickford, Nathaniel Halstead, William Hill</span>, and <span class="sc">John Clark at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, January 12, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 53).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Yours of the 4th currant wee received the 10th att even, and yesterday your latter of the 8th att our retourne from Saffy Caun and Coja Nessan, where (to our greife) wee were compelled to give them two severall scripts from our broker for payment of 23,000 larrees for discharge of our goods, payable att 15 dayes, viz. to Saffy Caun 5,700 larres and to Coja Nessan 17,000 [<em>sic</em>]; and doe therfore intreat your speediest supply hither to discharge both that and other debts which necessitie will draw us into before wee cann dispeede our goods hence.’ Will send all the goods together as soon as transport can be got. As they will see from this, Rustam Khān’s parwāna was worthless. For details as to the indigo sent they refer to the invoice. It is likely to be very cheap this year, viz. about seven rupees a maund. Reflections on the conduct of the Dutch. Are glad Cambay has been supplied direct from Surat. There is another claim against them for 1,400 lārīs, which they will pay if necessary to secure the clearing of their goods. Would be glad to have instructions regarding these claims. (<em>Copy. 2 pp. Received January 17.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Joseph Salbank and Richard Lancaster at Cambay to the Same</span>, January 12, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 67).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received and disbursed the money sent by exchange. Particulars of goods purchased. The investment for Mokha amounts to 26,500 rupees. Have borrowed from Visondas [Vishnu Dās] and Gopaldas [Gopāl Dās], merchants, 6,000 ‘rup[ees] siccaus’ [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 113], to be repaid (at the rate of 100 mahmūdīs for 42 rupees) to certain shroffs in Surat. Request payment accordingly. The Governor has given them leave to start when they like, and has offered his assistance. Request a suitable present to be given him by way of acknowledgement. (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp. Received January 15.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Same</span>, January 14, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 52).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received their letter of the 6th. The Governor still forbids their buying narrow baftas, and has now prohibited the weavers and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-59" data-page="59" data-folio="20" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n58_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 20">[p. 20]</button> brokers from selling them any broad ones until he has had all he wants. Have examined samples of his indigo, and finding them satisfactory have bought the lot. Request a further supply of money. Details of cotton goods provided. They hear from Ahmadābād that Safī Khān has slighted Rustam Khān's parwāna, but now that the latter is there himself they hope the question will be settled satisfactorily. (<em>Copy. 2 pp. Received January 15.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Barber and Justinian Offley at Baroda to the Surat Factory, January</span> 18, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 55).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received theirs of the 2nd, enclosing Is-hāq Beg's letter on their behalf, which is likely to be very useful. The 2,000 mahmūdīs remitted by exchange have also been duly received. John George and another Englishman arrived yesterday to meet the Ahmadābād caravan. Request permission to use them to convoy their goods to Surat, as these are quite ready and the house is sore pestered with them. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received January 20.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, January</span> 20, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 61).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Governor's investment is nearly finished, and then they hope to be permitted to buy freely. He has given an acknowledgement of the money owing by him, with a small abatement. According to instructions, they have sent John George and Richard Brown to meet the Ahmadābād caravan. Tapī Dās, the broker at Baroda, has offered his assistance, and believes he can get permission from the Prince for the English to continue their trading there; or if this fails, he can supply them secretly with any goods they may want. Suggest that he should be written to from Surat. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received January 21.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes and John Parker at Agra to the Surat Factory, January</span> 22, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 100).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Refer to previous letters. No change in the situation yet. Have received the Surat letter of December 24, forwarding one for Āsaf Khān, but an enclosure is missing. For want of access to their books they are unable to give Mr. Bangham the particulars <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-60" data-page="60" data-folio="21" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n59_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 21">[p. 21]</button> he needs in connexion with the debt of 'Herpexad' [Hariprasād], the father of 'Rampexad', but they can be supplied from the accounts sent to Surat. The two bills for Rs. 21,700 given by the former are among their confiscated papers. Their broker 'Jado' and his kinsman appear to have received bribes in this matter to the value of Rs. 500, though 'Jado' strongly denies it. The model of 'the greate jewell' has been sent to them from Burhānpur, and they intend to dispatch it to court, together with the Surat letter to Āsaf Khān, under the charge of the broker. He is to call at 'Syomude<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-032" href="#sec-04-fn-032" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-032" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, which lyeth on the waye, where Mr. Younge and John Willowbye is resydent at present'. Have written to direct Willoughby to accompany Jādū to the court, with orders first to treat 'of our present troubles and the occatione which hath induced them; and haveinge gott our relesment by letter from the Nabob [Āsaf Khān] to his Governor heere (wherof wee doubt not, yf it consisted not of more then the Syndemens busynesse) then to make knowne unto him your letter and intentione in the plesuringe him with the sayd jewell, upon his writinge given for the securinge it from Brampore to courte. Otherwise, as before, in any case to conseale that busynis; and not to yeeld to any agrement, eyther with the Syndye merchants or elce, without your further order.' As regards the great jewel at Agra, refer them to Biddulph, who best knows its value. They advised the factors at Samāna of their treatment at Agra, and they, fearing the like usage, have removed 'from Semmyan to Symude (?), a place (as they say) of more securytie'. Have instructed them, after disbursing what money they have in hand, to return to Agra, for they have no more to send them, and do not expect a supply from below until 'these disturbances are overblowne'. They are keeping the camelmen still in prison till they procure satisfactory sureties for the payment of the amount claimed. Expect to hear from Surat 'howe you disgest this our disgrases', and advice as to their future proceedings. <em>P.S. (by Hughes).</em>—Since writing, they have been to the Governor to procure leave for Jādū to repair to court. He absolutely refused, and threatened, unless they waited quietly for further orders from thence, to put them in irons, as he had been told to do in the first instance. Suggests that orders should <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-61" data-page="61" data-folio="22" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n60_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 22">[p. 22]</button> be sent from Surat to Samāna for some one to go to court from thence ‘to procurate the busynis their’. (<em>Copy. 3½ pp. Received February</em> 13.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">NATHANIEL HALSTEAD AND WILLIAM HILL AT AHMADĀBĀD TO THE SURAT FACTORY, JANUARY 22, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 73).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The caravan of goods for Surat is being dispatched. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Much damaged. Received January</em> 27.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">WILLIAM MARTIN, GEORGE PIKE, AND JOHN GLANVILL AT BROACH TO THE SURAT FACTORY, JANUARY 23, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 67).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Money brought by Robert Tottle yesterday. Some returned as not current. The Governor complains that the Surat factors have ignored his wishes and sent back his servant empty-handed. He has now placed an embargo on their goods and has forbidden their making any purchases. Beg earnestly that some present be dispatched to appease him. Request also a supply of gunny. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received January</em> 24.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">THE SAME TO THE SAME, JANUARY 24, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 71).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Money received, and some returned. They note the instructions to buy goods for the southwards, and will do their best, but fear they cannot procure any large quantity. The Governor has been pacified, and has given them leave to buy and to lade their goods at pleasure. He is now on his way to Surat, and they think it advisable that on his arrival he should be waited on with a present. <em>P.S.</em>—Supply of gunny wanted. They have presented the ‘Merga’ [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 178] with a fair knife and a bottle of sack. (<em>Copy. 1½ pp. Received January</em> 25.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">WILLIAM BELL, HENRY DARRELL, JOHN PURIFIE, AND JOHN BENTHALL AT ‘KUHESTEK’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-033" href="#sec-04-fn-033" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-033" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> TO THE SURAT FACTORY, JANUARY 24, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 109).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In answer to their letter of November 20, 1621, received December 23 at Kuhistak. Urge a large supply of powdered sugar and sugar-candy; pepper they think might also be sold to profit. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-62" data-page="62" data-folio="23" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n61_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 23">[p. 23]</button> Baftas and shashes [turban-cloths] are likely to be in demand. It is unnecessary to wait for patterns, as information regarding the most suitable sorts can easily be got at Surat. ‘The round sorte of steele caled butt [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 88] is indeed the sorte heere best requested.’ ‘Cowha [Arabic <em>qahwa</em>, ‘coffee’] in seeds is an exelent commodity in these parts, and is worth at present in Spahan 50 shahees [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 83] the maunde shah’ [<em>man-ī-shāhī</em>, ‘royal maund’]. Regret that none was sent from Surat, owing to the late arrival of the Mokha junk. Of ‘gumlack in clods’ 200 maunds should be sent each year; it is worth at Ispahān 35, and at Ghilān 40 shāhīs the ‘maune shah’. Cottonwool is both useful for packing and readily vendible. Biāna indigo is most in favour, and is worth here 560–600 shāhīs the ‘kella’ [<em>kūlah</em>, ‘a man’s load’] of seven ‘mauns shah’, Sarkhej indigo being valued only at 480 shāhīs the ‘kella’. China roots and benjamin are the only drugs worth sending. Conserves and dry ginger will produce three for one; and logwood is worth 20 shāhīs the ‘maune shah’. Coral beads they return as unsaleable. Regard the prospects of trade as good, especially if the designs now in hand succeed. Disapprove of Monnox’s project of ‘newe wayes of driveinge the trade by the Casp[ian] Sea’, as likely only to alarm the Shāh. Complain of the non-supply of gunny, dutties, and ropes. Excuse the ill-packing of the last consignment of silk. As to want of weight, all silk coming from ‘Guylan’ [Ghilān] to Ispahān loses at least a maund in every load, owing to its being heavy with moisture when packed and drying on the road; but it is thought that the moisture will be regained during the sea passage to England. The brimstone forwarded was forced upon them by the Sultan of Lar, and they will take care to send no more. Will furnish some ‘ruhanas’ [<em>rūnās</em>, ‘Indian madder’] next year. Robbins’s jewels should be sold at Surat or sent to England. Morse teeth in good demand. Hope to send their accounts regularly. Mr. Jeffries, appointed Chief in Persia, ‘in his passage hether dyed<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-034" href="#sec-04-fn-034" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-034" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> . . . in whose place, by a generall councell held aboard the <em>Jonas</em> the 19th present in the Road of Kuhestek, I, William Bell, was elected Agent’ and John Purifie was appointed fourth in council. Will as directed ‘solicite the Kinge, buildinge our <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-63" data-page="63" data-folio="24" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n62_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 24">[p. 24]</button> grounds [on] our Soveraignes letter’. Have received all the goods sent, except some sugar retained for use in the fleet. Have laden on the ships 772 bales of Ghilān silk, thirty carpets, and two bales of ‘chamlets’, besides certain commodities returned. The reasons moving the commanders to assist the Persians are given in a consultation of December 26. ‘Mr. Monox is retorninge to you now in these shipps, accordinge to the Companies order. Never did they doe themselves and us a more happie turne then to call him home. Mr. Strahand [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 237] is longe sence dismissed the Companies service. Mr. Cardro [<em>ibid.</em>, p. 144] was appointed home, but dyed in Kuhestek. His boye also is dead, and one of the coachmen, to witt Christopher Askwith; also Robert Gifford; whose accounts, inventoryes etc. wee doe now send for England. Wee have all of us been vizeted with sicknis in our passage downe.’ Refer to Monnox and the commanders of the fleet for further information. <em>P.S.</em>—‘Hadgenor’ [Hājī Nūr], a Persian, has embarked in the ships with a quantity of goods. On arrival he should be made to pay freight ‘as other merchants paye from Ormuze to India, which is 12 larres per camells load, and which hee offered to paye’. An extra bale of cloth taken on shore. Two ‘buckshawes’ [Pers. <em>bukchak</em>, ‘a bundle’] of silk wrongly marked. (<em>Copy. 6 pp. Received February 28.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham and John Leachland at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory, January</span> 24, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 79).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received of the Khānkhānān [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 59] Rs. 4,000 in part-payment for tapestry sold to him long since. Have Rs. 10,000 in hand and would be glad of instructions for its disposal. The rate of exchange on Ahmadābād is 6¼ per cent, ‘to Agra monny for monny, and few that will write thether, in regard of the sicknes ther, people fl[y]inge out of the towne’. Are negotiating for the sale of their amber at Rs. 9½ the seer of 14 pice. Send a bill of exchange on Surat for Rs. 6,066¾. The caravan from Agra arrived on the 22nd, and left again the same night under the charge of Thomas Hawkridge and Scander. They are accompanied by an Englishman, named Francis Hyde, recently ‘come out of Decan’. To avoid any dispute as to tolls, they have procured a farmān from Rājā Partab Shāh [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 74, <em>note</em>] <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-64" data-page="64" data-folio="25" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n63_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 25">[p. 25]</button> for the quiet passage of their goods on payment of the customary charges. Money, &amp;c., delivered to Hawkridge for expenses. Send a farmān from Partab Shāh ‘for your more quiatt transeporte of your rialls to Moulear [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 91], if your occasion shall cause your neede thereof, with many faire and large promises of good usage there, promising at any tyme, if you would advise him when you would send, hee would meete your rialls and conduct them safely in and out of his territories. This wee thought of for your better supply for mamoodies in Surratt; which if it may stand in steed, wee shall thinke our tyme and a smale present given him well bestowed; or if not, soe much tyme and labor lost, hee seeminge very willing hereunto.’ ‘Jamser Beage [Jamshed Beg] is now very like to bee your Governor. Hee hath twice [                    ] for it, and wee thincke had bine fully adopted ere this, had not the Princes sonne<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-035" href="#sec-04-fn-035" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-035" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> died some 2 dayes past [ ]. Isack Beage is sent for heather and accused of [                    ] ruppes to have cousened the Prince of, which [                    ] that you shall never see his sonne shine more.’ Jamshed Beg and Jam Qulī Beg have both been called to court. (<em>Copy. Much damaged. 2½ pp. Received February 1.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Rastell, Giles James, and Joseph Hopkinson at Surat to the President and Council at Batavia</span>, <span class="sc">January</span> 24, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Java</em>, vol. iii. pt. i. p. 292).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last on January 4, and have since received theirs of March 21 and April 8, 1621. Cannot encourage them to send more spices than mentioned in their last. No great demand in these parts for pepper, ‘wheareof from the Decan they are suffitiently furnished’. As for cloves, ‘though theare worth at present be 210 mamodies this maune, yett the Dutch have nott fownd any life in despatch of theires . . . allbeitt the[y] weare disperced soe far as Agra.’ Have no information as to the demand for such goods at Mokha. Their own fleet missed that place last year, and the Dutch are too false to be believed in such matters. They say, however, that their <em>Sampson</em> left a good stock of commodities there, and that the <em>Weazof</em> [<em>Wesp</em>] has brought away a good cargo for Holland. As regards Persia, the factors there <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-65" data-page="65" data-folio="26" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n64_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 26">[p. 26]</button> are asking for pepper, ‘wheareof that cuntrie may anually vend 10,000 mands of this place, and is worth theare at present 29 shahees theare maund shaw, which beinge 12½ pound haberdepoze and 13 shahees makinge a riall of eight, yeelds about 27 mamodies this maund, charges of transport to Spahan included, which in our nearest computation may amount to 5 mamodies at least’. Fear, however, that the resources of the Batavia Factory will not permit of such a supply. Have already advised of last year’s success against the Portuguese at Jask, and also their reasons for detaining the <em>London</em> instead of sending her to Batavia as was intended. This year they have been specially instructed from home not to make any investments for the southwards, the Company apparently imagining that the Batavia Factory would have abundant means from the restitution made by the Dutch; but they understand, from a letter of October 8 just to hand, that these expectations have not been fulfilled. Hope, however, to send goods to Achin to the value of Rs. 6,000 or 8,000, which had been provided before the Company’s letter arrived. Know not whether any profit is to be expected there. As to certain charges in dispute between the two factories. Heard from the escaped surgeon of the cruelty exercised by the Portuguese towards the crew of the <em>Unicorn</em> [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 266], and have consequently ordered their ships ‘to prosecute like measure against them till our captived countrimen be redeemed’. Thank them for their news. As regards the Dutch, ‘we pray God that theare mischevous practizes transfer not hither’. ‘The losse of our fleets monsone last yeare for Mocha gave fayre advantadge unto theares (then theare) to become masters of the pray for which our voyadge was projected; wheare haveinge landed belike some petty cargason of goods, and to secure theare trechiry given severall passes to the tradors, the <em>Sampson</em>, leavinge her consort in port, getts out to sea, whear, awaittinge opertunytie, she most perfidiouslie surpriced five of those junks who belived themselves proteckted, of whom one of Diue, another of Cannanore, a third of Kutcha, and other two of Dabull; which tow latter, with most of theare people and treasure, accompanied with 40 or 50 Dutch; weare on theare voyadge designed for Botavia; butt the justice of God prevented them, the Mores in the greater vessell redeemeinge themselves with the blood of 20 off theare gardians, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-66" data-page="66" data-folio="27" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n65_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 27">[p. 27]</button> and with onely four that survived retourned to Dabull in safety; her consort beinge alsoe left in distres, and by all conjecture could never recover her port.' It is suspicious that the Dutch have brought to Surat only a little money to clear their debts and some goods of small value which they could not sell elsewhere, 'most of theare former estate beinge shipt away on the <em>Sampson</em> and noe ships or penny supplie out of Holland,' in spite of the talk first of four and then of six vessels to be sent direct hither. They spend almost as much as the English, 'to maintaine a companie of spies, that in our judgments live cheefly for intelligence and two await coulorable pretence to bringe all things at onst to ruine; for in robbinge theese, tis we must suffer the pennalltie, our quiett and safetie dependinge mearely on theare ports securitie, which if onst overthrowne by the Dutch thear wilbe noe distinktion of nations, beinge heare estemed one companie.' The Dutch know this, but it only encourages them in courses which may at the same time enrich them and impoverish the English. They are much hated by the natives, but at the same time much feared, and therefore better treated. 'Theare <em>Weazof</em> (which you call <em>Mullen</em>) is nott heard of, which themselves impute to the lose of her monsone, butt by others conseved shee never ment to come hither.' The business in Persia is on a doubtful footing, for the Shāh abases the esteem of English goods and enhances the price of his own. With cost of transport to the waterside the silk will cost quite 9<em>s.</em> per lb.; yet the factors there have purchased a very large quantity. Hope to send to England two of the largest ships full of goods, and also an investment of 8,000<em>l.</em> to Mokha. Supply of stores to the Batavia Factory. Arrival of Weddell's fleet. The exploit of the <em>Richard</em> [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 272, <em>note</em>]. Return of Blyth's squadron. Departure of the combined fleets for Persia. Monnox recalled, and Jeffries sent to be agent in his place. <em>P.S.</em>—This letter sent open for Capt. Fitzherbert's perusal. (<em>Copy.</em> 6½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">James Bickford, Nathaniel Halstead, William Hill, and John Clark at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">January</span> 24, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 76).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Safī Khan desires a pass for his ship the <em>Tavori</em> [<em>sec previous volume</em>, p. 278], and so they have been constrained to give the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-67" data-page="67" data-folio="28" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n66_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 28">[p. 28]</button> bearer a note requesting the grant of one; but they hope the Surat factors will bear in mind the Governor’s ill behaviour. (<em>Copy. p. Received January</em> 31.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, January</span> 25, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 74).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received their letters with bills of exchange for Rs. 12,750. The goods of this season’s investment have been dispatched, and John Clark is about to start with ‘Muttredas’ [Mathurā Dās], the broker, to see them safe to Surat. The caravan consists of over 260 carts, and they have had the greatest trouble in procuring its dispatch. Invoices, &amp;c., will follow. Request a supply of money. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>pp. Received January</em> 29.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">James Bickford, Nathaniel Halstead, and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, January</span> 28, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 79).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forward letters received from Agra, and recapitulate previous advices. (<em>Copy. In parts illegible.  p. Received February</em> 1.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">George Robinson at Achin to the Surat Factory, January</span> 28, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 147).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Introduces the bearer, who has been sent by the King of Achin to sell 140 bahars of tin and make returns in ‘candekins’ [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 95], coarse baftas, &amp;c. Begs they will assist him. (<em>Copy.  p. Received April</em> 19.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">George Robinson and Richard Allen at Achin to the Surat Factory, January</span> 28, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 151).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In answer to theirs of April 4 and May 10, 1621. Cannot give fuller details of goods suitable for Achin, but they will gather from the enclosed list [<em>missing</em>] what are most saleable, and at what prices. They could have sold any quantity of blue cloth this year, as pepper was abundant and this cloth much in demand. Whites are not so saleable. Tyrannical behaviour of the King, who forced them to buy his pepper at his own price, while allowing the Gujarātīs to purchase theirs in the open market. The <em>Globe</em> arrived from Masulipatam on September 19, and departed with the <em>Eagle</em> on the 28th. Goods urgently needed from Surat, as pepper is plentiful. ‘Pray advise us how many duckys [Hind. <em>dukrā</em>] <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-68" data-page="68" data-folio="29" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n67_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 29">[p. 29]</button> goeth in account for a m[ahmūdī].’ The Dutch have five ships before Malacca, and seven (with four English) off Goa. Ships coming hither should be careful not to allow natives on board till they have communicated with the factory, ‘for wee know not how longe wee shall staye heere if this Kinge contenewe his forbedinge of us trade,’ and treachery is always to be suspected. The King’s base behaviour to some Frenchmen. ‘One of our folke is nowe in the irons for killing [one of] the King’s horses by throweing a lance at him,’ but they hope soon to procure his release. (<em>Copy. 2¾ pp. Received April 29.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Hawkridge at</span> [                ] <span class="sc">to the Surat Factory, January</span> [29?], 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 82).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Is now two days’ journey from ‘Netherbar’ [Nandurbār]. In crossing the river at Thālner two of the bundles of cloth from Patna got wet, but have been dried again. Hopes to receive money from them at Dhāita to pay the tolls, but can manage without it, if necessary. (<em>Copy. Part illegible. 1 p. Received February 4.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Barber at Baroda to the Surat Factory, January</span> 30, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 75).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Sixty-one bundles of goods dispatched under the charge of Offley, in company with Salbank. John George has been sent to meet the caravan from Ahmadābād. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Much damaged. Received January 31.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, February</span> 1, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 82).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The caravan from Ahmadābād arrived on January 30, and has started to-day for Broach, taking also twenty-three bundles of goods provided by this factory. He is now settling with the brokers, and hopes to follow the caravan to-morrow morning. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received February 4.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">James Bickford, Nathaniel Halstead, and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, February</span> 2, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 85).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Thank them for bills received.
