Had previously drawn on them for Rs. 12,600. Progress of their investments. Complaints against the Dutch. (<em>Copy. Largely illegible. 2 pp. Received February 6.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-69"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="69" data-folio="30" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n68_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 30">[p. 30]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin and George Pike at Broach to the Surat Factory, February</span> 3, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 80).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received their letter enclosing Is-hāq Beg's parwāna 'for free passage of our goods at Uncleseir' [Ankleswar]. Are now dispatching the remaining bundles to Surat. Several have been damaged 'by a kinde of worme which had eaten the gunny duttie and to the cloath'. Are still in need of gunny. The Governor of Broach did not go to Surat as expected. (<em>Copy. Much damaged. 2 pp. Received February 4.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Richard Barber at Broach to the Surat Factory, February</span> 4, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 84).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Ahmadābād caravan has arrived, and will be dispatched. Awaits their orders as to his own movements. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received February 6.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Clark at Broach to the Surat Factory, February</span> 5, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 83).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrived here yesterday with the goods from Ahmadābād. Hopes to have them 'customed' to-day, and to get them over the river in six days more. Begs that directions may be sent to him at 'Jangirpore' [Jahāngīrpur] for the better prosecution of his journey, as also regarding the discharge of his 'peanes' [peons], who number 300. (<em>Copy. Part illegible. 1 p. Received February 6.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory, February</span> 5, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 88).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Damage to some cloth of gold. Cannot get any settlement yet of the tapestry, &amp;c., 'in the Princes sercar,' 'in regard of the sodaine trobles that hath hapened by the death of Sultan Cosse[row], whereof here goeth many reportes, Corome being, and was then, abroad a hountinge some 10 corse off.' Sale of coral and amber. Supply of money. Regarding the recovery of Ramprasād's debt. Payments to Leachland, who has started for Surat. All the sheet lead sold at Rs. 9 per maund of 36 pices to the seer, and 42 seers to the maund. Bangham delivered <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-70" data-page="70" data-folio="31" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n69_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 31">[p. 31]</button> the letters forwarded by the Surat factors. On presenting that written by Is-hāq Beg to his ‘vekell’ [<em>see</em> p. 11], the latter went with him to ‘Assuff Caun’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-036" href="#sec-04-fn-036" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-036" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, who kept them waiting a quarter of the night and then merely said he would acquaint the Prince and receive his orders. ‘Isack Beages vekell, beinge a banyan, semethe to bee an honester man then his maister, and doth promise to goe to the lesker and follow that busines.’ Requests that these two ‘casseds’ [<em>qāsid</em>, ‘a letter-carrier’] may be sent on without delay to Ahmadābād, as they have bills of exchange to carry thither. News has just arrived that the Agra factors are at liberty, though their goods are detained. Papers also received from that place about Ramprasād’s debt. Illness of John Young. <em>P.S.</em>—Money lent to ‘Signor Frinces<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-037" href="#sec-04-fn-037" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-037" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, the Duche’. <em>P.P.S.</em> (6th).—John Young is better. Begs that some one may be sent up to assist in the factory. (<em>Copy. In bad condition. 3½ pp. Received February</em> 13.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Blyth, John Weddell, Edward Monnox, and Nicholas Woodcock, aboard the <em>Jonas</em>, in Gombroon Road, to the Surat Factory, February</span> 7, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 115).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-038" href="#sec-04-fn-038" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-038" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have decided to send back to them the <em>Lion</em>, <em>Rose</em>, and <em>Richard</em>; therefore briefly relate the chief occurrences to date. Reached Jask on December 14, where John Purifie came on board with letters from the Agent, advising them to proceed to ‘Costake’, as the land journey from Jask was unsafe for caravans, both on account of the Portuguese and of the ‘rob[bers] of Mocroone’ [Makrān]. Moved therefore to Kuhistak, arriving on December 23; ‘wher haveinge roade not manye dayes, came aboard us one Samander Sultan<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-039" href="#sec-04-fn-039" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-039" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>, a man greatly in favour with the Chan or Duke of Sheraze, from whom he was sente to congratulate our saufe arryvall, but cheifly to intreat the aide of our shipps against <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-71" data-page="71" data-folio="32" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n70_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 32">[p. 32]</button> their and our enymies the Portugalls. Upon which motione wee proceeded to councell and perusall of our generall comission conserninge the premises; wherin fyndinge our selves sufetiently aucthorized both by our Soveraigne Lord the Kings Majestie of England, also by our honourable ymployers and your comissione from Surratt, wee oposed our selves against the wilfull oppositione and peremtorye refusall of so honourable an enterprize made by the unrulye multetude, whom in thende, not without greate labour, were soe fare pacefyed and also sattisfyed to proceed and to be ruled and obedyent unto their commanders and to doe whatsoever they would desyre them to doe. Wherupon it was resolved by councell to propound certayne artickles to the Duke, which if he would agree unto, then to undertake the service; the coppie of which artickles wee herewith send you [<em>not entered</em>]. The sayd artickles beinge translated into the Pertian, it was ordered that Edward Monox and William Bell should make their repayre to the Chan, who was then att Myna [Minau], and from himselfe to receave answeare theirunto; which in the space of three dayes they effected, accordinge to the Pertian coppie herwith sent you [<em>not extant</em>], which differeth very litle from our one demands. This busynis beinge concluded, we endevoured with all posseble speed to get on board our cavidall [i.e. capital] of silke and other goods, to thende that if it weare poseble we might save the <em>Londons</em> monsoone for England; which busynis, notwithestandinge our much dilligence, was not effected before the 19th of January, by reasone of the Pertians delayes to furnish us with cammells to bringe downe to the porte any of our goods before such tyme as they weare well assured we would undertake the service; which beinge don, the Chan commanded his owne camells to bringe downe our sayd goods; which for the more securitye beinge distributed aboard the severall shipps, the sayd 19th of January wee sett sayle from Costek. And the 23th [22nd] ditto wee ankored before Ormose within lesse then two leages of the castle, in expectacion that our daringe yet dasterdly enymie would have come forth to fight with us with his five gallyones and two smale shipps, besydes frigetts and boats a suffetient nomber to encounter with us, if they durst but be soe bould to have come forth; but they for their more securitye haled both gallyones, shipps, frigitts, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-72" data-page="72" data-folio="33" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n71_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 33">[p. 33]</button> and other vessells close aboard or under command of the castle, by which meanes wee could not com at them with our shipps ; which when wee perceaved, and understandinge our vowed eniemye Rufrero, who had taken the sacrament in Portugall to ruine both us and our trade in these parts, was himselfe in person in his new erected castle of Kishme, the daye followeinge, beinge the 23th of the same month of January, wee sett sayle directly for the castle of Kishme and aryved the same daye aboute none within lesse then a leage of the sayd castle ; and findinge suffetient water to bringe our shipps within faulkin shoote, it was resolved by consultacion to bringe our shipps soe neare as they might convenyently, and to invite our enymies to a banquit of fyer flyinge bullitts the ensueinge morninge ; which when they perceaved, they weived us with naked swords ; yet one, more wiser then the rest, hunge out a napkin or white cloth ; wherupone in Christianlike compassione Edward Monox was sent on shoare to parlie with them. And comeinge to the castle, two Portingalls were ready prepared to come on board our shipps ; to whom the sayd Monox acquainted hee had order from the comanders to talke with Rufrero, if he would be pleased to admit him audyance ; which was presently graunted him, and Rufrero with a good guard of souldyers came forth to mett him. And beinge sett togeather in the courte of guard, the sayd Rufrero began with a longe storye of the antient love and amytie betwene the two nations, English and Portugalls, and the noble acts that the English had done in asistinge the Portugalls to expulse the Moores out of their countrye ; to which the sayd Monox replyed hee came not to treate of busynisses of such antequitye, but hee came to treate of sattisfaxione and revenge for the warrs begun and attempted by himselfe, the their present Rufrero, against our last years fleete in the roade of Jasques, to the losse of a very worthy commander and our Kings sworne servante, besydes other of His Majestis subjects, and likewise to the losse of our monsoone for that yeare to the greate hindrance and damadge of our ymployers ; wherunto he replyed hee was no[t to be] condemened, for he had done nothinge but by his Kinge his masters command and com-m[ission]. And after much otheir ference to the like efecte, which would be over tedyouse in this place to relate, the sayd Monox replyed, notwithstandinge hee deserved smale favour at our <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-73" data-page="73" data-folio="34" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n72_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 34">[p. 34]</button> hands, yet oute of a Christyen feelinge of their present estate, we weare willinge [to] shewe them mercye and to free them from the hands of the Pertians and Moores; which hee seemed willinge to accept, but withall demaunded the like quarter fore the Moores which had served him in his warres as for the Portugalls themselves; wherunto was answered the Moores weare for the moste parte the Kinge of Pertia his subjects and wee could not take upon us to protect them, but of necessity most leave them to the mercye of the Chan. Then sayd Rufrero: "Rather then wee will doe that, wee will ende our lives togeather"; which answer of his seemed to be utered with such vehemencie as if he resolved to doe as hee spake; which Monox perseaveinge, tould him hee would goe unto the Pertian generall, named Emoumelybeage [Imām Qulī Beg], and solicite him in there behalfe; with whom hee soe fare prevayled that he was content to promise them both life and pardone for their offence; only he desyred to take notice of their names and to have it soe provided that they should not goe to Ormose to the further strenghtinge of the enymie there, which request seemed reasonable; but Mr. Monox retorninge to the castle to acquent Rufrero with what he had don, he absolutly refused any such agrement. Wherupon worde was sent us aboarde, and wee supposeinge hee used but delayes onely to practice his owne escape, wherfore wee presently resolved to trie the strength of his castle with our ordynance; which was performed by the whole fleete with neere upon 1,000 shoote, in which fight wee killed him above 30 men, with some breaches made in his castle walls, yet not soe much as wee intended. Wher[upon] wee gave it over and resolved upon the landinge of five peeces of our ordynances, to batter him nearer at hand; which was in two or three dayes soe well followed that wee dismounted three or four peeces of his ordenances and soe shooke the wales of his bulwarke that in the [end?] hee was content to listen to a second parley, wherin wee for the gayninge of [the castle?] were content to suffer them to departe souldyerlike with bagge and baggage, his maymed and sicke people for Ormose, the rest of his Portugalls souldyers for Muscatt or Swar [<em>see</em> p. 15] and not to retorne agayne for Ormose within the terme of six weekes, and Rufrero himselfe, with some tenn or twelve more of his princepall captaines and commanders remayne aboard our shipps to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-74" data-page="74" data-folio="35" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n73_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 35">[p. 35]</button> our further disposure of them in Indya; and the Mores, by the greate opertunitye [importunity] of the Pertian generall, for the most parte weare delivered unto him. And soe the first of February, haveinge dispeeded both Portugalls and Mores, to the nomber of six or seven hundred men, weomen, and childrin, wherof 300 were Portugalls, the Pertian generall and wee hand in hand tooke possessione of the castle, where the culours of both nations weare at one instant sett upp, where they stand, and wee hope shalbe ther be [<em>sic</em>] maynetayned, to the honour of our natione and perpetuall disgrase to our proud enymies. In which enterprize, both at sea and on shoare, wee lost onely three men, and two other maimed and hurt. One of them is Mr. Abraham the Dutchman, who is in daunger to loose one of his armes, beinge shoote with a greate shoote from the castle on shoare; but the man whom wee shall finde the greatist misse of is Mr. Baffin, that was kiled outright with a muskit on shoare, plyinge a peece of great ordenance against the castle.’ As regards other occurrences, Mr. Jeffries died within seven days of their leaving Surat, and Mr. Bell has been appointed Agent in his place. Mr. Monnox desired to go in the first ship for Surat, but his services are so necessary that they have prevailed upon him to remain with the fleet. ‘This enterprize, though it may verie properlye be caled ours, for the efectinge [of] our owne designes, yet are wee therin aided by the Pertian as partaker of our shipps charges to the amount of 600 tom[ans]<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-040" href="#sec-04-fn-040" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-040" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> per month for his parte, besydes the moitye of all the powder and shotte shalbe spent in the prosecution therof,’ with other conditions shown in the enclosed articles. ‘The aforesaid busynes beinge ended at Kishme, and four English appoynted to keepe our possessione in the castle, the 4th of February wee sett sayle with our whole fleet for Gombroone, ther to confer with the Duke upon our further attemps; by whom wee weare the nixt day invited on shoare to a very princly feast and banquet; the which beinge ended, and wee ready to take our leaves of His Exelencie, hee demanded of us what shipps we purposed to send awaye and which should remayne here for the prosecution of his and our further designes and atempts for the gayninge of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-75" data-page="75" data-folio="36" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n74_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 36">[p. 36]</button> Ormose; to which his propositione wee answered him directly that such and such shipps wee purposed to dispeed away, and the rest should remayne upon the intended service; wherwith he seemed well content, and soe we departed.' Have notwithstanding sent the <em>Lion</em> in addition to the other ships mentioned, in order that the Mokha factory may be supplied; but if it be thought at Surat too dangerous to venture a single ship to the Red Sea, the <em>Lion</em> should be at once sent back to them with a suitable cargo. Sugar and conserves would be particularly profitable; and indeed all India commodities are in demand now, 'by reasone of the present restraint of trade with Ormose.' Moreover, broadcloth would probably sell quite as well in Persia as at Mokha. Would be glad to have the <em>Primrose</em> dispatched to them from Surat with provisions and stores for the fleet. 'Wee send you herewith for a present Capt. Rufrero, with six more of his princepall captaynes and gentlemen, which we refer to your disposure, wherin we knowe you wilbe carefull of our poore afflicted countriemen who suffer messerable bondage under their ensultinge enymies to the sothwards [<em>cf.</em> p. 26]. Our opynions are that unlesse the Companies shipps to the sothwards have beter ymployment there then wee can imadgin, it weare very fittinge you gave order for som of them to com for the succour and reliefe of this place, for it is very certayne the nixt yeare, if not before, wee shall have all the force of Indya upon our necks; and if wee can but hould it oute and give them the repulse but once more, wee shall not need to feare ourselves to be the absolute masters and comanders of these seas, wherby for the future our honourable ymployers may negotiate their affayres in these parts with their single shipps and not with whole fleets in this maner, the charge wherof devoreth the proffitt (if not more), wherby the Company are only traders but litle gayners. The late victorye which God hath gave us and the conquest made upon the castle of Kishme hath in a maner brought us to as good a possession of these seas and the traffique therof as our enymies, that have benn plantinge themselves this 120 yeares, for wheras they have the succour of their castle of Ormose for defence of ther navie, so lickwise we have the same succour for our navie under the castle of Kishme, where wee have 6 fathom water within muskitt shott of the castle, and we have the country of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-76" data-page="76" data-folio="37" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n75_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 37">[p. 37]</button> Pertia to our frinds and they their mortall enymies, which is noe smale advantage on our parts, and none of the least inducements to incite us to the prosecutione of our intendments against Ormose, wherin our cheife ayme and prime endevours shalbe by Gods assistance [to] ruine there armado; which will not be effected but by some stratigem of fyer, they keepe themselves soe close under the protixione and defence of their castle; but wee doubt not before longe to make that place to hott for them. The castle of Kishme will necessarily require some beter fortificatione for defence of itselfe and our shippinge; wherin wee desyre you to advize us your opynions whether wee shall doe anythinge therin or noe. It will also require a greater nomber of ordynance then now it hath, beinge only 15 peeces, greate and smale, and one base; and by all meanes wee desyre you omitt not to cause some quantetye of demie cullverine shott and saker shott to be made against the nixt yeare, and send us by the first soe many as you can in soe shorte tyme procure to be made.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 4¾ <em>pp. Received February</em> 28.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Francis Pinder at Gombroon to the President at Surat</span>, February 7, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 107).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The fleet reached Jask on December 14, but found that no silk was to be had unless the English consented to help the Shāh to take Kishm, Ormus, &amp;c., ‘hee haveinge so ben promysed 6 monthes before our theire aryvall.’ A consultation was held on the 26th ‘for the followinge these designes, groundinge their warent on your consultacion<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-041" href="#sec-04-fn-041" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-041" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> granted to this efect; if by me not misunderstood, you athorised to seeke the distruxion of the Portugalls, even in their owne portes, soe it wear without daunger or prejudice to the Company.’ Pinder advised that the silk should first be taken on board, in case the Persians proved faithless, and this course was adopted. ‘Soe, the silke taken in, wee went for Kishme, where the 23 January aryved, beseiginge it by sea, the Pertian by land, wee landinge ordynance, with men to manage them, the 24th, haveinge made some shotte from the shipps, with the losse of two men, being more hardy then provident. Betwene the 23 and 29th weare many parlyes betwene the Portugalls and our Cheifs for the surender <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-77" data-page="77" data-folio="38" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n76_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 38">[p. 38]</button> of the castle, which the 29th was concluded. Mr. Bafine slayne on shoare at conclutione of the surender. Condicion I truelye know not, but spoken they weare to departe with bage and baggage, all the Portugalls and Moores, Rufrero and some few otheir excepted, which weare detayned to redeme our natione. The Portugalls and Moores haveinge yelded under our protixione, some daye after the Mores were sent on shoare to the Pertsan, who formerly had promysed them mercie, but falsly murthered them most unhumanly.' Commendations to friends at Surat. <em>Addendum.</em>— A note of the questions put at the general consultation by the company of the <em>London</em>. 'First, wheather tolerable by our Prince to joyne with a heathen against the Portingalls, and not to be caled in question. Secondly, if allowed by our imployers; and in case of losse of shipp or shipps, whear our reward for tyme spent should be lookt for. Therdly, if any lost life or lymes, how he or his should be rewarded. The answeare: your authoritye was suffetient warrent for proceedinge, but the reward rests in the Companies pleasures.' (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp. Received February 28.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Weddell and Nicholas Woodcock, aboard the</span> <em>JONAS</em> <span class="sc">in Gombroon Road, to the President at Surat, February 8, 1622</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 109).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Certifying that the bearer, 'Rufrero', 'was promysed to be sett on shoore within six weaks att Surratt or Damon, but leaveinge him to your owne discretions.' <em>P.S.</em>—'His desyre is not to goe ashoare at Surratt att all, for feare that they will take him from you and keepe him prisoner.' (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received February 28.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin and George Pike at Broach to the Surat Factory, February [9?], 1622</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 87).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Progress of their investments. Gunny wanted; also packers, if available. (<em>Copy. Mostly illegible. 1½ pp. Received February 10.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory, February 10, 1622</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 105).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The merchants refuse to give more than Rs. 9 per seer for the amber, on the ground that it is too small. Hopes to agree for the tapestry in the Prince's 'sercar' [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 160]; also for the picture of Diana, which he understands cost 700 mahmūdīs.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-78"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="78" data-folio="39" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n77_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 39">[p. 39]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">But ‘in this devilish sercarr noe man can be master of their owne goods.’ ‘The Prince is retorned from huntinge, and Assuff Can [Afzal Khān : <em>see</em> p. 31] now fallen seick and keepeth howse. Soe that here is litle hope of speedy procuringe a fermaune of Assuff Cane for our frinds fredome in Agra ; but I shall use my best diligence when tyme will permitt. Jamcolybeage [Jam Qulī Beg] is dispeeded hence towards you. Jemsearbeage is to stay till Isake Beage com upp, and then, as reportes yett, hee shalbe your Governor. The Narose<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-042" href="#sec-04-fn-042" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-042" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> I hope wilbe keept here, which wilbe a meanes to put of some of all sortes of our commodities ; in which I will not sleepe.’ Urges dispatch of some one to assist him. <em>P.S.</em>—‘Your picture is in the Princes sercare.’ (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received February 20.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Clark at Jahāngīrpur to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">February</span> 13, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 92).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Requesting Rs. 200 by the bearer for payment of the peons ; also instructions as to the number to be retained to look to the goods.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(<em>Copy.  1/2 p.  Received February</em> 13.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">WILLIAM MARTIN AND GEORGE PIKE AT BROACH TO THE SURAT FACTORY, FEBRUARY 14, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 92).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Gunny, &amp;c., received. Will make purchases sparingly, according to order. The four packers have arrived and have been set to work. They have usually paid only three-quarters of a mahmūdī per bundle, but will pay these at the rate ordered. (<em>Copy. Part illegible.</em> 1 <em>p. Received February</em> 16.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">CONSULTATION OF THE COMMANDERS OF THE ENGLISH SECTION OF THE FLEET CRUISING AGAINST THE PORTUGUESE, FEBRUARY 17, 1622<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-043" href="#sec-04-fn-043" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-043" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> (<em>O.C.</em> 1032 A).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As Michael Green has been chosen to succeed the late Capt. Fitzherbert, Bartholomew Goodall is transferred from the <em>Diamond</em> to the <em>Anne</em>, Mr. Philpot from the <em>Exchange</em> to the <em>Diamond</em>, and Richard Swanley, master's mate, is promoted to be master of the <em>Exchange</em>. (<em>Copy.  1/4 p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-79"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="79" data-folio="40" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n78_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 40">[p. 40]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">James Bickford, Nathaniel Halstead, and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, February</span> 19, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 103).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Note what has been done regarding the pass for Safī Khān's vessel. Are glad the caravan has got as far as Jahāngīrpur in safety. 'Ours of the 25th past, togeither with a later breife and the inclosed from our frinds at Agra, wee perseave you have receaved, and understand what you have done for their releife. God graunte Isack Beages letter to his master be of more vertue then his masters firmaunes have bynn in Surratt in our behalfe; but that tyme will shewe. In the meantyme wee conseave it would not be amisse to learne (yf wee might) what those doggs of Cambaya are that are the complaynants, which beinge knowne, hapely some redresse might be procured nerer home, but heerof wee doubt not but you have heedfull thought.' Explanation as to the rate of exchange. Bickford thinks of going to Surat shortly. 'It hath pleased God latelye to vizet him with a sudayne sicknis, wherin in two dayes and one night hee thrice fell dead, but through Gods mercie he is now reasonable well.' Have set in hand the provision of broad 'duttyes Dowlka' [<em>see previous volume</em>, pp. 28, 62] and broad 'seryas, both playne and stripte, for napkins'; have also ordered the buying 'in the bezar' of narrow baftas from Rs. 1¼ to 2½ apiece. Have received from Burhānpur bills of exchange for Rs. 9,500. The rate from Ahmadābād to Burhānpur is Rs. 93 per cent, while at the latter place Bangham paid Rs. 6¾ per cent for the exchange; this, though dear, is not exorbitant. 'The washers that lost our lynens wee have had before the Cazie [<em>Qāsī</em>, judge] (whose busynis it is), and after a very formall hereinge of the mater, the fellow that lost our 16 baftas stolne oute of his howse, but cannot shewe any reason howe, hee is adjudged to sattisfie us; but hee that lost our 50 dutys, theire beinge proffe apparent that both the pore and his howse [were?] robd by force of theeves, wee weare adjudged to stand to the lose of them ourselves, it beinge the costome of this country; onely wee have his screit [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 4] that if hereafter wee com to the knowlidge that he lost not soe manye, he is lyable to sattisfaxione for the whole; and so beinge throughly informed that whatsoever in this countrye is taken by theeves perforce <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-80" data-page="80" data-folio="41" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n79_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 41">[p. 41]</button> is to be borne by the owner, in whose custodye soever.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 1¾ <em>pp. Received February 27.</em>)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes at Agra to the Surat Factory, February</span> 23, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 134).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Having been appointed chief of this factory, he solicits the continuation to himself of the yearly allowance of 20<em>l.</em> made to his predecessor towards apparel, and doubts not they will grant it, ‘apprehendinge the rest of my meanes to be poore and not suffetient to maynetayne a computent porte in this imployment.’ (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received March 25.</em>)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory, February</span> 23, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 106).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As directed, will remit all available funds to Ahmadābād. Has just sold all the coral, the money to be paid half at once and half in seventy-five days, with a discount of one per cent on the latter payment if made at once. Hopes also to conclude with the Prince about the tapestry. ‘I have had a great deale of attendance upon Assuff Con [Afzal Khān: <em>see</em> p. 31] sence his health, for a firmaune to our frinds at Agra; who for two or three dayes gave me some hopes, but sence newes of the death of Etteman Doulat<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-044" href="#sec-04-fn-044" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-044" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and the imprisonement of Assuff Cane, or (as most reporte) his beinge put to death, aboute this late murther, I cannot gett him to heare me speeke in that kinde; wherupon I have advised our frinds at Agra not to rely on such unsertayne hopes as heare are for their releife, the tymes beinge soe full of troubles as at present. Auzuff Cane [Afzal Khān], as reporte sayeth, is postinge to Vizapoore [Bijāpur]. Other sayeth the Kinge hath sent for him and Hackam Alamandine and Bickermazete aboute the death of his sonne Cossoro.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-045" href="#sec-04-fn-045" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-045" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Many unsertayne reportes goeth, this Prince not beinge dismayd in the worlds eye. The Kinge hath, as all men reports here, retyred himselfe towards Agra, and ther will keepe his Narosse’ [<em>see</em> p. 39]. Is much grieved at their order that he is to carry the jewels to Agra, ‘wherin it semeth you will now in my ould dayes make me more <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-81" data-page="81" data-folio="42" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n80_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 42">[p. 42]</button> inferyor then ever, and therby to bringe my head under the subjectione of younger men. . . . Besydes, Mr. Hughes acquaintance in that courte cannot but most [<em>sic</em>] produce beter frinds for the sale theirof then I can, beinge but a straunger.' Further, he is now in suit with Soares [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 89], and hopes to prove him a villain, for it is notorious that he received back from Sprage the bulk of his goods. It is important that he should see the case through; trusts therefore that they will rescind their order. The sale of the jewels is also unlikely, 'for that Assuffe Cane is in disgrace, if not dead; besydes that those comodities are not every mans monye.' He would rather agree to return to England, 'or be a boye [i. e. servant] in Surratt,' than quit Burhānpur at present. <em>P.S.</em>—Many rumours prevalent, but nothing certain. (<em>Copy. 2 pp. Received February 28.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes and John Parker at Agra to the Surat Factory, February</span> 23, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 134).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">When last heard of, their caravan was nearly at Surat; trust it has now arrived. Will do their best to sell speedily the goods about to be sent up to them, but fear they cannot dispose of them in time to invest the proceeds and make return this season. As to 'Ramperxad's' account, they cannot answer precisely, for want of their books. The payments said to have been made to the brokers are denied by 'Jado' and they cannot disprove him; 'yet understand of some lolledge<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-046" href="#sec-04-fn-046" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-046" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> they have taken, but to what some hee will not acknowlidge.' The two bills of 'Shaw Daud Canue' [Shāh Dāūd Khān] will be sent to Burhānpur when recovered. The Italian who brought the letter from Surat has gone to Patna to make his investment there for Persia, but is expected back shortly, and any letters for him to carry should be sent at once. 'The Dutch reporte the good successe of their caphilo past Brampore. And wheras you desyre further to partake of their proceedings, wee insist that although they have more lybertye, yett it appeares they have lesse to doe then ourselves, their princepall impedyment wante of meanes, wherof they ar altogether destetute, as haveinge longe <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-82" data-page="82" data-folio="43" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n81_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 43">[p. 43]</button> before our comeinge heather sould all their cloves att (or aboute) 220 ruppees the maunde of this place. From hence wee conceave their indico now sente cheifly to proceede, and nowe they have nothinge lefte save a litle benjamen and camphore, commodities as litle esteemed heere as sought after, and therfore lyes by them. They devouldge not anythinge of their faithelesse proceedings with us at the southwards [Java, &amp;c.], but contrary to [<em>sic</em>] a fleete of shipps departed thence in October last for these coasts, consisting of seven Dutch and fower English, which by ther one reportes brings them quantetyes of southrine commodities, with a further intentione to curbe the Guzeratts &amp;c. in their trade and full resolucion to depresse any obstickle that maye affronte them. Our frinds in Sammana, hereinge of our molestacion here in Agra, lefte their former resydence and repayred to Synnode [<em>see</em> p. 21]; where, after consultacion taken (though contrarye eyther to order, advice or expectacion from us), Mr. Younge with upwards of 5,000 rupp[ees] in ready spetia, besydes goods, departed for Lahore, with an intente (as themselves wright) to have invested that monye in indico and soe to have gone overland for Pertia, leaveinge Willowby behinde for the recoverye of 2,600 and odd rupp[ees] wee had litle before remitted them hence; who was to have followed, but Mr. Younge arryveinge in Lahore, hee was emedyatly taken upp into the custodye of Agga Abram [Āghā Ibrāhīm], Assuffe Chons veskele [<em>see</em> p. 11] their, who, as it should seeme, detayned both him and his goods; yet upon the receipt of sundry letters received from hence hee replies us hee is determyned to retorne agayne for Sammana yf (which his assistance [assistant?] now in Synnde [<em>sic</em>] questyoned) hee can gett leave. Wee sent them a coppie of your liste and have advized them the best wee can how they should goe forward for the accomplishinge of what you expected from them and the seazon lymitted; but the tyme alreadye lost, wherin they have done just nothinge, breed suspetione whether they can contenewe the provissione of the quantety and qualytie of calicos inordered in that factorye; wherof wee are yet altogeather destetute, as haveinge founde noe releife towards the releasement of our goods still under the possesione of these currs, who will not be brought to anythinge but approbacion from courte, from whence first sprong their authorytie; <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-83" data-page="83" data-folio="44" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n82_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 44">[p. 44]</button> where wee this month have had a pititione attendinge, and as yet hath founde noe accesse or audyence, by resone of the late death of <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Etemaddoulet</span>, which in that familye hath occationed late <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">troubles in Burania</span>, espetially in Assuff Chonn his sonne. And <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">upon the news thereof</span> Mr. Banggam speeded your letters <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">which came to the recepte of your</span> Governours testymonyall letters to the <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Prince, and him her was</span> to solicite a nishane [<em>nishān</em>, <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">order], and so from thence</span> to Assuff Chon in our behalfe, <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">but findinge it soe</span> difficultye at that instant of <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">troubles, and through the Princes</span> then absence in [<em>sic</em>] <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">pursute and the late murther of</span> Sultan Casserowe, which hath bred <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">much hubbub as</span> well then as here in Agra, and is likely <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">every day to increase, which at present is in</span> Congera,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-047" href="#sec-04-fn-047" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-047" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> but Mr. Younge <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">with the Wazeer [or part of it] is</span> kept in Lahore. Soe that wee <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">cannot yet feare we</span> shall a longe tyme be quitt of these troubles <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">untill express comittance</span> comes from lesker, whether the <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Lapoora botto (if granted)</span> must goe, and answere retorne before <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">which will require</span> tyme, all loste to us, and then hapely <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">not granted untill</span> satisfaxtione made these Syndamen, who beinge at lesker and wee in Agra, wee shall fynde it deficulte to <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">come to agreement or</span> cleare their demaunds, which what they are <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">or howe farr we</span> are not justly posest, but whence [once?] <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">again in questione</span>, haveinge nowe your full approbacion for an absolute <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">resolutione</span> to be made therof by giveinge them satisfaction, we shall endevour it the best we maye for our owne advauntage. Our frinds in Amadavad writes us of some sattisfaction made Cambaia merchants for sundry goods Mr. Kerridge apprized. Wee by that convayance intreated testymonyeall letters from those Governours, which questionlese would have benn much avaylable unto us heere, espetially Agga Salyes [Āghā Sālih], Governor of Cambaya, who is for Assuff Chon, and the only man (as wee conceave) which was the occatione of our attachment heere, by wrightinge his masters most untrewethes, the recalinge wherof under his owne hand could not but be worth the solicetinge.’ That accounts were not sent in duplicate, owing to want of time for copying them. ‘Have advised Mr. Younge &amp;c. to reforme <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-84" data-page="84" data-folio="45" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n83_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 45">[p. 45]</button> theire plurality of cavyilluryoo.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-048" href="#sec-04-fn-048" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-048" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Jādū makes little profit from his brokerage here and is discontented at being detained. The Māndū camelmen have been cleared on paying Rs. 2,000 and giving an undertaking to pay Rs. 1,900 more. Refer to their letter of January 22. Are still keeping back the Surat letter for Āsaf Khān pending the receipt of further instructions. Little hope of dispatching any goods before the rains. (<em>Copy.</em> 3½ <em>pp. Received March</em> 25.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Methwold, Matthew Duke, and Francis Futter at Masulipatam to the Surat Factory, February</span> 26, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 130).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In answer to theirs of January 9, received on February 10. Condole with them in their late sufferings. ‘Wee have your judgment of gumlack and are theirby confirmed in our former opynions; yet resolve not to proceed further untill receipt of that sente give further lybertye then any yet received. All that comes heather is of that sorte you call rustack,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-049" href="#sec-04-fn-049" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-049" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> but some cleaner then other and fewer stickes; of which soe long as the trade of Pegue conteneweth (which of late yeares hath somewhat declyned) there may be procured some quantetye, but noe haste to be dealinge without some assurence of proffitt. Wee are glad wee are acquit of further search after Beng[ala] silke, wherunto we weare somewhat ingaged, for beinge [m]isleed through a vayne promyse of an unable merchante to write of some large hopes of good quantetyes procurable in these parts, which after soe longe expectacion vanisheth into smoke, for here seldome comes anye eyther in itts quantety or condicion worth the surveigh; of which nature in one boate from Bengala (which is all arryved this yeare) here is brought some fewe maunes, for which the owner dare aske 30 pag[odas] per maune; indeed at this tyme all silke is both deare and scarce. As for correll wee have, wee finde enough of this one chest to condeme this sorte as uncapable of more. Whilst the dyamond myne drewe recourse of banyans in[to] these partes, wee might some tymes shewe it and were once reasonable well offered, but hung of because wee are injoyned to hould it to a good price or <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-85" data-page="85" data-folio="46" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n84_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 46">[p. 46]</button> seeke meanes to sende [it] unto you; but sence the myne is imbargued [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 265] wee have not once benn asked after it; soe that this smale proofe maye with litle prejudice assure our masters that this poore place affords noe vente for soe importante a commoditie both in its qualety and value. Your readynis to have transported good parte of your greate quantety if we had given incoradgment wee have great reasone to comend and thanke, but trewly this is noe place for such a purpose, nor hardly our howse capable of receipt, soe extreamly we contenew pestered with everlastinge hepes of course and fine pursleene [porcelain] of all sortes, which vend both slowlye and at cheape rates. The order and maner of its [i.e. the coral's] workeinge we understand not noe further then by inquirye, which wee partecipate; and [as?] hear in soe longe tyme wee finde not merchants for one chest, we se litle incoradgment to enquier further into the course of that merchandize. The monopoly erected here upon gould and silver [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 205] quickly vanished, and cheifly by the Dutch and our oppositione. This goverment is farmed at a racke rente, and he best welcome to the princepall undertaker that can devise a newe course of raysinge newe revenewes, among the which this was propounded, and farmed for 3,000 pag[odas], but contenewed not above three monthes, and duringe that tyme litle beneficiall to the undertaker, for each man whom necessity did not inforce would not soe much countenance this inforcement as to bringe his quoine to this singuler serasse [Arabic <em>sarrāf</em>, 'a moneychanger'], for under that title was erected this new office, which not long after fell of itselfe; wheras your goverment, subject to a Prince proude enough of himselfe, without the flaterye of his followers, ambitious and in his rise, hath his will for his lawe, and like a Mahomytane pursues that thorough whatsoever prejudice of people or comon wealth; soe that your sufference is like to be more lastinge, and, for the reasons you give, maynely inconvenient to the comon of your trade, which wee here admire to heere soe farr extended as to have in readynis soe large a cargazone that with Persian helpe maye freight two great shippes for England, besydes soe lardge a compatencie for Jacatra and the Reed Sea. Our masters affayres to the southwards will maynely wante your helpe; which, as it seemeth by letters from thence, comes soe fare shorte of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-86" data-page="86" data-folio="47" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n85_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 47">[p. 47]</button> necessarye and expected meanes that manye of their newe setled factoryes must paye their proportion of charges and learne the course of that trade by lookeinge upon the Dutch, who more plentifuly then ever exporte masses of ryalls to supplye all places. It was an easye compositione; wee thought to enter into their conquests without disbursments of any former charges, but their senister proceedings in ther falce and disemuled promyses, with ther insolente comportements in the places of comaund, doe daylie teach us that our masters had beter have bought further lyberty with more expence; a touch wherof Pollicatt hath nowe lately afforded us. You have longe sence understood the agrement for that trade, that, contractinge togeather to the full extent of the least capitall, who had further meanes might proceed further, but in receipt of goods the joynt contracte was to have the preference, and beinge received to devide equalye per lott or judgment. The <em>Goulden Lyone</em>, which arryved in December, departed hence in January and from Pollicott aboute the begineinge of this month; upon whom because they would send a cargazon of goods to be at markitt before us, they commanded in all the cloth which could be made readye and, not once acquentinge our factores, apropriated all to ther use, and by this meanes embarqued at that tyme 138 fardles; which beinge by ours perseaved, they complayned of the injury and demanded reasone for the actione, and weare answered ther busynis then required it and that hee [<em>sic</em>] their did by their Governors order in Muselapatnam, and if they had incroched upon the mutuall contracte upon this occatione, wee should receave the nixt cloth was readye; which was indeed performed, but because they were not intresed [interested] they would not affoard us their judgment in the oversight, but lefte ours to their owne unexperyenced knowlidge to make choyce as they could best understand, and this in our opynione was a most insolent proceedinge, most repugnant to equitye and ther owne agreement; wherunto occurr daylie more agrevances, though of lesse consequence. They make us paye our parte of ammunition of the Holland price and buye it themselves at the Indyen; they charge the Forte with unnecessary souldiers to man out (?) their friggatts upon occatione, yet denye to participeate with us in ther reprisalls; they permitt us not to build, nor will affoard our people more then ther lenghth square to lodge in. In <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-87" data-page="87" data-folio="48" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n86_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 48">[p. 48]</button> all which I have sought reformacion, but they have their evation ; somthinge is done without their knowlidge ; others they perswayde us wee understand not aright ; indeed nothinge they meane to remydy, but as yf it weare one most necessary poynte of their flate trade cheifly recomended to their practice, they doe whatsoever they maye to weary us. Elce what needed their delusive and abusive temporizinge with our masters, promysinge assistance of shipps as to Surratt, restitucion of shipps taken att Bantam, with the value of goods accordinge to agrement, whilst they fayle as much themselves in Holland, and wee feare gives such order to ther servants that should effect the rest. Somthinge is performed ; they finde here in Indya newe damadges not formerly advised, which unlesse wee will make good they will attend further order out of Europe ; so that our imployers, deluded with their promyses in expectacion of their performance, scants their supplye of moneys, whilst ther busynis stands still, hardly stronge enough to disburse the necessitye of garrissons and defensive fleete. And all this wee are of opynione they have purposely comploted to discoradge or weary oute our employers, and to graspe this trade in their onely manadginge ; which may once [i.e. at some future time] hapen, for what the common opynione of this trade is as it consernes England hath been discovered in <em>The Trades Increase</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-050" href="#sec-04-fn-050" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-050" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and like busye intermedler, and is glanced att in the late newes from Spayne. What they [i.e. the Dutch] intend aganest the Guzeratts, they keepe that place soe bare, you ga[ve] good reasone to misdoubt, for where they cannot hurte us per ther power wee ha[ve] more reasone to feare ther polecye. Wee are too well grounded in these partes, both [in] meanes and men experyenced, to be indamaged by any of ther inlade machinac[ions], who can easely deserne that their rumours devulged of dyamonds was only to terryfy others from buyinge, the beter to vend what they have formerly bought, wheras (as you trewly apprehend) quantetise most produce cheapnes; they are even in this place rysen to doble their former prise and fewe or none extent. If it weare not policie in them it wear palpable ignorance (for who can saye but they may be mallitiously ignorante); the price and quantety compared with <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-88" data-page="88" data-folio="49" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n87_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 49">[p. 49]</button> those of Jaecadanna<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-051" href="#sec-04-fn-051" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-051" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> might be the grounds, wheare the sight of stone must be purchesed with guifte and the stone itselfe with stoare of ryalls three tymes the value they have ben bought in this place. Why they weare heere soe cheape this Kings covetiousnis must give account, that sett noe lymett to the myner; and for their dearnis at present his feare hath to answere, who hath of late laded whom [i.e. home] chargable embassidours with uncreadable presents for sattisfaxione to their unreasonable demands, and dar[es] nowe hardly be knowne to have a myne in his countrye, least it should undermyne his kingdome. The fleete disigned for the coast of Mallabar is most certayne speeded, and wee had longe sence hoped to have heard of their arryvall, and some happie ef[fects] of their intendments wee doubt not but you have before this tyme, for the commandour of the fleete, one of the Councell of Indya and of good account in Holland (Signor Dedell by name), is, as wee are creadably informed, authorized to supplant Vanderbrook and to resyde cheife of all those factoryes. Wee have heard that you are plentifully stored with good horses; we the contrary (of any worth their meate). It weare a thriftye service to the Company, and to us both acceptable and necessary, if you could fynde meanes to send us one, for here they are very scarce and deare. Wee made of late yeares the same motione to Mr. Kerridge, who seemed well inclyned therunto, but then insued the warres in Decanye, which are nowe agayne removed; soe that if convenyencie admitt wee intreate your remembrence; otherwise wee should feare to trouble you.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 4 <em>pp. Received March</em> 28.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Richard Swanley’s Journal of his Voyage from Persia to Surat</span> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xxxiv. p. 37).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1622, <em>February</em> 5. Swanley transferred to the <em>Lion. February</em> 7. They sailed from Gombroon. <em>February</em> 14. Lost sight of the <em>Rose</em> and <em>Richard. February</em> 24. Saw the coast of India. <em>February</em> 25. Saw the <em>Rose</em> ahead, and the following day overtook her. ‘Shee had taken three Mallabars juncks bound for Gogo.’ <em>February</em> 27. Arrived at Surat. (3½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-89"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="89" data-folio="50" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n88_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 50">[p. 50]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Davis, Master of the</span> <em>Richard</em>, <span class="sc">at Swally to the President at Surat, February</span> 26, 1622<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-052" href="#sec-04-fn-052" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-052" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 289).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has brought as prisoners Ruy Freire and ten more of the principal Portuguese captains, taken at Kishm and now sent to be ‘att Your Worships disposinge’. They left Gombroon on the 8th, being three ships, the <em>Lion</em>, <em>Rose</em>, and <em>Royal Richard</em>; but the third day after their departure the two former lost company with them. The bearer will explain the state of their ship. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received February</em> 28.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Johnson, Master of the</span> <em>Rose</em>, <span class="sc">at Swally to the President at Surat, March</span> 2, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 120).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Sends a note of stores required, and requests a list of any that may be dispatched to him. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">James Beversham, Master of the</span> <em>Lion</em>, [<span class="sc">at Swally</span>] <span class="sc">to the President at Surat, March</span> 2, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 120).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The President’s order for the release of the Malabars and their goods was sent on receipt to Johnson, who refused to take notice of it because it was addressed to Beversham. Hearing that Johnson’s men had taken some goods from the Malabars since the President’s departure, he has thought it best to do nothing until further instructions are received. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin and George Pike at Broach to the Surat Factory, March</span> 2, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 120).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The 100 mahmūdīs mentioned in the letter from Surat were not (according to his tale) delivered to the bearer. Request they may be paid instead to the packers, who have now returned to Surat. Have dispatched, under the charge of Thomas Aldworth and some peons, the remainder of the goods they had provided, viz. 47 fardles for the Red Sea, 52 for the southwards, and one for England; the invoices are sent herewith. Have also forwarded 8 fardles of Ahmadābād goods, ‘which are whited heere’. The Governor, ‘Hamitt Chan’ [Himmat Khān], has not yet returned, and so they have not been able to show him the broadcloth, for <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-90" data-page="90" data-folio="51" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n89_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 51">[p. 51]</button> which they intend to ask 35 mahmūdīs the covado [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 21]. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received March</em> 3.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Johnson [at Swally] to the President at Surat</span>, <span class="sc">March</span> 3, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 121).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Intended to release the Malabar junks this day, but, finding that the commanders and pursers had gone to Surat, he desisted until they could be informed. Requests that this may be done. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">James Beversham at Swally to the President at Surat</span>, <span class="sc">March</span> 4, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 121).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The release of the Malabar junks has been deferred until the masters, &amp;c. are present, in order that they may give receipts for everything that is handed over. The Persian goods have been landed. Will assist Mr. Johnson as much as possible to get his vessel ready ; she has been careened and sufficiently repaired. Requests a receipt for the gold belonging to Mr. Jeffries, and orders for the disposal of his papers. Has sent ashore his Arabs, but Mr. Johnson is retaining his until he has spoken to the President. Would be glad to see him at Swally. Has two barrels of biscuits for Mr. Salbank. Should any of his men come to Surat without written permission, they should be sent back. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same</span>, <span class="sc">March</span> 4, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 122).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Moors have been set on shore, according to orders , fearing they may carry letters from ‘Rufrero ’, he has sent two men with them to search them. Requests that six or eight ‘ Guzeratts ’ may be hired to guard goods on shore. The Persian has received all his belongings, and has promised to hand over to the President any letters he may receive from ‘ Rufrero ’. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received March</em> 5.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Johnson</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-053" href="#sec-04-fn-053" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-053" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <span class="sc">at Tanjūr to Thomas Brockedon</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-054" href="#sec-04-fn-054" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-054" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, <span class="sc">March</span> 4, 1622 (<em>O. C.</em> 1036).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Loving and kind frind Mr. Thomas Brokidon, The case of my <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-91" data-page="91" data-folio="52" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n90_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 52">[p. 52]</button> wrighting to yow at this time is conserning two of the Companies men that is by chance litted one this place, where at this time I doe ly residente, sore against my will, but I hope the Lord will in His good time free me heareof, for I doe abide heare in paune, for the Captaine of our house is gone home and the Great Naige [Nāyak] deseired to have [one ?] to abide in the towne of Tangore by him, whear, having lost my shipp one Sealone, [I] was made choyse of. And conserning our voyage it is to long for me to wright thereof at this time, but the bearer heereof cann give yow a little lite therof. And as for these twoe men, I desier yow to thinke one the myseries that thay have indured to come into the Companies servies againe, for when thay came to me [it] made the watter roune doune my checks to looke one them, soe misserable ware thay bourned with the sonne, and hade noe more then there shirts, and being robbed of that little the King of Cochine had given them<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-055" href="#sec-04-fn-055" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-055" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>; and for myselfe my meanes is but small, yet for any honest men that goes in true afayres shall not want soe long as I have to helpe them, as I hope the bearer heareof cann report noe otherwise of me. And as for the persone that doth tarrie beehind by meanes of his sicknes, I thinke it not fitting for hime to take mony of the Naige, by reason that when yow doe send meanes, that then hee maye at his pleasure take his jornie, which otherwise hee maye staye hime att his pleaser. The Great Naige demands of me what the reason is that the English doth not deseier to trade in his land as well as the Portingall, saying thay shall have pepper and anything the land doth afford, and likewise buye those commodeties that thay doe bring with them, as tine, leade, iron, and redd cloth is well sould. Littell doth our nation knowe howe thay are excepted all this land; therefor the Danes doth trade under the name of the English and are marvilous well eused, hath given them a towne<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-056" href="#sec-04-fn-056" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-056" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and place to build a castell, which is finneshed, and hath thertie sixe peeces of ordinance mounted therin.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-92"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="92" data-folio="53" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n91_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 53">[p. 53]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2">JOHN JOHNSON AT SWALLY TO THE PRESIDENT AT SURAT, MARCH 5, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 122).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">His purser has been four days at Surat but has sent no provisions, and the crew begin to 'muter and complayne'. Begs that this may be remedied. The Malabar junks have been released. Requests that one of his carpenters may be sent down at once. Would be glad, if permitted, to make a step up to Surat himself. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">NICHOLAS BANGHAM, ROBERT HUTCHINSON, JUSTINIAN OFFLEY, AND THOMAS HAWKRIDGE AT BURHĀNPUR TO THE SURAT FACTORY, MARCH 6, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 124).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Hutchinson arrived here on March 2. A consultation was held, at which it was decided that, for the reasons stated in Bangham's letter of February 23, it would be inexpedient for him to leave Burhānpur at present, and Hutchinson and Hawkridge were appointed to proceed with the jewels to Agra in his place. Had they felt at liberty to sell the jewels here, they might have found a customer in 'Abdela Chan [Abdala Khān], who hath benn wondroslye importunate with us for any such thinge nowe against this Narrose [<em>see</em> p. 39] and hath offered to send home 30 or 40 thousand ruppees to buy any such jewell'. As, however, their orders were strict, 'besydes the feare wee have that it should come to this Princes eares, and therby to worke us a greater mischeife, wee utterly denyed any such thinge.' Hutchinson and Hawkridge will start on March 7 for Agra; 'the wayes thether, soe far as we can learne, are quiett, and as yet noe kind of disturbance to be heard of in this state, the King haveinge (as reporte sayeth) altered his determyned jornye for Cabull and is retorned for Agra and from thence for Ashmere' [Ajmer]. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received March 14.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">NICHOLAS BANGHAM AND JUSTINIAN OFFLEY AT BURHĀNPUR TO THE SURAT FACTORY, MARCH 6, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 125).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">'Sence my last unto you of the 23th past I have dayly followed the busynis of courte to procure an ende with those 17 peeces of tapistry taken into that sercare, but have been and am daylie <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-93" data-page="93" data-folio="54" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n92_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 54">[p. 54]</button> put of with faire words untill the tyme of the Narosse be passed; some of the best of them beinge sent to the Kinge as a present from the Prince, as I doe understand. The picture of Dyanna alsoe is sent as a present and (as the officers in courte affirmeth) that picture was given to this Prince by Jemserbeage as a presente; which hee dnieth and sayeth hee will see it shalbe paid for, but as yet nothinge done, nore have I any hope of an ende with that or the tappistrye untill this tyme of feastinge be past. Sence my last also the Prince sent to the howse for all the velvetts which he formerly refused; wherupon I chose out seven of the worst of them and carryed to him, whoe founde greate faulte with them but neverthelesse bad his officers take them, as indeed they did at what price they listed, urginge me, perforce as it weare, to sell them at 8¾ ruppees per coved; which I measured and delivered and made account for them at the price mentioned, wherof there is 9 per cent to be abated. Thuse you may perseave the extreame dealinge of this dooge and all his officers, with whome I desyre of God that I might never have to doe withall. Asuff Chann [Afzal Khān: <em>see</em> p. 31] is gon to Vezapoore on embassage, and heere is nowe in this place but Hackam Allamondon, to whom a man weare as good speake to a devill as to him aboute the busynis of our frinds in Agra; besydes the reports that goeth heere are such conserninge Assuff Chon as all men suppose him deade.' Enclose a copy of a letter received from Agra two days since. Have had trouble over the sale of their amber, but hope to settle the matter satisfactorily, and will forward the proceeds to Ahmadābād. The exchange is at present 6¼ per cent, but they fear it will rise when they seek to remit. Send an inventory of John Young's things, but most of his effects are at Agra. 'All the rest of the tapstrie be yet upon our hands. Wee hoped to have sould some of them against the Narrosse; but monye is very scante with such men, and loath they are to laye out soe much monye upon a comodity that serveth them but once a yeare. All the sattins and cloth of gould in howse; but all our ylcondicionened [<em>sic</em>] velvetts are sould, save one peece ylcolloured.' (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp. Received March 14.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-94"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="94" data-folio="55" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n93_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 55">[p. 55]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">President Fursland and Council at Batavia to the Company, March</span> 6, 1622 (<em>O. C.</em> 1039).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-057" href="#sec-04-fn-057" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-057" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">... ‘By letters receaved from Pallicatt wee understande of the Duches indirect proceedings with the factores there, as in all other places, which theire injurious dealings are insufferable, if wee any waye could helpe ourselves. The Duche in thatt place, as formerly in Amboyna, etc., pretende debts standinge out for 60,000 pago[da]s, and by this tricke appropriatte unto themselves the whole benefitte of thatt investment, and will not suffer us to have any share therein. They denie to make us acquainted with whatt sortes of clothe they buie, and if wee knowe any thinge of our owne experience wee must make them acquainted with itt, and they will share therein if they lyke itt. For accommodacion with howse roome, ware howse and counting howses, insted thereof they torne your factores into a cottadge, and thatt muste bee theire patterne to builde by, which is cleane contrary to whatt theire masters promised you. But wee have soe throughly by our formers acquainted Your Worships with theire proceedings in this kinde thatt wee shall not neede nowe further to inlardge heerin. But not onely in this they abuse you (which might for a tyme bee indured), but allsoe in the maine, your trade, which gives life to all our proceedinges, they extreamely wronge us. As nowe, theire shippe the <em>Lyon</em> arrivinge there, they caused all the clothe which was redye to bee brought in, and althoughe our monneies bee given out for parte thereof, which wee ought to receave, yet they take itt all themselves to serve theire present tornes, and heereby serve the markett in all places, whilste our goods must come in att leisure and theire pleasuers, and soe bee sent hither unto us a daye after the fayer, which is a manifest jurye, and noe small losse redounds unto you thereby. And when wee demaunde right heerein, wee are answeared with triviall excuses, and thatt they accounte sufficient satisfactione for us, not regardinge the Accorde, neyther equitye or justice, as wee have often formerly advized Your Worships. The coppies of the letters receaved from Pallicatt wee sende heerewith in the booke of coppies; wherein Your Worships maye more att lardge understande theire proceedings; to which wee referre you. Whereas <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-95" data-page="95" data-folio="56" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n94_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 56">[p. 56]</button> Mr. Mills writes hee hathe sent a breefe of the halfe yeares chardges, wee fynde noe such thinge, and therefore cannot advize whatt thatt chardge may amount to; but by guesse your parte cannot come to less then 6,000 r[ial]s per annum; which will fall heavie this yeare, when wee shall not bee able to supplye thatt factorye with a cappitall sufficient to provide any quantetye of clothe answeareable to the Hollanders investements. But soe large a quantetye as they nowe provide wee cannot yett perceave where they may vend them; but tyme will learne us, and wee shall accordingly make use of thatt factorye by providinge soe many sorts of clothe as may bee there procured fittinge for theise marketts, and give order for the lesse quantytye from Musulapatnam, if they may bee had better and cheaper in Pallicatt.’ At the latter place the English factors had twenty bales of goods ready, but the Dutch refused to receive them into their ship. In other places they refuse even to carry English letters. (1½ <em>pp. The rest of the letter deals with non-Indian affairs. Endorsed as received by the ‘Star’ about September</em> 26, 1622.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Rastell, Giles James, and Joseph Hopkinson at Surat to [the Ahmadābād Factory], March</span> 8, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2086, f. 83).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(<em>First part missing.</em>) Reprove them for not forwarding their accounts. They should keep in touch with the Burhānpur factors, who will remit them money as it becomes available. Cannot yet give them a definite list of goods to be provided this season; but will certainly need 10,000 pieces of the broader serijas, 10,000 of dutties, and 20,000 narrow baftas, at about 22, 25, and 30 rupees the corge [score; Hind. <em>korī</em>] respectively, or Rs. 53,500 in all. Rs. 9,000 recently remitted by exchange. News from Persia. The ships ‘are prosecutinge their ayreye enterprize in th[e Persian?] service against the castle of Ormoose’, of which Monnox is probably the instigator. Fear the result will only be to damage their masters’ estate. (<em>Copy.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">James Beversham at Swally to the President at Surat, March</span> 8, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 123).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Explanations as to Mr. Jeffries’ gold and papers. Sailors exceeding their time limit should be sent down. The ship is now ready to take in goods. Rejoices to hear that ‘Father Sa[lbank]’ is to go <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-96" data-page="96" data-folio="57" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n95_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 57">[p. 57]</button> with them to Mokha. Some of the Portuguese prisoners are sickly; requests permission to put them on shore. Expects some boats from Surat with wood, and to help in lading the goods. Wishes his purser were aboard and attending to his business. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes and John Parker at Agra to the Surat Factory, March</span> 8, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 141).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Burhānpur factors have advised their inability to procure ‘the Princes letter to Assuffe Chon and this Governour for our inlargument’, and have left them ‘to procure redresse from courte ourselves or to persiste in your resolucion, which what it is wee apprehend not, unlesse to patience, which already in some good measure observe, by induringe the insufferable wronges and disgrace of these our oppresors, still hopeinge for some releife, eyther from you or your courte; which not arryveinge hath urged the sendinge you this expresse, chiefly to advertice you Assuff Chons answere to our petition longe sence put upp, which for your beter understandinge and sattisfaxione a coppie theirof wee send you here inclosed [<em>not entered</em>], which wee intreat you well to peruse, wherby you will perceave how much hee is incensed against us, whether justlye or not yourselves are best acquented and wee wholy ignorante eyther of his informacion or intentione, yet hee threatned us shrodly and will not affoard us lybertye, eyther of our persons or goods, untill such tyme hee be trewly asertayned from the governours and merchants of Guzeratt that wee have made full restetucion of the people and goods hee supposeth us to have taken. Soe that now you maye perceave our onely remydie to rely in you, who (as wee formerly have intreated) are to procure under som generall writinge which may cleare us of these imputacions the affirmacion of the Governeurs of Surratt, Cambaya, Amadavad, &amp;c., as also most of the princepalist merchants their abydinge to put ther hands therto.
