Without some such writinge wee have noe hopes to be sett at lybertye, and therfore if you purpose our releife wee hope you will endeuour it and that suddaynely; for as wee are willinge to indeuour [endure] restraints and bad usadge for the good of our masters proceedings, yet on the contrarye wee should be unwillinge to be further ingaged theirin then with the sauftye of our <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-97" data-page="97" data-folio="58" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n96_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 58">[p. 58]</button> persons wee might be assured, and therfore in such case what your proceedings maye be wee (as yet asertaynd to us) besydes [<em>sic</em>] your advice etc. and that to be tymely ; the wante therof hath alreadye brought us farther intangled then otherwise might have ben prevented. The barer our servante that brought us this answere from courte reportes the Nabob to have given order for the restoreinge the Syndamens 8,000 and odd rupp[ees] oute of this our cash in their posessione, whose performence therof wee shortly expect, though cannot withstand. Mr. Younge is retorned from Lahore to Symmana, where he and Mr. Willowbye are disbursinge their monye lefte ; which done, Mr. Younge purposeth to retorne for Agra, wee not haveinge abylytie or meanes to supplye him with anye more monyes untill this our troubles are come to some peryod ; which when may be is most incertayne. The Kinge and his lesker is gon directly for Cabull and by this tyme maye be their arryved, beinge lefte by our servante 40 course [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 82] beyond Lahore. Yf wee maye yet gett lycence and Jado to goe, we purpose to send Mr. Willowby and him to courte, although wee conceave it bootlesse untill you send us such certeficate as you may be confedent can releave us, unlesse it maye be that they may be redye their to receave them ’. Bangham writes that John Young is dead in Burhānpur. Send list of his effects at Agra. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp. Received March</em> 28.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Halstead and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, March</span> 9, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 138).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Their bills received and the money obtained. Send an inventory of all goods &amp;c. remaining at the time of Mr. Bickford’s departure. Have forwarded by William Layton (who accompanies Mr. Bickford) a specimen dutty of Dholka of special size ; request orders concerning it, and also as to the quantity of striped and unstriped serijas wanted. Would also like their opinion regarding the advisability of selling off all their lead and tin. ‘ Wee perseave the Dutches <em>Wezopps</em> [<em>Wesp’s</em>] arryvall, with her fortunate robeinge ; as also the happie successe our frinds have had over their enymies about Ormose.’ Inquire whether any ships are to be sent to England this year. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p. Received March</em> 26.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-98"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="98" data-folio="59" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n97_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 59">[p. 59]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham and Justinian Offley at BurhĀnpur to the Surat Factory, March</span> 10, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 128).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The enclosed letter has just arrived, with one to themselves, ‘wherin our frinds at Agra exprese ther still contenuence in their trouble; which is noe litle greife unto us, we not knowinge at this tyme to whom to seeke for releife, Assuffe Chon [<em>see</em> p. 31] beinge gon into Decan on embassadge and not as yet retorned, and here noe man in his place for us to solicite, the Prince beinge now in the hight of his Narosse and not any such busynis to be put upp unto him; besydes, the common reporte of the death of Assuff Chon, or at least his imprisonement, causes a kinde of discontent in this courte, as in our last to them wee advised, desiring [   ] seeke some helpe by themselves, for that heere were litle hopes to assist them in theire [   ].’ Have sold their amber and will remit the proceeds to Ahmadābād. <em>P.S.</em>—‘Jamserbeage’ has offered to have Rs. 2,500 paid in Ahmadābād against a like sum paid to him here. To this they have agreed. (<em>Copy.  <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>2</sub> p.  Received March</em> 18.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William [Methwold], Matthew Duke, and Francis Futter at Masulipatam to the Surat Factory, March</span> 10, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 113).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As to letters sent. <em>P.S.</em>—‘[The] newest newes here is that Sultan Caroone hath slayne [his eldest ?] brother, but after what maner wee know as little as of what [   ].’ (<em>Copy. Fragment only.  <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>4</sub> p.  Received May</em> [   ].)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Henry Wheatley, Purser of the</span> <em>Lion</em>, <span class="sc">at Swally to the President, March</span> 11, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 123).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Requests that the 2,000 ‘coveds’ of duties for sails may be brought aboard at once. Has shipped most of the stores. Robert Breedstreet, the purser of the <em>Rose</em>, is at Surat, and might be directed to look after the provision of stores for his ship. The matter is pressing, if the <em>Rose</em> is to accompany the <em>Lion</em>. (<em>Copy. <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>4</sub> p.  Received same day.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-99"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="99" data-folio="60" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n98_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 60">[p. 60]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">James Beversham at Swally to the President at Surat</span>, <span class="sc">undated</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 124).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has just heard that he intends to bring some friends aboard the ships. Regrets he had not longer notice, for they are short of provisions and their ship is sore pestered, as the hold is being cleaned; however, he will do the best he can. Mr. Garrett might have warned him that strangers were coming. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Richard Swanley’s Journal of his Voyage from Surat to the Red Sea and back</span> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xxxiv. p. 41).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-058" href="#sec-04-fn-058" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-058" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1622, <em>March</em> 14. Commenced to lade goods. <em>March</em> 16. ‘This day night, the skiffe being by the shipps side, the master commanded the boatswaine to more the skiffe astarne. So through the boatswaines forgettfullnes it was neglected. So about midnight Capt. Ryfrera with two Portugalls more and a neger, watching their opertunity, the watch being asleepe, gott into the skiff and went ashoare. So half an hower after, the skiff was missed. Then wee made after them ashoare, but could not heare of them, for they made presently for the river of Surratt, and so gott over. The one of them was drowned; the other gott to Deman, which afterward we heard by leters which Ryfrera sent to the Presedent of Surratt.’ <em>March</em> 24. The <em>Lion</em> came out of Swally Port. ‘This day the Duch shippe set saile with a smale pinace, the shipp bound for Bantom, and the pinace for the [Red ?] Sea.’ <em>March</em> 25. The <em>Rose</em> and the <em>Richard</em> came over the bar. <em>March</em> 26. The President and merchants were set ashore at Surat bar, and the ships sailed. <em>March</em> 29. The <em>Richard</em> being in distress was relieved of part of her cargo. <em>April</em> 18. A dispute between the boatswain’s men and the carpenter’s assistants having led to blows, the chief offender was ducked three times from the yard’s arm. <em>May</em> 3. Socotra sighted. <em>May</em> 4. Anchored near Tamrida. <em>May</em> 5. Three of the merchants went on shore, and carried a present to the King, who gave them in return some sheep, goats, and dates. Water brought aboard. <em>May</em> 6. After obtaining some more provisions, the voyage <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-100" data-page="100" data-folio="61" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n99_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 61">[p. 61]</button> was resumed. <em>May</em> 8. Lost sight of the <em>Rose</em>. <em>May</em> 17. Saw the coast of Abyssinia. <em>May</em> 22. Saw the coast of Arabia, to the eastward of Aden. <em>May</em> 25. Mr. Heynes went ashore to find out where they were, but could not speak with any of the natives. <em>June</em> 6. Sighted the African coast, about 23 leagues from the Straits. <em>June</em> 7. Saw 'a very faire towne . . . with three churches or steeples in it' [Zeila?]. <em>June</em> 7. Met the <em>Richard</em> again. 'In the tyme of her being from us, shee keeping on the Arrab side tooke a gyllboate [<em>jalba</em>, an Arab boat] neere Aden, by report of their owne men worth 14,000 [<em>sic</em>] ryalls of eight.' <em>June</em> 8. 'In the evening wee went through the Babb.'<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-059" href="#sec-04-fn-059" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-059" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <em>June</em> 9. Anchored off Mokha. <em>June</em> 10. The merchants went ashore and were kindly received by the Governor. At their return 'they brought newes that there was a matter of 20 or 30 Duchmen prisoners ashoare, which wer taken for the abuses and wrongs that the <em>Sampson</em> and hir consort did to the juncke the yeare before; which by reporte they being in Mocha Road gave passes to the juncks, and when they came without the Babb they tooke five of them and killed and hove overbord many of the people. So theis two shipps coming from Bantom, making account to have traded there, after they had landed their goods they tooke there master and men, being ashoare, prisoners, making them slaves.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-060" href="#sec-04-fn-060" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-060" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> In the meane tyme there shipps went over to Assab, for to fitt themselves with water'. <em>July</em> 6. Set sail from Mokha. <em>July</em> 10. The <em>Richard</em> and the <em>Lion's</em> long boat went to an island near Asab to cut wood. <em>July</em> 15. A letter received from the King of Asab apologising for the theft of some linen. <em>July</em> 16. Returned to their anchorage at Mokha. <em>August</em> 13. The merchants came aboard, 'with at least 50 or 60 <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-101" data-page="101" data-folio="62" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n100_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 62">[p. 62]</button> Turckes that wer passengers to goe for Surratt with us.’ In the evening they set sail. <em>August</em> 14. Passed through the Straits, in company with ‘ the juncke of Meslepotan and the juncke of Surratt, with four small juncks more’. <em>August</em> 15. The <em>Lion</em> grounded, but was got off again. <em>August</em> 24. Agreed by consultation not to make the coast of India till September 20, for fear of foul weather. <em>September</em> 14. Saw the mainland of India, near Bassein. <em>September</em> 16. Passed two Dutch ships, the <em>Wapen</em> and <em>North Holland</em>. <em>September</em> 18. Met a junk bound from Surat to Achin. Heard from them that Ormus was taken and that the English at Surat were well. <em>September</em> 19. Anchored off Swally. <em>September</em> 23. Two Dutch ships, the <em>Fortune</em> and <em>South Holland</em>, belonging to the Fleet of Defence, anchored near them. <em>September</em> 24. They all went into Swally harbour. ‘In the afternoone our fleett came from Ormous with victory. Praised be God.’ <em>October</em> 2. Sighted the fleet from England, which next day came into the harbour. <em>October</em> 25. The <em>Exchange</em>, <em>Anne</em> and <em>Diamond</em>, of the Fleet of Defence, came into the road. <em>November</em> 19. The <em>Exchange</em>, <em>Anne</em>, <em>Blessing</em>, <em>Reformation</em>, and <em>Dolphin</em> went out of the port. <em>November</em> 23. The Fleet of Defence sailed, leaving the <em>Diamond</em> to lade provisions and meet them off Goa. <em>December</em> 6. The <em>Blessing</em>, <em>Whale</em>, <em>Dolphin</em>, and <em>Reformation</em> set sail for Jask. <em>December</em> 7. Two Dutch ships arrived from the southwards, one Lemon commander. <em>December</em> 13. The <em>Discovery</em> and the <em>Rose</em> sailed for Batavia. <em>December</em> 16. One of the Dutch ships and a Dutch pinnace sailed. <em>December</em> 27. The other Dutch ship departed.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-061" href="#sec-04-fn-061" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-061" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (20 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">JOHN JOHNSON AT SWALLY TO THE PRESIDENT AT SURAT, MARCH 15, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 126).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A boat or two needed to help in lading the ships, as his own boat is worm-eaten and leaky. Requests also an order for a supply of wine from Mr. Beversham. Is most anxious to know whither his ship is to be sent. (<em>Copy.</em> ¼ <em>p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-102"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="102" data-folio="63" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n101_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 63">[p. 63]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3">EDWARD HEYNES [AT SWALLY] TO THE PRESIDENT AT SURAT, MARCH 16, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 127).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The carters, having been kept waiting two days for their unlading, have at last thrown down their goods and departed. Begs the dispatch of thirty or forty porters to carry the bales aboard; also orders to the ships to assist. Johnson is anxious to know the destination of his vessel, which is now ready to take in goods. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">JOHN CLARK AT JAHĀNGĠRPUR TO THE PRESIDENT AT SURAT, MARCH 16, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 127).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The 'addowaye' [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 129] has provided 15 carts, which are now being laden. Requests a supply of chaff to place under the bundles when they are stored on arrival. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">PRESIDENT FURLAND AND COUNCIL AT BATAVIA TO THE COMPANY, MARCH 16, 1622 (<em>O. C.</em> 1041).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">. . . 'Haveinge veiwed the musters sent us from Pallecatt, wee find thatt as good sortes maie bee provided at Pettepooly and other places with lesse charge and more freedome. Soe thatt in all places where wee are under the Hollanders you purchasse noethinge but excessive charges and a slavishe subjectione to theire insolent wills; which you maie please to consider of, whether to bee free from them and in other places may not bee more beneficiall for your trade then to live under theire subjectione and take theire leaveinges'. (<em>Copy. ½ p. The rest of the letter deals with affairs at Batavia, &amp;c.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">THOMAS RASTELL AT SURAT TO JAMES BEVERSHAM AT SWALLY, MARCH 16, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2086, <em>f.</em> 85).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Requests him to provide Mr. Johnson with a country boat, as his own is useless. He is also asking for wine. How much does he want? And can Beversham spare him any? 'Ruy Freire will bee a troublesome guest in the voyadge, if hee bee not fittly accomodated.' He and his companions should be lodged in the poop, an awning <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-103" data-page="103" data-folio="64" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n102_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 64">[p. 64]</button> for which is being provided. As for clothing and linen, Rastell will gladly provide him on learning his wants. No time should be lost in taking in the cargo. <em>P.S.</em>—‘Lett noe powder bee wasted, I pray you.’ (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to John Johnson at Swally, March</span> 16, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2086, <em>f.</em> 85).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As to the provision of a country boat. Has also written to Mr. Beversham about the wine. It has been decided that his vessel is to accompany the <em>Lion</em>, but for the present this is to be kept a profound secret. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to Ruy Freire in Swally Road, March</span> 16, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 85).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has received his letter by Signor Alfonso. Regrets he cannot entirely meet his wishes, but if he will inform him of what he stands in need he will do his best to supply him. (<em>Italian.</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-062" href="#sec-04-fn-062" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-062" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <em>Copy. Damaged. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Rastell, Giles James, and Joseph Hopkinson at Surat to the President and Council at Batavia, March</span> 17, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 84).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-063" href="#sec-04-fn-063" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-063" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forward copy of a letter of January 24, 1622 [<em>see</em> p. 25], sent in the <em>Sampson</em> under the charge of Signor Gooree. Since then her consort, the <em>Weasopp</em>, has arrived (February 23), having spent some time on the coast of Arabia, where she ‘hath proved hirselfe as notorious a theefe as hir fellow, by the roberrie of two other juncks of Chaule and Diew, the latter yeeldinge only certaine Sindie toyes, horses, &amp;c., of small vallew, but the other inricht with gould and silver to good worth, which proceeded from goods were sould in Mocha’. According to report these amounted to not less than 30,000<em>l.</em> sterling. Only a small proportion landed in India. Fear the English will be damaged by their proceedings. Arrival on February 27 of the <em>Lion</em>, <em>Rose</em>, and <em>Richard</em> from Persia, ‘where <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-104" data-page="104" data-folio="65" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n103_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 65">[p. 65]</button> contrary to all order, and to the greate dishonour of our nation amongst Christians, the rest of the fleete have contracted themselves to the Persian service and are prosecutinge his ends (not ours) in warr against the Portingall. Their first victorie (as they call it) was takinge of a late erected castle sated on an iland some three leagues distant from Ormoos, and callde by the name of Kishmee; where, except for benifitt of the porte, which they affirme to yeeld 6 fadam water within muskett shott of the castle, noe proffitt at all will accrew to our masters therebye, unles it bee found in the advancinge of an English flag, attended by only four of our people, who with the others collours are guarded by five or six hundred of theirs; yett ours doe vainely call it their fortresse, and demand munition by the next for its better strengthninge; whilst in the interim they will trye their vallours on Ormoos, but still in asistance of the Persian designes; for which they have agreed for 2,000 <em>li.</em> pay by the month, and (after the castles surprizall) have the promise and firme of a subject to warrant performance of contract; and all this contrived by Monax to the confusion and distracting of all our bussines.' The silk is still on board the fighting ships, while the <em>Lion</em>, &amp;c., came to Surat empty, bringing only Ruy Freire and eight other captains taken at Kishm, 'whome wee resolve to deteyne in our shippes till a mandate from Goa, [signed] by the Vice Roye and sealed with the armes of Portingall, bee brought us for redeeminge of those English prizoners to southwards.' Hope the fleet will succeed in destroying the Portuguese galleons at Ormus; if they do, 'their stay will bee the better excused.' In the meantime the opportunity of the 'execution of our designes on these people' has been lost. Moreover, it has become necessary to send the <em>Lion</em>, <em>Rose</em>, and <em>Richard</em> to Mokha, and therefore they will not be able to supply Sumatra and Batavia this year. The only chance is that these vessels may meet the fleet at 'the Baub' [<em>see</em> p. 61], in which case the <em>Rose</em> will proceed on her voyage 'towards you'. 'To all these crosses unexpected may well bee added the doubtfull events and revolts which maye succeed in this countrie by the death of the Kings eldest sonn, whoe in absence of the Kinge, remote in his progresse, was strangled in Brampore by Corum his brother.' <em>P.S.</em>—No news of the Fleet of Defence. (<em>Copy.</em> 2¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-105"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="105" data-folio="66" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n104_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 66">[p. 66]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes at Jahāngīrpur to the President at Surat, March</span> 17, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 127).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Martin and Barber had stowed many of the bundles before his arrival. Some must of necessity lie abroad till carts can be procured to take them to Ranele.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-064" href="#sec-04-fn-064" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-064" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Is off to Swally, to send up the carts there. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Joseph Salbank and Edward Heynes at Swally to the President at Surat, March</span> 18, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 127).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">French has brought down the native porters. He wishes to return to Surat, but this they will prevent. The porters are not now required, as they have obtained 'coolyes [<em>see</em> p. 153 <em>note</em>] of this place at a lesse charge'; still, they will utilize as many as possible. The goods intended for Mokha seem to be too many to be got into the <em>Lion</em>; recommend that the <em>Rose</em> be sent as well. Would be glad if the President could come down to Swally. <em>P.S.</em>—The Surat porters have left, refusing to work for less than a mahmūdī per bundle. Any money paid in advance to them should be recovered. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">James Beversham at Swally to the President at Surat, March</span> 18, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 129).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Repudiates charges of negligence, and explains the difficulty of stowing the goods. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham and Justinian Offley at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory, March</span> 18, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 140).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">After setting out on their journey to Agra, Hutchinson and Hawkridge were obliged to return owing to the latter's illness; but he is now better, and they will make a fresh start. The country is quiet, and the King by all accounts is on his way to Kābul, intending to winter there. Request a supply of goods for sale before the rains. The amber beads have all been sold, and Rs. 3,000 received on account, 'with allowance of 75 dayes for inkist.'<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-065" href="#sec-04-fn-065" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-065" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Rs. 12,500 <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-106" data-page="106" data-folio="67" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n105_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 67">[p. 67]</button> remitted to Ahmadābād. More shall be sent as the outstanding debts are realized. Can get no satisfaction regarding the tapestry in the Prince's sarkār. The success at Ormus will raise the reputation of the English. 'Jem[sir] Beage hath acquented this Prince with that newes, who s[eem]ed [to] be glad theirof.' The business of 'Rampaxad' cannot be settled until his bills arrive from Agra. 'Hee hath not as yet recovered any monye on his bratts [<em>barāt</em>, 'an assignment on the revenue'] nor cannot untill the nixt harvest, when he promyseth to see [us] first sattisfyed.' (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received March 28.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Rastell at Surat to James Beversham at Swally, March 18, 1622</span> (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS. 2086, f. 86</em>).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Hearing that the work of lading the ship was proceeding but slowly, he yesterday sent forty porters and two large boats to help. There will now be no excuse for delay. As for getting all the goods into the ship, he feels sure this can be done, and will not believe any assertion to the contrary. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Rastell at Surat to Joseph Salbank at Swally, March 18, 1622</span> (<em>Ibid., f. 86</em>).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Is as much vexed as Salbank over the delay in lading the <em>Lion</em>. Has now dispatched boats and porters to assist. As for the ship not holding the goods, 'I vow that not a bale of private goods (without favour unto any) shall bee hindrance thereunto.' If any of the Company's goods are left behind for the sake of taking in private trade, he will seize the latter to the Company's use. Sends 100 mahmūdīs by the bearer. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Clark at Rānder to the President at Surat, March 19, 1622</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 129).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The indigo has been stored and nearly fills five houses. Requests sanction for hiring another house at 22 mahmūdīs a month to store the cotton goods. A dozen locks wanted. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Lancaster at Rānder to the Same, March 19, 1622</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 129).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Difficulties in carting the goods. (<em>Copy. ¼ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-107"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="107" data-folio="68" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n106_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 68">[p. 68]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Richard Barber at Jahāngīrpur to the President at Surat, March</span> 20, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 134).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As requested, is sending down the goods intended for the southwards under the charge of John George. After checking and housing the Baroda goods destined for England, will himself repair to Surat. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received same day.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Halstead and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, March</span> 21, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 138).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received theirs of the 8th, ‘with the inclosed contract betwixt our frinds at Ormose and the Pertians, wherunto wee have not to saye more then that wee are hartely sorrye that soe unwarrentable and tedyouse an enterprize is soe unadvisedly undertaken, and pray God give soe happie an issue therto as our masters be not damenefyed theirby.’ As regards their ‘lynen investments’, have provided 1,400 pieces of long narrow baftas ‘of this place’, and about 300 of ‘the shorte, called Tuckeryes’ [<em>cf. previous volume</em>, p. 197 <em>note</em>]. The prices of the former range from Rs. 1¼ to Rs. 3 per piece, and of the latter from Rs. 1¼ to [   ]. They could buy 200 pieces a day, but are careful to reject all of inferior quality. Are expecting a large quantity of striped serijas from Dholka. As for dutties, they request details of the size and price before putting them in hand. ‘The difference nowe betwixt takeinge upp and deliverye of monye by exchange for Surratt . . . is todaye <sup>9</sup>⁄<sub>16</sub> and ½ rupp[ee] per hundred m[ahmūdīs].’ Yesterday acquainted the Burhānpur factors of the rates of exchange for bills on that city, ‘which was the takers upp shall receave 91½ and the deleverers to pay 92¾ rupp[ees] seccawes [<em>see</em> p. 19], to pay or receave 100 Brampore rupp[ees].’ ‘The other day wee weare called before Mammood Tukey [Mahmūd Taqī], the Princes Deuwan [Dīwān], to the [<em>sic</em>] answer to the demands of certayne Voras<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-066" href="#sec-04-fn-066" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-066" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (wherof Taja Chan [Tāj Khān] is cheife), who demanded but restetucion of 10,000 ryalls of eight which wee had robed oute of a juncke of Choule; which wee wondred to heare, but presently perceaved it to be that our frinds the Dutch have robed, and soe tould them they should goe aske what they had lost of the Hollanders, another <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-108" data-page="108" data-folio="69" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n107_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 69">[p. 69]</button> nation; wherunto they answered that wee weare joyned as one and that all wee tooke or stoole wee shared, and wished us give better sattisfaxione then words, or elce we could not be beleived but that wee weare one; withall crabedly tellinge us they would give us 15 dayes rispite to write for better assurence, but after that tyme wee should heere more of them; which wee leave to your consyderacions, and pray you thinke upon some course to make these dogges beleve treweth and that our negociacions be not hindred.' <em>P.S.</em>— 'The exchange for Surratt: to take up, att rupp[ees] 42¼ and 42–3 raines; to deliver, att rupp[ees] 42¾.' (<em>Copy. 1¾ pp. Received March 27.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham and Justinian Offley at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory, March</span> 24, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 143).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">'In ours of the 18th present wee advised of all then need[full]; sence when Robert Hutchinsone and Thomas Hawkridge are once agayne departed on their jornye towards Agra with all such jewells as by you soe inordered. Wee have also receaved, perused and speeded your letters for Agra, and with them receaved two letters of Isacke Beages to Assuff Chon [Afzal Khān: <em>see</em> p. 31], and the other to his vekill. [That] to his vekill wee have delivered, but that to Assuff Chon wee cannot till his [retorne] from Vezapoore; by whose absence (as formerly advised) we are altogeather [frust]rated of our hopes in procureinge a firmaune to Agra, and now less the[n ever?] that this Prince is postinge hence with all speed towards Mando and from [thence], as reporte sayeth, to Asmeere; his tents are this daye gon forth, and all m[en] affirmeth that upon the 20th [<em>sic</em>] present hee himself wilbe gone. Heere [is] great preparacion for his jornye, and like to be trewe. Noe man here knoweth [the] reasone of his suddayne departure.' Have done their best to procure payment for the tapestry, but the Prince's officers are in no hurry to settle the matter. 'They themselves are both merchants and chapmen, and hath never asked us what wee will take for them, but have offered us 16 rupp[ees] per coved, and more wee shall not gett, nether be masters of our owne goods agayne.' Fear that if they accept this offer the Khānkhānān will insist on a similar reduction in the price of those he has taken. As, however, it is very desirable to conclude the business before the Prince starts, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-109" data-page="109" data-folio="70" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n108_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 70">[p. 70]</button> and in any case they do not want the tapestry thrown back on their hands, they have decided to agree to his terms if they cannot get an advance. Lead is worth Rs. 9½ and 10 the great maund. Any goods intended for this place should be sent early, in order that sales may be effected before the rains. The money due for coral will be remitted to Ahmadābād. ‘Rampashad's’ business is at a standstill for want of the papers from Agra. <em>P.S.</em>—‘Chunderouse [maize: Arabic <em>khandarūs</em>] is worth from 15 to 35 rupp[ees] per great maune in this place, and (as informed) a good quantetye will vende.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp. Received April</em> 2.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[THE FACTORS AT MASULIPATAM] TO THE SURAT FACTORY, [MARCH 29, 1622] (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 151).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have little news. ‘Our resydence is placed in so obseque [obscure?] a corner, soe meanly stocked and soe little valued by our masters and their ministers that if in twelve months wee may but see an English shippe wee accounte our indevours infinitly countenanced.’ Their chief intelligence comes from the Hollanders, ‘who with their stoore of shippinge are heere and everywhere upon this coast, discoveringe newe trades and supprizinge sometymes fatt boottyes: [and?] although wee can better affoard them then their proper owners [yet?] wee could wish wee had also meanes to share with them, sence [we are?] equall in their greatist charge.’ The <em>Schiedam</em> and another Dutch ship sent towards Ceylon in [December?] to intercept the Portuguese shipping from Malacca, caught sight of the enemy on January 1 and attacked them. A Portuguese frigate was captured, the crew of which jumped overboard, ‘trustinge more to the mercy of the sea then the inraged Hollanders.’ Of the whole crew only thirteen survived, all wounded and now prisoners at Pulicat. ‘Amongst those that perished in their furye their fell one Englishman named Henry Batchelor, one of the Unycornes company, sent with his fellow, one Thomas Robinsone, from Macca [Macao] to be further captived in Goa.’ (<em>Copy. Unfinished.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">PULICAT FACTORY ACCOUNTS, APRIL 1 TO JUNE 26, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. xxiv. p. 29).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Journalized entries of goods bought or issued. (3 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-110"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="110" data-folio="71" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n109_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 71">[p. 71]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, April</span> 3, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 144).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Martin and the rest arrived here safely yesterday with the money, viz. 18,000 mahmūdīs. Return ten mahmūdīs by Robert Tottle, and beg that they may be exchanged for better ones. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received April</em> 4.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Halstead and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, April</span> 7, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 145).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Your last of the prime present we yest[erday] receaved, with the inclosed in our behalves from Isacke Beage to Mah[mūd] Tuckie ; and not doubtinge any such rigorouse dealinge as it seemes you feared or suspected, wee went this morninge to deliver ; which Tuckie have[ing] read, answered us that Merza Isacke Beage had write very favourably [in our] behalfes, but till hee had replye from your Governour of an expresse [sent] him aboute this busynis hee would saye noe more to eyther partye. [We] hope, as our greatist feare is past, soe is our trouble for a tyme, exce[pt en] corradgment come from the Prince or your Governour &amp;c. ; for, as wee are [told ?] by two greate ones in this towne, Saffie Chan and Merza Myna [Mīrzā Mainā ?], R[ustam ?] Chon (six dayes sence came in from robbinge the countrye), upon manye complaints against us at dabarr [<em>darbār</em>], in publicke sayd wee and the Hollanders weare not one people, and as it playnely appeared traded aparte ; but what trust their is to any of these greate ones sayeings or doeinge wee pray God wee never experyence, and promyse you, when we certaynely here and are perswayded we shalbe layd hould on to sattisfaxione [of ?] the Dutches robberyes, wele use our utmost endevours soe to order or remitt all our monyes in cash as it may be at yours or other our fellowe servants disposure that wee heare are least disturbed. Itts much wondred at heere (wee clere of the facte) wee should be called in question, and the archtheeves Hollanders suffered to domyneer in every place without molestacion, espetially in Surratt, breeds suspetion in many that your Governour is a sharer in their ill begotten goods. The complaints heere not soe much for justice as formerly, but have <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-111" data-page="111" data-folio="72" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n110_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 72">[p. 72]</button> framed their generall petition to the Prince [for?] revenge upon the wronges sustayned by the English and Dutch and desyre of justice, wherin that the Prince may adjudge the robers to restetucion. We leave to your discretions to cause him [to?] be informed wee are nether faltie nor partners in honist trade or robberyes with them.' Have received bills from Burhānpur for Rs. 12,500, all of which were accepted, save one of Rs. 2,500 drawn by 'Jemseir Beage' on 'Merza Myna', who will give no certain answer. It is much better to remit money in the ordinary way than to deal with 'dishonist greate ones, whose meanes and creaditts are not worth a strawe longer then they are in their Princes favour, and [how] instable that is daylye experyence shewes.' Have instructed the Dholka brokers not to buy any more striped serijas, but all plain; they are also to provide dutties of given sizes. Note the orders about baftas. (<em>Copy. 1½ pp. Received April 14.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, April 12, 1622</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 144).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">'Our markitts doth contenewe in the same frame, without augmentacion of quantetys. For narrowe baftas the Dutch doth buye dayly as much as wee buy; which wee cannot prevent, for wee intend not to rayse the price of any thinge wee buye, commodities beinge now at reasonable rates. Wee must attend for quicker markitts, which wee hope wilbe within a month or two.' Disapprove of the proposal to send cotton cloth from Ahmadābād to Broach to be bleached, as after the rains their washers will be fully employed; besides, the double packing gives trouble. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received April 13.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Halstead and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, April 16, 1622</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 147).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">'Our last to you was of the 7th present, in answere to yours of the prime, wherin [told?] you that wee hoped our troubles weare past for a while; which proved much lesse then we imagined, for the fourth daye after, Mr. Hill with our broker goeinge to Merza Myna, to receave mony or his answere aboute his depte per exchange from Br[ampore], was mett by Mussuff Chons [Mausūf <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-112" data-page="112" data-folio="73" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n111_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 73">[p. 73]</button> Khān's] men comeinge on purpose to call us, and so ca[rried] before him, who demanded restetucion of to the value of 10,000 rupp[ees]<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-067" href="#sec-04-fn-067" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-067" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> or theraboutes wee had taken of his in the Choule juncke &amp;c.; which noe way being acknowlidged, they were kept prisoners in his howse four dayes and four nights, [a?] Jewe and I laboringe to cleere them by makeinge frinds and tellinge our tale [to great ?] ones. The second eveninge came before our hellhound Governowr, who stopeinge [his ears ?] against all our and our frinds reasons sayd wee were ransadoes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-068" href="#sec-04-fn-068" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-068" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and one with the [Dutch ?], and comanded the Cottwall<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-069" href="#sec-04-fn-069" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-069" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> to keepe us saufe till nixt morninge, and that then hee [would] call us and the complaynents (Voras [<em>see</em> p. 68] currs) and doe justice; which accordingly [he did ?]. And in assembly of all the greate ones in the cittye hee tould the Voras that hee kn[ewe] that the Hollanders and wee were not one, and he sayd he would comand the Holl[anders] hether to here their answeres herein, and in the intrim bed the Cottwall let me free upon giveinge suertie for my person to appeare when caled for per Rustem Chon ; [which was ?] given to content, yet nothinge could be well till the Cottwall had somethinge to eate betle<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-070" href="#sec-04-fn-070" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-070" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>; soe wee agreed with him for 25 rupp[ees], besydes somthinge that his followers had, which I could noe wayes shunne, it beinge a custome that all which come in the comon prisone must paye or have their clothes torne from their backs. God keepe us a second tyme out of their cloches. Mr. Hill was cleared, Gurdas [Gurdās] beinge suerty for his personall appearence. Wee are informed by the complaynants that yesterday the Governowr dispeeded his letters to call the Dutch from Barroch, and to day will doe the like to call some from Surratt. Besydes, the Cambaya Governowr (who is present heere and our great enymie) hath sayd hee layd [will lay ?] hould of those Hollanders in his towne when hee knoweth they have in their hands soe much as this yeare they meane their to disburse. Soe that its playne wee shall not be undisturbed till the one or other of us have given some kind of sattisfaxione. The Governowr (whose <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-113" data-page="113" data-folio="74" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n112_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 74">[p. 74]</button> word the Devell hath noe reasone to beleve) sayd who is found in falte shall onely give sattisfaxione; wherfore wee intreate you use your best endevours to procure your Governors, officers and merchants acknowledgments that wee and the Hollanders are not one, and that they and not wee have robed theise juncks, and that it was their 40 [<em>sic</em>] men and not ours that was slayne in the Dabull junke<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-071" href="#sec-04-fn-071" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-071" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (for our enymies say all was done by the English), and such other prouffes as your discretions shall thinke will best demonstrate our inocencie and their knavery; and dispeed them, wee pray, by this expresse or oth[er with] what expedicion posseble, and afforde us your directions how [wee] shall proceed in this troublsome busynis, and that whether you thinke it w[ould] be better for us (if worst come to worst) to buye justice with some res[onable] bribe then be adjudged to pay all, halfe, or any parte, for wee con[seave] these currs (Voras &amp;c.) thinketh the Dutch have not suffetient in this kingdom to sattisfye their demaunds, and therfore endevour to drawe us in as also culpable; which if a bribe may prevent, wee thinke it will no[t] be [ill bestowed?] neyther would some small presents, if wee had them, wh[erof if] you have to spare, wee pray you remember us.' Are arranging to put out all their ready money, and request orders whether they shall sell off their lead and tin. 'Att Suffi Chans requests (our extreordynery frind) and Merza Mynas, and in regard of our troubles, wee have not yet [claimed?] the rupp[ees] 2,500, but as today by both very seryously promysed it.' (<em>Copy.</em> 1<span class="sc">¾</span> <em>pp. Received April</em> 20.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Young [at Agra] to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">April</span> [ ], 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 113).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote on April 3, announcing his arrival in Agra with a quantity of cloth equivalent to the full value of the money sent him. Willoughby set out for the royal camp on March 14. With regard to the excess expenditure on coach and horses, the fault is not his but the Agra factors'. He was only given one galledback horse (since dead), and his request for the loan of one of their two coaches, with a second horse, was refused. Thinks there is much waste in household expenses and unnecessary <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-114" data-page="114" data-folio="75" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n113_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 75">[p. 75]</button> peons. Begs to be allowed to return to England as soon as possible. Considers that the factory might well be dissolved, for Agra yields nothing of itself, and indigo, the chief article of merchandise, is now too dear to give much profit. Recommends Tatta, in Sind, as a good place for trade. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1 <em>p. Received May</em> [ ].)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert [Young] at Agra to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">April</span> 26, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 115).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Company is put to much needless expense by Mr. Hughes. ‘Hee keepes 25 peones ; [          ] of them are gillopdares [<em>see</em> p. 13], not horskeepers but fellowes which [most of?] them are ould men past labour and fitt for nothinge but ease. [I urged him?] to put away those two ould men at the newe moone, being [          ] as likewise some others as haveinge [          ] but hee [answered me?] in such disdaynfull maner as if I we[re unw]orthy to spe[ak with him, abusing?] me in such scurvie language as I neth[er          ] nor will [          ] to doe, to speake or medle with his busines.’ Again requests permission to quit the factory and repair to Surat. ‘Hee hath likewise put the Company to 50 rupp[ees] charge for a table, onely to play at g[aming?] and nowe wants nothinge but lyberty and company to ecercise that sporte. Hee keepes a horse of his own, bought of John Willowby, which horse was given John Willowby by Grewe [Guru], our ould depter, but the Company hath paid well for him and still runs upon their charge 10 or 12 rupp[ees] per month and noe use for him, for wee have a horse of the Companies and two coaches, and litle use for any of them. Hee likewise lefte a debt of 300 and odd rupp[ees] in Pattana, with a mare of 80 rupp[ees] to sell, and two or three peones to receave the debt and keepe the mare, but yett is noe retorne of men, monye, nor mare. Ten or twelve rupp[ees] a month is eaten upp theire ; seven or eight monthes ar past alreadye, and still good mony is throwne after bad. Seventy five rupp[ees] spent on a feast, besydes the apurtenances which lyes by the wales for the wormes to eate. Eighteen rupp[ees] at once given in pane<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-072" href="#sec-04-fn-072" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-072" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> to certayne <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-115" data-page="115" data-folio="76" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n114_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 76">[p. 76]</button> banyans at the feast of Wholy.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-073" href="#sec-04-fn-073" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-073" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Whollie in Indiston [Hindustān] is sastilye,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-074" href="#sec-04-fn-074" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-074" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> but methinks it was a litle to hastely given. Twenty three rupp[ees] in a shash [i. e., a turban-cloth] to Jado broker; with many other needlesse things to increase the plewerallytie of expence, which I forbare at present to reherse. . . . Had not this gallente gallupt upp an downe towne from [        ] like an umra <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-075" href="#sec-04-fn-075" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-075" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> he had had still the libertye of [        ] Companye of a double charge.’ Purchases by the Dutch. Reiterates his desire to quit Agra. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 1 <em>p. Received May</em> 22.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Edward Monnox, Richard Blyth, and John Weddell at Ormus to the Surat Factory</span>, APRIL 27, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Persia</em>, vol. i. p. 42).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘The 10th of February last past the Persians, by means of our convey, had safe arrivall upon this island of Ormus, to the number of 3,000 souldiers and piners <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-076" href="#sec-04-fn-076" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-076" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> or thereabouts; and beinge landed about two myles from the cytty marched directly theither and without any manner of resistance worth the writinge tooke the same, beinge wholly abandoned by the Portugalls, who had betaken themselves to the castle, and with them the most part of the inhabitants of the cytty, as well Moores as Christians, especially the wealthier sorte, with all theire substance that was portable. But the cytty beinge in the Persians and ours possessione, ordinance was landed from the shipps and siege laid to the castle; wherein the Persian used such dilligence by trenches and mynes that the 17th of March followinge was blowen up a part of his castle wall, but the breach proved somewhat difficulte and bad to enter; yet the Persian gave a very resolute assault thereunto, but it was soe well defended by the Portugalls that the Persians were forced for that tyme to retire. Our endeavors, both from the shore with our ordinance and other stratagems of fire, hath beene cheefely to bring destruction to his armado, which one after another (blessed be God) wee have <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-116" data-page="116" data-folio="77" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n115_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 77">[p. 77]</button> effected in such sorte that wee have seene the ruyne of five galliounes, one galley, and soe many fryggotts that wee left them destitute wholly of those helpes, eyther for their escape or other. In briefe, such hath beene the dilligence of us and our associates, and Gods blessinge thereunto, that wee brought the besieged unto such necessitie that the 22th present, beinge Easter Mundaye,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-077" href="#sec-04-fn-077" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-077" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> they surrendred themselves and castell into our hands; and such hath beene the Christianlike care of them [that] wee have not laboured only with the Persians to spare their lives but wee have alsoe fitted them with shippinge and other meanes to carry them away. Such a courtesie as this hath seldome or never beane shewed on their parts unto any of our nation, but such charitie cannot wante his reward from heaven, howbeyt they are slenderly thankfull for yt. Such was the Portugalls care to save the brasse ordinance that was in theire galliounes that they landed them all before ther shipps were ruyned, which ordynance, with those in the castle, by report beinge upwards of 200 peeces, the most of them beinge cannon, demy-cannon, whole culveringe and demy-culveringe, which will amount to a good some of monny. The certayne vallew, eyther of them or other purchase, wee cannot wright you. The cytty yealded us very little, by estimation about 12,000<em>l.</em> [<em>sic</em>] sterling. What the castle will yeald is yet unknowne, but our hopes are the voyage will prove benefitiall to our ymployers, not soe much for the present as the future, the castle and cytty beinge halfe ours and now in our possession and at our dispose. Wherefore it will necessarily require that you by your next letters doe appoint in what manner the buisines shalbe ordered and by whom; first, to send or nominate who shall remayne for cheife directour and whom to be officers and collectors of the custome house duties; and for the better advancement of the benefitt thereof, you shall doe well in our opinions to solicite the merchants of Indya and Sinde to continewe theire former trade, with promise of kinder usage from us then ever they had from the Portugall. And for the particuler benefitt of our masters wee cannot omitt to advise you to streyne yourselves to supplie <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-117" data-page="117" data-folio="78" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n116_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 78">[p. 78]</button> this place with all sorts of commodities heere vendible, and for quantitie as much as you can eyther with your present meanes or creditt procure, wherein wee for our parts will not be wantinge to bringe you meanes in contador<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-078" href="#sec-04-fn-078" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-078" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> for satisfaction.’ This will be better than investing in silk at so dear a rate. As trade has been at a standstill for two years there is a great want of commodities, and the first comer will find a good market. Besides the benefit from the Company’s own trade (custom-free), much profit will ensue from the freight of merchants’ goods and the share of the duties thereon. ‘And as God hath blessed our endeavors in procuringe you this fayre waye, soe wee hope the same God will blesse you and your endeavors, that you may safely walke therein, to the advancement of His glory and to the accomplishment of our masters longe wished for designes in theire most hopefull trade of Persia, which by noe other way nor meanes could ever have beene brought to any perfection.’ Have decided to send the <em>London</em> and another of Weddell’s ships to Surat, to be there by about the middle or 20th of September, in order to take in cargoes for England. Their men are mostly in good health. Some were killed and wounded in the operations, ‘and Mr. Stevenson, master of the <em>Doulphin</em>, dyed his naturall death aboord his shipp shortly after our arrivall to this place.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 2½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes [at Agra] to the Surat Factory</span> [ABOUT APRIL 27] 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, <em>f.</em> 116).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has already advised that by order of Āsaf Khān Rs. 10,200 have been taken from them to satisfy ‘the Syndemen’, and they themselves carried prisoners to the castle. Hutchinson and Hawkridge, with the jewels, have reached Gwalior. Have advised them to obey their instructions from Surat, and in present circumstances to return to Burhānpur. Had those instructions been less positive, they would have encouraged them to come on to Agra, as the ways are dangerous between this place and Burhānpur, ‘occationed through the [       depar]ture thence of Coroome, whose behaviour in those parts hath sett all in con[fusion] ; as also that wee hope our troubles heere cannot longe contenewe.’ ‘Though wee be under tuitione, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-118" data-page="118" data-folio="79" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n117_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 79">[p. 79]</button> yet Mr. Younge hath lyberty, both of himselfe and disposure of our howse withoute controlement or further inquisitione; and some particuler encomends of our owne have been sent us from Robert Hutchinson, which is saufly arryved without interruptione.’ The Company’s goods are still under the seals of this Governor’s officers. They are trying hard to procure permission to return to their house and have promised bribes to this end; but their main reliance is on testimony to be procured at Surat, Cambay, &amp;c. <em>P.S.</em>—The messenger from Surat was 34 days on the road. (<em>Copy. Damaged. 1 p. Received</em> [   ].)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham and Justinian Offley</span>] <span class="sc">at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 6, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 113).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have heard from Ahmadābād that the money remitted has been duly received, except part of ‘Jemseir [Beage’s]’. Have now received an order from the latter for the balance due, as also for the price of some tapestry purchased by him. (<em>Copy. Damaged. ½ p. Received May</em> 16.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham and Justinian Offley</span>] <span class="sc">at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 9, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 112).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Sultan Cossero is taken oute of his grave [and carried to?] Dillye [Delhi], beinge so ordered by the Kinge, who take[s      ]. This Prince is in Mando, and their will re[main].’ (<em>Copy. Fragment only. ½ p. Received May</em> 14.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Rastell and Council at Surat to the Masulipatam Factory</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 11, 1622 (<em>O. C.</em> 1047).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-079" href="#sec-04-fn-079" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-079" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In answer to theirs of February 26 and March 29. Refer to their former accounts of the ‘many excessive wronges’ they have suffered. ‘Such is the increased height of presente insolencie and our miserable thraldome per a trebled estate in the cuntry as that besides the inforced restitution of 23,000 lares that weare lawfully taken out of a Portinggall vessell, and 10,200 rupes in satisfaction of certaine surprised provisiones else, whose worth could not amount to 1,000 att the most, they have now full five months time detaned our <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-119" data-page="119" data-folio="80" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n118_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 80">[p. 80]</button> merchants prisoners in Agra, first in ther owne howse but now in the common castle, have imbarqued ther whole estate, and now latly have donn no lesse to our ferinds [<em>sic</em>] att Amedevares, merely and wholie for the Duches roberie, which in remote places are imputed to the Englishe, and neare porte for that both are supposed on [i. e. one] company; of whome because they are not ignorante of the others beggerie and our plentie they lay ther clames on us as best able to pay them and most fearfull to offend them, whilest on the contrarie the other, not having much to losse heare, even awes them with his threats if in the least they should mollest him; which indeed they neither have nor dare to doe, but wright both to Prince and Governor in ther behalfes, though they devulge themselves and boast ther thevery, which if any of these question, tis suffitiente answere they have order from ther Generall. Leet us in the meane time use all possible indeavour per petissions, complants, bribes and daylie soliciting for certificats to be granted from this Governor in our favours, yett nothing prevailles more then plausible words and promises unperformed; soe that wee suffer not a generall disturbance and stope of trad alone, but remaine on most perrilous tearmes, even to the hazard of all our masters affaires and estate in India, unlesse the remembrance of ther owne abroad in Arabia doe a litle restraine them. And that to only concernes the marchants only, whose interest in the eares of greate men, who are the parties bribed, and the complanants will hardly be regarded soe long as they may share of what they flece from us. Theise on would thinke weare miseries enow of themselves to outweare all patience and further sufference, yett this is not all. If the Duch receive but the least affronte from these people (as how can they possible long indure them, how [i. e. who] are the undoers of the world and spight of there teeth wilbe braving guests to those whome they have most offended), I say if once they picke but the least pretence, the next of ther mischeefe lights on this porte, a thing most manifest and purposly plotted, not soe much to there owne enriching as our masters damage per unavoydable losse of ther estate and trad both att once in this place whensoever that sceane comes to acting. Now wee had esily prevented by begining first ourselves, not as theves but as justiceres of wrongs and henderances sustaned, if our castle takers att Ormus had followed comission per retur[n]ing in time to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-120" data-page="120" data-folio="81" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n119_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 81">[p. 81]</button> have lessened our greate estate in the contry ; which nevertheless, if they from thence or the fleete from England may soe happily arive as to be here some few dayes before ther junks from Judda, that wee geet our goods downe to Swally, twill yett be in our powers to be doing, unless the Duch interceipte them before us ; which to prevent wee have sent the <em>Lion</em>, <em>Rosse</em>, and <em>Richard</em> to Mocha, for more conveniencie to be first possessores themselves ; which they have express order to attempte so soone as the junke is out of porte, but not suffer or offer the least violence or wrong in any kind, only to hould them in safe custodie for our securites here on shore, till conducted to this porte, when notwithstanding, if neither fle[et] be arived, then wee conceive not good to land a pice untell all our provided goods be delevered us, and then alsoe if of necessitie wee must prosecute our other demaunds (as for the reason alledged it cannot longer be delaied) our cases, as yourselfe have soundly apprehended, is still the same, and worse if wee continew still a residency, in soe much as wee cannot possible discerne how a dissolution for a season should be any way avoyded, nor how with savety the trad restablished without assurance contracted with the Duch att home not to doe spoyle on these people but on such competent warning or conditiones with our masters that they be not damnified. In the meane time you may perceive that in all liklihood that with the monie out of England disigned hether, and that which ther juncks must afford in satisfaction of just pretences and estate which of force must be leaft ashore, if all things sorte as projected wee shalbe soundly stored with meanes suffitient for southern supplie (whose want is soe much complaned of) and yett have a spare proportion for you, besides goods out of England, from whose proceed and our monies haelpe you may be stockt to purpose in every degree to equall with the Duch, who it semes in the Mullucos this yeare have far outstripte our masters in ther trad for want of meanes to follow it.’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This is largely the fault of the Company at home, who, thinking there would be plenty of money available from restitutions made by the Dutch, countermanded the supply of goods from Surat to Batavia. Commodities to the value of Rs. 25,594 have been provided for Sumatra, but owing to the detention of the fleet in Persia are still unshipped. ‘The fleete designed for the coast of Mallabar have doubtless per accidente or <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-121" data-page="121" data-folio="80" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n120_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 80">[p. 80]</button> merch<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">...</span> the <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">...</span> latly and put<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">...</span> on <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">...</span> of <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">...</span> be th th in <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">...</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">...</span> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-122" data-page="122" data-folio="83" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n121_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 83">[p. 83]</button> out, and yett some afferme that that alsoe is surrendered. That which most discreditts us hath benn the reccles neigligence of the master and people of the <em>Lion</em>, who notwithstanding our often and exp[ress] charge, as well per wrighting as per word of mouth, to continue a st[rong] gard on Ruy Freres person and constantly to releave the same by turnes to prevent whatsoever his intents, eyther escape or otherwise, they suffered both him and three more of his company with a skife in the night to worke ther owne libertie ; <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-080" href="#sec-04-fn-080" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-080" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> which, besides the disgrace, hath prevented us the meanes of our pore peoples redemtion from out that barberous ussage wherof your letters make mention. And for the rest whome they call captaines, being men of small reconing, and forasmuch as wee wanted conveighance to send them to the southwards, and that Ruy Frere per letters sence his departure hath pawned his honnor uppon the releace to procure and send us ther Vize Royes mandate for all our peoples inlargmente, wee have given them also ther liberties to oblidge them the more to performance.’ Their intentions as to coercing the natives should be kept secret, especially from the Dutch. Hope to dispatch a ship and a pinnace to the southwards in September or October. (<em>Copy.</em> 3½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Fursland and Council at Batavia to the Pulicat Factory, May</span> 12, 1622 (<em>O. C.</em> 1048).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-081" href="#sec-04-fn-081" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-081" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last on April 25 [<em>not extant</em>] by a Dutch frigate. ‘Sence which time their Generall here hath given us for answere concerning the division of the cloth that they will make no other division then proportionally according to the capitall which each shall invest ; or wee may take our choice and invest ours soe well as [we] can without making them acquanted. With this ther answere wee must rest satisfied untell wee may heare from you what prejudice wee have suffered by this course, for if your capitall be all invested it is att present no henderance to us, but if you <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-123" data-page="123" data-folio="84" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n122_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 84">[p. 84]</button> came short and that you are not fully furnished, or in this next investmente should be disapoynted and cannot gett in such sorts as you desire, then you must protest against them for all cost and dammages which may be sustaned by these ther indirecte dealings, for it is contrarie to reason that wee should pay the halfe of the charge and cannot have one quarter the parte of trad ; and wee, when you have enformed us of the inconveniencies which may accrew by such division, will accordinglie proceed against the Generall by protest, and it may be wholie removing from thence. But ther reffering it to our choice for the investing of our monies soe well as wee can without making them acquanted, wee conceive it not good for us yett to pute the same in practize, least in ther mallicious humores they by one cuning trick or other doe us a mischeefe unexpected in raysing the price of cloth or making the sorts worse then theres, and many the like tricks which they may use ; but if you find that you may pass your businesse currantly without making them acquanted as now you doe, uppon your answere wee will give you order to proceed in your affares accordingly. For building of a howse the Generall tells us hee will give order that you shall have a place appointed to build a warehowse and lodging rome suffitiente for your accommodation such as yourselfe in reason shall find fitting. And for the deniing of us to shipp those balles which you had readie, hee answeres us with the same fruvolous excuse that they answered you withall ther ; that wee demaunded no such thing of him heare, and hereafter if wee desire it here of him he will accomodate us in any of ther shipps soe far as conveniently they may. Thus are wee allwayes answered with fare words when wee desire anything which may pleasure us, but when it comes to performance they wilbe sure by on tricke or other to prevente us. In the building of your howse wee make accompte you will doe nothing untell you have better supplie of meanes from us ; but in the meane time you may see what place they will appointe you now uppon the Generalls advice and promise, and writte whether it will be suffitiente and conveniente for you or not. It shall [may ?] be you shall find [them ?] as frindly now againe as they weare att your first entertainmente, for wee suppose the Generalls spleene is now quallified ; but if you find no alteration then you must resolve to beare all <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-124" data-page="124" data-folio="85" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n123_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 85">[p. 85]</button> with patience. Wee have now made readie this shipp, the <em>Unetie</em>, to send her unto you, and have laden abord on her five chests of monie, containing 20,000 rialls of eight which wee have consigned unto you. To furnish you with this capitall wee left ourselves soe bare of monie that if supplie come not speedily out of England wee shall be put to an exigente for the defraing our charge. For Muslupatam wee have not sent any mony att all; soe that excepte they have made sale of those commodities which they had remaning wee can expecte nothing to be donn for our next yeares supplie of goods from thence.' The money sent should be employed in accordance with previous instructions. Will furnish a further supply towards the end of the year if possible. The <em>Unity</em> should be sent on to Masulipatam without delay. 'Mr. Methwold comes away uppon this shipp, and wee have appointed Mr. Duke to be our Agente in his place, with whome you are to keepe like respondence as you have with his predisseccor. Mr. Dod, whome wee have sent marchant uppon this shipp, is to be imployed att Muslapatam according as wee have advised them concerning him. If in the shorte tyme of this shipps stay ther with you you can provide 10 or 15 man slaves, wee would have you to buy soe many and send them unto us uppon this shipp; but if soe many cannot be procured in soe shorte time then soe manie as you can. Lett them be from the age of 16, 20, or 22 yeares oulde, and of able bodies fitt to doe labour.' Have sent them a runlet of sack, but are themselves so short of provisions that they could only spare the <em>Unity</em> three hogsheads of meat. Methwold has been authorized to draw upon the Pulicat factory for money to clear his cargo, but they hope this will not be necessary. (<em>Copy. 2 pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 14, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 113).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Acknowledge receipt of money forwarded. Unless the Governor will give way and allow rupees to pass current at the same rate as in Surat, there will be a general stoppage of trade. Shortage in gunny sent. Request a list of goods to be provided at this place. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received May 16.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-125"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="125" data-folio="86" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n124_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 86">[p. 86]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2">[<span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham</span>, <span class="sc">Justinian Offley</span>, <span class="sc">and John Clark</span>] <span class="sc">at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 15, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 115).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Leachland and Clark arrived on the 11th. Are sending to Surat by the former all their remaining goods. Hope shortly to make an end of ‘those falce demands of Swares . . . without hazerdinge ether bodye or goods, though with some expence of charges’. Perceive that the factors at Agra are ‘in mecerye and litle hope (as you write) for their libertye. God send them and all us free out of this basse countrye, where is soe litle justice in the Kinge and Prince’. Send by the bearers bills for 16,000 mahmūdīs; will themselves bring down the rest as soon as possible. Rs. 150 given to Leachland for expenses. The feathers they wrote for were sold to the Khānkhānān. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ¾ <em>p. Received May 22.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Halstead and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 16, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 114).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forward bills of exchange for Rs. 5,985, equivalent to 14,000 mahmūdīs. They are trying to sell their lead in one batch. English tin is priced at Rs. 12 per maund, and Achin tin at Rs. 15; but no demand at present. One of them will go to Dholka shortly to procure and dispatch some serijas. Their own ‘lynens’ come back but slowly from the washers. <em>P.S.</em>—Request instructions whether they shall visit the new Governor who they hear is coming. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received May 20.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham</span>, <span class="sc">John Leachland</span>, <span class="sc">Justinian Offley</span>, <span class="sc">John Clark</span>, <span class="sc">and Thomas Hawkridge at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 17, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 116).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Return of Hutchinson and Hawkridge. The former was too weak to continue his journey to Surat: so Leachland, Clark, Offley, and Hawkridge will take down the jewels, &amp;c., Bangham and Hutchinson following as soon as they can wind up affairs. <em>P.S.</em>—Heard last night that ‘our frinds in Agra have procured their owne howse for their persones, and are their kept with a good guard over them; and a new Governour comeinge called Etewar <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-126" data-page="126" data-folio="87" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n125_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 87">[p. 87]</button> Canne’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-082" href="#sec-04-fn-082" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-082" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Have paid Rs. 104 p[ice] 43 to Offley, and Rs. 182 to Hawkridge, on account of their salaries for the present year; and have repaid to Clark Rs. 64 p[ice] 34 expended on the way. (<em>Copy. Damaged. <span class="sc">¾</span> p.
