Received May 23.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, May</span> [21?], 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 114).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received the list of goods to be provided here, and trust to procure them without difficulty, except perhaps the fine narrow baftas, of which there are but few about. Detail the steps they have taken. Send a note of what the Dutch have already bought. The latter are still buying narrow baftas; and some of the brokers are also purchasing on behalf of Surat merchants for Achin and the Red Sea; all these ‘are lets to us’. The question of the rupees passing here at the same rate as at Surat is still unsettled, for the Governor is with the Rājā;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-083" href="#sec-04-fn-083" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-083" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> the trouble is caused by the Governor’s officials refusing to take rupees except at a discount. Beg in the meantime that any mahmūdīs available at Surat may be sent to them. In any case they need a supply of cash, and also of gunny. ‘[We] perceave you have vizeted the Ra[ja? who is] to be Governowr of Amada[vad] and have g[iven him a] present of good worth; who [hath] given you good words and pleasinge pro[mises]. . . . Wee are glad Rostom Chon, that ould curr so falce [to his] promise, is excluded from that place and goverment.’ (<em>Copy. Damaged. 1½ pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham and Robert Hutchinson at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory, May 23, 1622</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 117).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On May 20 Leachland and the rest departed for Surat with the jewels and all the goods remaining in the factory. The jewels they carry on their persons, ‘for that a man may better escape the hands of knaves then laden cartes.’ Hope to hear of their safe arrival, and also of the payment of the bills of exchange previously <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-127" data-page="127" data-folio="88" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n126_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 88">[p. 88]</button> sent. There is ‘this daye flyeinge newes of the Princes retorne to this place.’ (<em>Copy. Damaged. <span class="sc">½ p. Received May</span> 31.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill [at Broach] to the Surat Factory, May</span> 29, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 117).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Acknowledge receipt of 25,400 mahmūdīs. Return some to be exchanged. Progress of their investments. The Governor has gone to Ahmadābād with the Rājā; so nothing can be done yet about the rupees. (<em>Copy. Damaged. ¾ p. Received May</em> 30.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Halstead and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, June</span> 5, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 117).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Wickarmagiit is become sencible of our wrongfull [             ]. Hee arryved heere the 24th ultimo about 3 cloke in the [afternoon].’ They visited him on the 26th with a small present. Agga Salie [<em>see p.</em> 44] departed the day following the receipt of the Surat letter, and they had no opportunity of asking him for a favourable letter to his master; but 'hees our great [enem]ie (as wee thinke), conseveinge wee have or will spoyle the trade [of Cam]baya by spoylinge and fearinge the Portugalls and others, and consecque[ntly] deminish the meanes and other proffitts of him and his master; and hath with many banyans of his cittie in five or six dayes of their beinge heere severall tymes complayned to the Raja of us; who, as wee here, gave them alwayse eyther slight or crabed answere; soe they retorned without expected justice upon us (lyinge, slanderouse rogues) as they came. Two or three dayes after the receipt of yours mentioned, wee the second tyme visated the Raja, desyringe his lycence to transporte (notwithstandinge Mussuff Cons command to the contrary) our goods from hence and Dowlka; that the screits<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-084" href="#sec-04-fn-084" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-084" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> might be retorned, and so the suertyes for our personall apperence cleared; that hee would give us the aradash [<em>see</em> p. 13] promysed you to his master in our behalves, and lastly that hee would write Agga Salie that hee hath don us greate wronge in fatheringe the Dutches roberyes upon us, therefore that hee should doe well to give his master beter information and cause our b[eter] usage in Agra; in all which hee <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-128" data-page="128" data-folio="89" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n127_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 89">[p. 89]</button> hath promised us our desyres, haveinge al[ready ordered we] may lade and carrye what goods hence wee please, and wee dayly and ho[urly expect from] him a perwanna to the Governour of Dowlka to procure the same licen[ce there]'. He has, however, deferred writing to Āghā Sālih. They had previously applied to 'Mussuf Can' for leave to transport their goods and also for the return of the 'scripte', as he was about to depart for his 'prigonie' [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 170]. He refused both requests, whereupon, as already mentioned, they appealed to the Rājā, who granted the desired permission, while as for the 'scripte' he forced 'Mussuf Can' to leave it behind him ('to his much vexacion') and they hope for its early delivery. They have not yet attempted to recover the 23,000 'larres', but trust to obtain justice from the Rājā. Have now dispatched a quantity of goods in carts to Broach for Surat. Yesterday sold 570 maunds of lead at Rs. 3¾ per maund (payable in two months), and hope to dispose of the rest very shortly. In three or four days all the 'lynens' will have been received, and no time shall be lost in forwarding them. They have a thousand pieces still in the hands of the washers, who are delayed by the fact that the 'wells and tanks are most drye'. The goods from Dholka will be sent immediately the licence is received. <em>P.S.</em>—In spite of his promises the Rājā has refused the licence for Dholka. After waiting all the forenoon they were allowed to speak to him, when he at once demanded a present. They replied that they had none in the kingdom and should not have until the next fleet arrived ; whereupon he said that until a 'toffa' [<em>tuhfa</em>, 'a present'] came he would dispatch no business. They know not what to think of this sudden change. Have written to the brokers at Dholka to send or bring the goods hither if they can get permission to do so. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 2½ <em>pp. Received June</em> 11.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert [Hughes] at Agra to the Surat Factory</span>, JUNE 5, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 121).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Young departed for Surat on May 14. Forwards a letter from Willoughby, announcing his arrival at the royal camp on April 23, relating his negotiations with Āsaf Khān, 'and in conclutione, howe hee had procured both us and the Companies goods releace, by vertue of three severall perwannas given him by the Nabob, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-129" data-page="129" data-folio="90" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n128_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 90">[p. 90]</button> to the Governour and his Callifa [<em>see</em> p. 11] heere in Agra; upon deleverye wherof wee weare incontenently freed, and all our goods and monyes, save the 10,200 rupp[ees] alloted the Syndemen, the which Assuff Chon, as you may perceave by the copie of his perwanna to Callifa, doth avere to be justice.’ This money has long since been paid to the claimants, except Rs. 2,200, retained until the receipt of further proof from ‘Synda’. As soon as they were released (which was May 26), they commenced their preparations for dissolving the factory, in obedience to the orders received from Surat. They decided to send away the goods provided this year by Young in and about Samāna, the remains of last year’s indigo, and such jewels as were unsold. Carts were hired to take them to Burhānpur, at Rs. 3 per maund (which is one rupee higher than the usual rate); and it was settled that Mr. Parker should go in charge of the caravan. ‘Yesterday he made his first muzell [stage: <em>see previous volume</em>, p. 74], in company of 60 carts more and the lesker [<em>see</em> p. 12] of Chon Azums soone[s?] mahalle,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-085" href="#sec-04-fn-085" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-085" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> now goeinge for Brampore, whose company hee hath attended these four dayes for the better securytie of the wayes betwene this place and Guallia [Gwalior], where the two Princes, Coroome and Sheriars, people are att varyence about Doulpore [Dholpur] jaggerie [jāgīr: <em>see previous volume</em>, p. 90], and causeth people to travill with the more securytie, for feare of the gaurds, who have committed [            ] outrage aboute those parts.’ The goods fill eleven carts. The carters have been advanced the amount due to them, less Rs. 55 to be paid on their arrival at Burhānpur. ‘[There] goeth with the carts 27 Jutts,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-086" href="#sec-04-fn-086" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-086" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> etc. for their safer passadge.’ Parker has received Rs. 300 for expenses on the way. He is also taking to Burhānpur the bill for Rs. 2,000 ‘appertayneinge to Herpershade’. Will endeavour to dispose of the ivory remaining, though buyers are hard to find for so large a quantity. Will not invest any money pending further instructions. His own opinion is ‘that you persist in your intents to breake up trade for this yeare’ and that no <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-130" data-page="130" data-folio="91" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n129_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 91">[p. 91]</button> investments be made at Agra this season, for the goods could not arrive at Surat till December, and ‘your resolucion takeing effect at the Barr of Surratt at September or October maye hazard the losse, or at least the surprisall of such goods’. Requests instructions on this point by the bearer ; ‘as also what course Mr. Willowby shall take ; untill when I have writen him to keepe att the lesker to hould Assuff Chon in expectacion of our comeinge to him with the jewells, wherof you will perceave Mr. Willowby hath given him good hopes, but was not suffetient to anymate us to the keepinge of the 1,000<em>l.</em> jewell here, in respecte of your expresse order to send it downe to Surratt by the first saufe oppertunytie.’ Has also sent him all the bills of the debts outstanding at court, in order that, while waiting, he may collect as many as possible. ‘The lesker is still at Cashmeire, and their doth stay till after the raynes. Yf Mr. Willowbys resydent be thought so remote as not with securytie to repayre for Surratt before your project against these people take effect, you maye order him (yf you please) to secure himselfe by way of Pertia, which is nearer now to his aboad.’ Sends copies of Āsaf Khān’s orders, and of ‘the cubez taken under the Cazes Chope<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-087" href="#sec-04-fn-087" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-087" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> for the monyes Assuff Chon ordered these Governours to pay the Synde merchants out of our cash then under their tuitione’ ; also lists of goods sent by Mr. Parker, &amp;c. The certificates lately asked for in order to effect their release will not now be needed. <em>P.S.</em>—Has failed to procure the copy of Āsaf Khān’s [orders ?]. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 2¾ <em>pp. Received June</em> 30.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">June</span> 10, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 112).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Some tin is coming from Ahmadābād ; will send it on immediately it arrives. (<em>Copy. Fragment only.</em> ¼ <em>p. Received June</em> 11.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Parker at NarwĀr to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">June</span> 12, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 123).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">No doubt they have heard of the release of Hughes and himself and of his dispatch with a quantity of goods. Has got thus far on <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-131" data-page="131" data-folio="92" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n130_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 92">[p. 92]</button> his journey in safety. Yesterday met two ‘pattamars’ [foot-posts] bringing letters from Surat, which he opened and read, and then sent on to Agra. Also learnt by a private letter from Young that the factors at Burhānpur were shortly to quit that place. Fears that this will hinder his procuring carts and men there, and possibly he may be obliged to remain until the rains are over. Begs they will send him instructions. Unless he can recover some of the debts at Burhānpur, he will probably have to draw upon them for money to defray his expenses. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received July</em> 7.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin and George Pike at Broach to the Surat Factory, June</span> [12], 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, ff. 112, 119).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The tin has arrived, and they are now endeavouring to procure carts to take it on to Surat. The rains, however, have made the ways impassable; so they send the bearer to learn what is to be done. (<em>Copy. ¼ p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin and George Pike at Broach to the Surat Factory, June</span> [13?], 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 118).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Progress of their business. Forward a letter received from Baroda, offering a parcel of baftas in payment of a debt. The goods [from Ahmadābād ?] cannot be sent on, because the roads are impassable owing to the rains. Request instructions. (<em>Copy. Much damaged. 1¼ pp. Received June</em> [   ].)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, June</span> 14, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, ff. 119, 112).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received the 31,000 mahmūdīs and credited them to Surat. Return some to be exchanged. Progress of their investments. The tin was dispatched yesterday. (<em>Copy. Much damaged. ¾ p. Received June 15.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Halstead and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, June</span> 18, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 120).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘The 10th present we vizeted the Raja and acquented him [                    ] that Isack Beage refused to make good to you his debt; where[upon he] gave order that wee should be presently <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-132" data-page="132" data-folio="93" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n131_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 93">[p. 93]</button> paid, abatinge, accordinge to [cus]tome, 10 per cent and three p[aisas] per rupp[ee] for differrence betwixt your rupp[ee] and ours. This he spacke with his owne mouth ; soe wee thought best take mony, which wee had withoute delaye payd us, giveing his officers about rupp[ees] 2.’ The rest of the lead has been sold. Goods have been received from Dholka and will be dispatched to-morrow. (<em>Copy. Damaged. <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> p. Received June 25.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, June 19, 1622</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 120).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A parcel of baftas received from Baroda; the quality proves satisfactory, and they recommend a further purchase. The markets have been slack for the last three or four days, ‘by reason of the Mores Ede’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-088" href="#sec-04-fn-088" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-088" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Only 9 corge yet received of ‘Daboyes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-089" href="#sec-04-fn-089" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-089" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> cloth’; the brokers are slow in procuring it, and the supply is likely to prove deficient. As requested, have sent down five ship carpenters ; the chief is to be paid one mahmūdī a day, and the rest three-quarters of a mahmūdī each. Have advanced them 25 mahmūdīs. ‘Cangee Park [Kānojī Pārakh] promysed us three others, but the Cotwall would not suffer them to departe. Their is wante of such men here, beinge but a fewe of them. They serve as well for howse buildinge as for shippinge.’ Send a statement of account. The piece of red broadcloth is not included, as the Governor has refused it. Request instructions for its disposal. (<em>Copy. Damaged. 1 p. Received June 20.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes at Agra to the Surat Factory, June 20, 1622</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 124).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forwards copy of a letter from Willoughby. Has received theirs of May 2, enclosing the desired certificate, for which he thanks them. Regrets that they have misconstrued his previous letters. Has sent the certificate on to the royal camp to show Āsaf Khān their innocence. Answers their criticisms on his action in regard to the goods under Young’s charge. The doings of their fleet <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-133" data-page="133" data-folio="94" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n132_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 94">[p. 94]</button> at Ormus are current talk here, ‘butt howe the Kinge disgests itt I knowe nott.’ ‘Most sencable unto this Kinge hath itt alreadey bine of the Persians invadinge Candahar. Intelligence itt [<em>sic</em>] is devolged thatt the Jehangers people hath lost a feelde ; and now the Kinge hath made Sultan Sherriare 30,000 horse, hath furnished him with a crower [Hind. <em>kror</em>, ‘a hundred lakhs’] of silver, whereof 50 lackes is alreadey gonn and att presentt 50 lacks more dispeedinge hence, besides 70 lackes in gowld, to mantayne thatt moheeme [Hind. <em>muhimm</em>, ‘an enterprise’] and appoynted him a selested [selected ?] armey ; to accomplish which the better he hath recaled all his umrawes [<em>see</em> p. 76] and servants with Corome, purposinge himselfe to sallye out of Cashiemere for Lahore the 25th of the next monnth Rammazan. Hee hath cut of the munsab <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-090" href="#sec-04-fn-090" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-090" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> of both our Governor[s ?] heere in Agra a thousand horce eache, for nott aydinge Sultan Sherryears people against Corrome in their differance about Doulpoore jeggier. [And ?] this daye is heere aryved Sultan [Khus]roues taboots <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-091" href="#sec-04-fn-091" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-091" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> from Brampore, [which to-] morrowe is to bee dispeeded to H[elobass ? <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-092" href="#sec-04-fn-092" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-092" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>] there to bee intered by his mother [           ].’ Cannot find customers for his ivory and other goods. ‘The Dutch doe nothinge [in] investments neither, nor have had ought to sell all this yeare, save a little rotten sandall, bengamin [benzoin] and camphier, which they have putt of att such base rates as will little encourage there future bringinge up the licke comodities.’ A mistake in last year’s accounts. Recovery of a debt. Amount due from Mr. Biddulph. Answers Young’s accusations. The latter charges him with employing 25 peons at Rs. 4 per month ; the truth is that the whole staff, including washers, washerman, and ‘hallolcore’ [Hind. <em>halālkhor</em>, ‘a sweeper’], costs only Rs. 54 <sup>3</sup>⁄<sub>4</sub> the month. As for ‘makinge a table for the houses use’, he is wrong both as to its quality and cost, as Mr. Parker can testify. Keeps one horse, and one only. As for the coach, one of the oxen died and the other went lame and had to be sold ; ‘which <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-134" data-page="134" data-folio="95" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n133_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 95">[p. 95]</button> is all the proceede of his caveluriree’ [<em>see</em> p. 45]. It is true a man was left at Patna to recover a debt and sell a mare, and that owing to the mare falling lame it could not be sold for some months; but in the end it was got rid of at cost price and the debt was duly recovered. As for the 75 rupees spent on a feast, 57 would have been more correct, and that is not so much as his predecessors have spent on a like occasion, which was ‘in feastinge the Cristians att Cristmas’. The shash presented to the broker was in accordance with orders from Surat ‘to give him dellassa’ [Hind. <em>dilāsā</em>, ‘encouragement’] and was cheaper than presenting him with a hundred rupees. Animadverts in turn upon Young’s ‘base and envious dispositionn’; considers, however, ‘the truth a suffitientt buck[ler] to defend his boults,’ and is confident they will judge him fairly. Believes his household expenditure will turn out to be twenty per cent less than that of former years. (<em>Copy.</em> 5½ <em>pp. Received July</em> 15.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">NICHOLAS BANGHAM AT BURHĀNPUR TO THE SURAT FACTORY, JUNE 23, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 121).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forwards a letter received yesterday from Agra, from which it appears that the factors are now at liberty; also that Parker is on his way from that place with eleven cartloads of goods. Has therefore postponed his own departure, and will endeavour to arrange for housing the goods till the rains are over, as transport cannot be obtained here. Young arrived on June 1 and intends to start for Surat on the 25th, ‘if the weather will give him leave.’ <em>P.S.</em>—Parker will bring a bill of ‘Rampashad’ for Rs. 2,000, which cannot, however, be realized till after the rains or the next harvest. Requests instructions. ‘Hee will give us a bratte [<em>see</em> p. 67] upon some aldeas [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 86] for the monye yf you thinke good to accept it soe.’ (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received June</em> 30.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">JOHN PARKER AT ‘VENTERA’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-093" href="#sec-04-fn-093" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-093" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> TO THE SURAT FACTORY, JUNE 24, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 127).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Having an opportunity of sending by ‘the Hollenders casside’ [<em>see</em> p. 31], he thinks it well to advise them of his progress. The <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-135" data-page="135" data-folio="96" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n134_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 96">[p. 96]</button> rains have so swollen the rivers that in some cases the carts could not pass, and he has been forced to await their subsidence. Yesterday he had to lie in the woods, and the ways are so bad that he is afraid he will hardly reach his resting place to-day. ' I purposed to have come Handia<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-094" href="#sec-04-fn-094" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-094" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> waye; butt here I understand thatt of late the Ragia of the cuntry, whereas formerly hee only tooke custom, now robeth marchantts and passengers of whatt hee findeth with them. Within these eight dayes a marchantt was robed of eight cammells laden with goods, himself and three men killed. Soe thatt, exceptt I can have better assurance of the waye then yett I have, I intend to goe by waye of Madoe [Māndū?], though itt bee six dayes aboutt.' (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Halstead and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, June</span> 25, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, <em>f.</em> 122).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The dispatch of their goods has been prevented by the defection of the carters, who ‘took them to their heeles and runn awaye (beinge of Barrega<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-095" href="#sec-04-fn-095" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-095" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and Dowlka)’. They were fetched back, but still refuse to undertake the job, affirming that the rain has made the ways utterly impassable for vehicles. Are forced therefore to await instructions. Oxen or camel transport will not do, as the goods would get damaged by wet; and, besides the trouble of repacking, ‘cartage upon oxen now is more dearer (as wee are informed) then uppon carts.’ Have received their instructions to remit to Surat the money realized by the sale of their lead, but find it difficult to procure the necessary bills. The merchants object to being obliged to pay in mahmūdīs at Surat, and in future will only consent ‘to satisfye you in current monye of the bazar’. The Governor of Cambay has ‘caused our brokers their to write heather that now he understands wee [are] blamlesse and the Dutch in the faulte, hee hath advised his master as much; and if wele come; and doe busynes as formerly, his cittye is ours, with other like complements’. In answer they expressed their <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-136" data-page="136" data-folio="97" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n135_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 97">[p. 97]</button> satisfaction that he now knew the truth, but added that they had been completely exonerated by a certificate from the Surat authorities. He replied that if they would send him the certificate, ‘hele be readye to chope [<em>see</em> p. 91] it also, and send us the letter of favour to his master which wee desyre.’ They intend to adopt this course, though ‘whether hee writeth sencerly or like a knave God knoweth’. Hope the report is true that their friends in Agra have been set at liberty. As soon as a fit opportunity occurs they will endeavour to obtain justice from the Rājā about the 23,000 lārīs, and their other demands. A merchant of this city came yesterday to the Rājā to complain of the English factors at Surat, who, after accepting an offer from his servant for a parcel of vermilion and taking the money, changed their minds, refused to deliver the goods, and repaid the money. Two of their native friends prevented this man from making his application to the Rājā, at the same time telling the English broker to inform his employers and get the matter put right. Beg them to avoid all cause for complaint to the authorities. <em>P.S.</em>—Enclose bills for Rs. 6,000, at Rs. 42 <sup>5</sup>⁄<sub>8</sub> per hundred mahmūdīs. (<em>Copy.</em> 2 <em>pp. Received June</em> 30.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, June</span> 29, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 120).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received their bills of exchange for 8,000 mahmūdīs, and will now pay for the baftas received from Baroda. Progress of their investments. Only two carpenters went after all. Cannot get more, as they are not permitted to leave. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> ½ <em>p. Received June</em> 30.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Methwold, Matthew Duke, and Francis Futter at Masulipatam to the Surat Factory, June</span> 30, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 127).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On June 22 received theirs of May 22, to which they now reply. Have, however, little to add to what they wrote on June 17. Are glad to hear of their better treatment by the native officials. ‘Concerning Signor Vishneitt,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-096" href="#sec-04-fn-096" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-096" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> whose intended employmentt wee <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-137" data-page="137" data-folio="98" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n136_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 98">[p. 98]</button> the rather advised you to conferme [the] reporte of a treatey of our masters with the Dutch to enter [?] jointlye in the ample prosecution of that Persiann trade; yf hee bee otherwise designed, and thatt all such theire substances [     ] shewes of procedinge accordinge to agreementt bee dilv[          ] are nott further endamaged then in wry[ting] over the reporte; nor can yett bee per[swaded] thatt hee himselfe, nor those which sentt [him] hence, knowes any otherwise then wee [          ]. The certainty of the surpryse of Ormose, espetially the castell, workes some admyratione in such as have seene thatt place and would seeme to knowe itts strength.' 'The yett defered vengance for Sultan Cosheroees unnaturall fratricide shall undoubtedly fall heavey uppon the bloody abettres, whoe may conceale itt from thatt vicekinge Mogall, per the distance of place and connivency of freinds, butt there is an omnesientt power, the Kinge of Kinges, thatt never leaves unpunnished the unnaturall and treacherous effusion of inocentt blud.' (<em>Copy. 1½ pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, July</span> 6, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 124).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Bills of exchange received and accepted. According to instructions they have sent 2,000 mahmūdīs to Baroda, for investment in baftas, promising more money if it can be employed. The cost of the remittance was ¾ mahmūdī per hundred. Progress of their investments. They are rather hindered in their purchases by the number of buyers, including the Dutch and some brokers who are purchasing cotton goods for Achin. Were forced to pay half customs on the 14 chests of tin 'that past this waye', as 'it came not within the tyme of ther order sett for goods to passe free that before paid costome'. The expense of this, and of cartage, &amp;c., to Surat, was 51 m[ahmūdīs] 2 [pice?] 9 'docres' [<em>see</em> p. 28], for which credit should be given them in the Surat books. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received July 7.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-138"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="138" data-folio="99" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n137_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 99">[p. 99]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">George Pike at Broach to the Surat Factory</span>, July 7, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 128).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Requests permission to go to England for the purpose of recovering a legacy. His term of service is not yet expired; but if the Company require it, he will return to India and complete the period. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham and Robert Hutchinson at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory</span>, July 7, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 129).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">No news yet of Mr. Parker. Fear he is detained on the way by the rains. ‘Heere is fresh newes thatt Cann[canna] is to goe for Mandoe speedely, and the Prince to pro[ceed] towards his father after the raynes; soe thatt this p[lace] is like to bee left very poorly furnyshed. The diff[erences] betw[e]ne the two brothers Crome and Shadyare [<em>sic</em>] h[ave] cost much bludshed on both parteyes, Corome havinge sentt a fresh supply of men to procecute the sivell discord. The Kinge of Persia, as reportt [say]eth, hath made an inrode into the Mogolls terytory on [?] this side Candahar, and hath sentt another army to take Tatta in Sindee; which yf so, itt will be[gin a] greate dell of disturbance in this cunt[ry of] all sorts of people, this Kinge havinge called from hence all his cheefe souldiers to supploye those places.’ (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Fursland and Council at Batavia to Thomas Mills at Pulicat</span>, July 10, 1622 (<em>O. C.</em> 1058).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-097" href="#sec-04-fn-097" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-097" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Trial</em> [from England] has been wrecked with the loss of nearly 100 lives; and with this, and the delay of the fleet from Surat, they are in a miserable case. The Dutch have also lost the <em>Eendracht</em> near Amboyna. Mills must not expect any further supply this year. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to the Masulipatam Factory</span>, July 10, 1622 (<em>O. C.</em> 1059).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">[<em>Portion missing.</em>] The ‘Matram’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-098" href="#sec-04-fn-098" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-098" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> has sacked Sukadana, and both English and Dutch have lost heavily. Ships recently arrived <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-139" data-page="139" data-folio="100" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n138_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 100">[p. 100]</button> from Holland bring news of an English fleet about to start for Batavia. They are to keep within their means and not borrow, and the Pulicat Factory should be instructed to do likewise. State of trade in the factories to the eastwards. The goods from Pulicat yield less profit than those of the same kind brought from other places. (<em>qy. 1½ pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Halstead and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory</span>, July 11, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 129).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In obedience to their instructions, they have solicited from the Governor of Cambaya 'his letter to his master in our behalfes and his scriptt for our quiatt trade there'; their letters, however, have failed to draw any satisfactory response, and so Halstead intends to go there himself as soon as the roads are passable. Delivered to the Rājā the letter from the Governor of Surat; he promised to write to the Prince as they desired, but intimated that he expected 'store of toffa' [<em>see</em> p. 89] when the ships arrived. He also promised a parwāna to the Governor of Cambaya, but they cannot get it from him. Intend to send down by the caravan the remainder of their English goods. (<em>Copy. 1¾ pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same</span>, July 15, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 130).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Theirs of the 5th received. Cotton goods are being prepared accordingly. The Rājā has at last given them a parwāna to Cambaya ('with continuall remembrance of toffa') and Halstead proceeds thither to-morrow. The Rājā's letter to the Prince is not yet forthcoming. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory</span>, July 16, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 130).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Baftas received from their broker at Baroda. Are also daily expecting cotton cloth from 'Duboyes' [<em>see</em> p. 93]. Would be glad of a further remittance of money. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received July 17.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-140"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="140" data-folio="101" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n139_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 101">[p. 101]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">GEORGE PIKE AT SURAT</span> [<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 129).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">1622 (Ibid., f. 128)</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">value</span> of 31,000 mahmūdīs. <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Requests permission</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">1/2 p.)</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ing a legacy. His term</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Company may be his debtor</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">period. (Copy. 1/2 p.)</span></p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">NICHOLAS BANGHAM</span> <span class="sc">AND Council of Surat</span>, <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">July 26,</span> [<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 35).</h3><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">and another at Bur</span><span class="sc">hānpur to the Surat Council</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">(Ibid., p. 36).</span></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">No news yet of the deer; cannot solicit the Nabob</span>'s agent for satisfaction of the <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">rains. There is no news of the Agra caravan</span> by his men<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-099" href="#sec-04-fn-099" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-099" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> being for <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Mandlespur, pretending that they will not</span> accept the prime cost and after the <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">rains according to promise. Compensation</span> for the losses of private men, forty-eight. The remainder of the goods in the <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">junk seized by the English and</span> and Shahpur [is being sold?]. The money <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">amounting to</span> Rs. 3,000 shall be divided by the Commodore <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">having sent assistance?]. Until</span> all claimants are satisfied shall go discord. The Governor <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">of this place says that</span> it is to be insisted upon as a prior inrode into the king's country <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">or else all is lost]</span> and should if possible be procured sent and <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">has received answers from the Khan-khā</span>nā, both 'to prevent all subtill delayes be[gin to] <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">grow into</span> [and to secure goods from being plundered] by cartasses<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-100" href="#sec-04-fn-100" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-100" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> for their shipps at sea, of people. <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">The Khan-khānān</span> [and to detain the] money for disposure therof before the soldiers <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">are on</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ships go for Persia,</span> that the Guzeratt juncks shall come to actinge'.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">[EDWARD HEYWAR</span>D AT CAMBAY<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-101" href="#sec-04-fn-101" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-101" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> TO THE SURAT FACTORY, <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">July 26, 1622] (Brit.</span> <em>Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 131).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Had an audience with the Governor, but</span> with the Governor, but without much success. <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Money cannot be sent; they were</span> told that without a large 'peskesh [<em>see</em> p. 10] <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">from the Prince no progress can be made. The</span> Governor cannot contradict what he has already <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">decreed, and he will do nothing unless he is expecting to</span> hear shortly from the Prince <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">in their business. No news yet of the Prince</span> in favour of the English. 'In the mean tyme <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">he seems very friendly,</span> to come or goe and doe what wee please.' Hal<span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">ālkhors</span> to Ahmadābād to-morrow. 'To assure our goods <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">at Cambay from being plundered,</span> they (as the tymes is, 2 or 2 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> per cent) in our owne <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">names, to send them by boats? But</span> itt will bee difficuld, except they could bee readey by <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the middle of August, so as to depart in</span> September, before any Portugalls or Mallebars looke <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">abroad to seize them or other-</span>outts.' (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received July 28.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-141"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="141" data-folio="102" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n140_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 102">[p. 102]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">LICAT TO THE</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">Factory</span></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">see p. 83].</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">our nvestments.</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">have not only</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">have given credit</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">particularly</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">proceedings of the</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">that the Holthey</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the authoritye</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">which tyme</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the Dutch for</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the ducats.</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">would have</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">agreement on</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">better to allow</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the Fort</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">shall more</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">concerning</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the worst</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">were</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">and so</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">we</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">that</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">is</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">and</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">in</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">the</span> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-142" data-page="142" data-folio="103" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n141_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 103">[p. 103]</button> the vendor; this makes the latter very careful. Some of the colours are dark, but that is set down to the brackish nature of the water here. The packing has been done with great care, and in accordance with the advice of the natives. Note that, in response to their statement that 50,000 rials could be invested here yearly, the Batavia factory reply that they would not know what to do with all the cloth thus procured, seeing that the Dutch have two-thirds of the trade in the Moluccas and pay their soldiers in cotton cloth. By Methwold's instructions they have bought some 'moores<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-102" href="#sec-04-fn-102" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-102" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>', but they are not so long as desired, being only 15½ and 16 'covetts'; think that probably they could be procured more cheaply at Masulipatam. The Dutch purchased 4,000 pieces last year, 'most partt uncurd, but ours are all cured.' 'They are very deere, for they stands us in above 1¾ rials per peece, being from 14½ to 15½ covetts long; and noe better for other sorts of Muslapatam, as bethills<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-103" href="#sec-04-fn-103" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-103" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and sallampours,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-104" href="#sec-04-fn-104" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-104" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> heere not to be procured.' Have used up the gold thread sent from Masulipatam. In future 2,000 papers will meet all their needs. As regards providing themselves with better accommodation, they will 'make a vertue of necesitie' and remain content until the money can be better spared. Consider the price given by the Dutch for pepper on this coast to be excessive. In answer to their remonstrances regarding the division of cloth, the Hollanders always appeal to the last article in the agreement;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-105" href="#sec-04-fn-105" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-105" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> think it very desirable this clause should be altered. Note the instructions to conceal from the Dutch their intentions as regards future investments, but find this would only harm themselves by raising prices; hope to find some method of agreement instead. The booty (39,000 rials) with which the Manilla fleet has returned to Japan seems a poor result, considering the heavy charges. Hear that the fleet sent to the Malabar coast is proceeding to Mozambique. Having now answered all points in the Batavia letters, they proceed to narrate what has occurred at this place. The <em>Unity</em> reached <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-144" data-page="144" data-folio="105" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n143_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 105">[p. 105]</button> they were obliged to remain within the limits of Pulicat, for fear of the enemy. Some of the native merchants express astonishment at the English quitting Petapoli to come and live at Pulicat at much greater expense, affirming that the cloth made at the former place is far better and cheaper than that of the latter; 'the only differance in the sorts of each fardell not their performed [as ?] heer, neyther the borders and richcos<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-106" href="#sec-04-fn-106" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-106" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> so neatly and clearly wrought,' though perhaps the provision of patterns might induce them to remedy this. Certainly the colours are better at Petapoli; and perhaps other kinds of paintings might be introduced. Many weavers and painters have voluntarily offered to follow the English wherever they choose to go; but they dare not encourage such a plan without sanction from Batavia. Explain the method by which the gold thread used in the cloths has been calculated in the invoice. Urge the supply of patterns of the varieties of cloth most in demand. 'Concerning your required provision of 14 or 15 slaves from the age of 16 to 20 yeares fitt for labor and to be sent along in the shipp, which att present wee can by no meanes performe, parte in regard of this short warning, besides the tymes much altered in that qualitye, the Duch haveing bought all that came to hand since our abidding, and now by a late advice brougt by our shipp are heer ordered to buy as manye as possible can be procured, to the nomber of four or five thousand of men, woomen and chilldren, and, rather then faile, to leave ther other affaires and follow that designe;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-107" href="#sec-04-fn-107" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-107" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> for which cause they have layd the countrey all over, standing uppon no price; for whearas uppon our first seating one might have bought a slave of the age afore prescribed for four or five pag[odas], now you cannot gett the lyke under 12 or 14, and not to be procured as in thos tymes. Thus [this ?] their proceeding is much distasted heer amongst all, and if not in tyme remydied and by them forborne will caus much alteracion; for most of thos slaves brought them to sell are stollen uppon the highwayes and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-145" data-page="145" data-folio="104" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n144_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 104">[p. 104]</button> the c local on 1 or 1: at : com de&lt; to ac&lt; 01 I' 1 } 1 <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-146" data-page="146" data-folio="107" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n145_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 107">[p. 107]</button> have imployment in our afaires, that will peradventure cause them forsake the placce, which is common in theise sorte of people to exacte.’ They can provide twenty bahars of gunpowder yearly, but will do nothing until instructions are received. That now sent is better than any brought out of the ship, and should be reserved for special occasions. Deficiency of 104 rials in one chest; fear there has been some villainy. At least 400 of the rials were ‘mild’ [milled?], and these will not pass here except at a loss. The Dutch manage things much better. Price of rials at present very low. The pagoda is here worth 1½ rials, or a quarter less than the pagoda at Masulipatam. The last Dutch ship from Holland brought a quantity of gold in small bars, which has proved very profitable. ‘Thus in every quallitye they goe beyound us.’ Have contracted, according to instructions, for 1,000 pieces of sail-cloth, ‘which is not heere made but att Tragnuputam [Tegnapatam or Cuddalore], whence comes also the blachues [<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 24] and rumbatteenes.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-108" href="#sec-04-fn-108" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-108" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Have also bought 480 pieces of sail-cloth, which are better than those of Masulipatam, and also longer and larger. Thank them for their present of wine. ‘Wee may not forgett the Duches curtesys in the lyke and many other qualities, who out of ther bounties have not sparred to furnish us in a plentifull manner; and find them forward and free in all exteriour curtisyes, and so live frindly together in these kinds; and they aleadg, if any thing by us distasted in the way of our trade, that wee appeale to the principalls; and withall tell us they proceed but as authorised, which they are oblidgd to follow, and wee to addvise if wronged, and wher the authoritie sitts ther to procure the remydie.’ The <em>Unity</em> is in bad condition and carelessly looked after; fear the goods will be damaged by rain. Our skippers should copy the Dutch in this respect. Accounts, &amp;c., sent to Masulipatam to be examined and then forwarded to Batavia.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-109" href="#sec-04-fn-109" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-109" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> (<em>Copy. 15 pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-147"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="147" data-folio="108" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n146_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 108">[p. 108]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory</span>, July 27, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 131).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have made a further remittance to Baroda to provide baftas. Some good ‘Daboyes cloth’ received and more expected. Progress of their investments. The Dutch are buying busily, and have sent their broker, Trickandas [Trikam Dās], to Agra with bills for 5,000 mahmūdīs to invest there. Other brokers are purchasing cotton goods for Achin and the Red Sea. (<em>Copy. 2 pp. Received July 28.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes at Agra to the Surat Factory, August</span> 1, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 132).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Thinks it useless in present circumstances to ‘revive the Sindie busines’, but Willoughby has instructions to press the matter on Āsaf Khān when presenting the certificate from Surat. Willoughby has also been furnished with money, and left to take his choice of means ‘for his best securmentt’. The ivory has been sold at Rs. 45 ‘the m[aun]d ackberye’ [Akbarī, i.e. of Akbar]; a low price, but it was in bad condition and there was little demand. Rs. 12,000 remitted to Burhānpur. Nobody in Agra will give bills on Surat at any price. Hopes shortly to close the factory (leaving a few remains with the broker) and join them. Has bought some spikenard, which will be sent to them as soon as the rains are over. ‘Candahar is reported to bee taken by the Persian, and some saye the Kinge is one his waye from Cashiemer to Lahore.’ (<em>Copy. 2 pp. Received August 30.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, August</span> 1, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 133).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Goods are coming in daily. Shortage in the gunny sent them. Have paid the balance due to the ‘puccalls’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-110" href="#sec-04-fn-110" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-110" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> who brought it. The ship carpenters sent to Surat from this place desire to return, alleging that they are not paid the terms agreed upon. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received August 2.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-148"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="148" data-folio="109" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n147_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 109">[p. 109]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Henry Darrell, John Benthall, John Hayward, Thomas Tomson, and Christopher Rosons at Ispahān to the Surat Factory, August 3, 1622</span> (<em>Factory Records, Persia</em>, vol. i. p. 159).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In April last the commanders of the fleet wrote advising them to purchase silk on credit to the value of ten or twelve thousand pounds, to be paid for from the spoils of Ormus. Since then they hear that these spoils have been sold at less than their value by upwards of twenty per cent, and that the proceeds are to be taken to Surat in ready money. As, acting upon the previous advices, they have already procured 200 bales of silk on credit, they hope that the Surat factory will upon receipt remit the money to them. A good quantity of silk and other merchandise will be ready at Gombroon before the end of October. Results of the sale of Indian and English commodities. Spices are scarce. <em>P.S.</em>—They request some cloth for packing silk. (<em>Copy. 3¾ pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Halstead and William Hill at Ahmadābād to the Surat Factory, August</span> 6, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS. 2123</em>, f. 134).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Bills for Rs. 8,475 received; but the list is missing, and should be sent by the bearer of this. ‘Our lynens come in dayly’, and will all be received within 15 days. Sugar is very dear, being Rs. 5 per maund; conserves, Rs. 4 or 4½ per maund; indigo, Rs. 8 or 9. They repudiate the charge of negligence. (<em>Copy. ¾ p. Received August 9.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, August</span> 10, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 134).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have been endeavouring to procure the goods required, ‘butt all this tyme itt hath beene soe extreame raynes thatt neather beater cann beate, washer can give cangee [starch: Tamil <em>kanji</em>], nor wee looke uppon nill’ [indigo]. Request a remittance of money. Details of the goods expected. Indigo has risen on a rumour that they intend to buy for three or four lacs of rupees. Sugar, however, has fallen, a caravan having arrived with a supply. It is a bad commodity to buy in the rains, as it is <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-149" data-page="149" data-folio="110" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n148_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 110">[p. 110]</button> then dear and weighs much heavier. Have bought ‘conserves of myrabilons’ and arranged for a supply of ginger. Will send the goods on camels, buffaloes, and oxen. Skins of buffaloes and oxen are needed for packing, ‘for since the Rajaes ary[val] hee hath suffered none to bee killed.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-111" href="#sec-04-fn-111" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-111" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Cost of the goods already provided. (<em>Copy.</em> 2 <em>pp. Received August</em> 13.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, August</span> 15, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 135).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The bills from Surat for 10,000 mahmūdīs were received, and duly accepted by the ‘shraffes’ [<em>see</em> p. 46] on whom they were drawn. ‘Surgee Ingedas’, however, has refused to pay the 3,500 mahmūdīs due from him unless they will allow him fifteen days more; so they return the bills for that amount, in order that recovery may be made in Surat. Have not received their cotton cloth from Baroda and Dabhoi, as the rains have made the ways impassable. Give particulars of their investments, including broad and narrow baftas, ‘neccanes,’ ‘poppele,’ ‘chokree,’ ‘cappere,’ ‘hussane,’ ‘buckar,’ ‘gingams,’ ‘duttyes,’ ‘trickandes,’ and ‘seales’. Of the 28,138 pieces of coarse narrow baftas, 23,000 are for England, while the rest (together with the fine narrows and the various stuffs) are for the southwards. Hope to dispatch these goods shortly, and request that arrangements may be made with Is-hāq Beg for passing them free at Ankleswar, as was done last year. The Dutch are still buying baftas, but not to any great extent, and they declare themselves that their investment is nearly finished. (<em>Copy.</em> 2½ <em>pp. Received August</em> 17.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Parker at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory, August</span> 17, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 136).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Fifteen dayes since I aryved heere, and left the cartes one this side the Ryver Nervada [Narbadā] aboutt 40 cources [<em>kos</em>] offe. Myselfe beinge then somewhatt ill disposed to health came before with the jeweIs, which with Rampaxads bills I have delyvered to Mr. Bangam. Since my cominge I returned to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-150" data-page="150" data-folio="111" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n149_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 111">[p. 111]</button> the cartes, and yesterdaye left them, with the goods in good conditionn, att Caralee [Khīrala, near Borgāon], 12 courses from Brampore, where they must attend two or three dayes of fayre weather, the wayes beinge soe deepe that cartes cannott travell.’ Expenses and troubles of the journey. ‘Neare Mandoe both cartes and goods weare seased one by the Princes officers for there masters use; where I was three or four dayes pusled to cleare them, and after expence of neere 40 rup[ees] in brybes att last was forced to goe to the Prynce myselfe, whoe through meanes of Mahmud Tucke [Mahmūd Taqī] gave mee a pirwana for there release.’ Mr. Bangham and Signor Robert [i.e. Hutchinson] intend shortly to go to Māndū to recover a debt in the Prince’s camp; then they will go straight to Surat. He himself remains at Burhānpur to dispatch his caravan of goods, but fears he will be unable to do this until the middle of next month. The jewels are also left in his care. Requests instructions what to do if he cannot get his goods away in time to be at Surat by September 20. Shall he come on with the jewels and leave the goods in the custody of ‘Burra,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-112" href="#sec-04-fn-112" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-112" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> a Jett [Jat] whome some tymes you have [app]roved off for trusty’, or shall he wait and bring both jewels and goods together? <em>P.S.</em>—Has just heard that the carts have reached Gur[      ],<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-113" href="#sec-04-fn-113" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-113" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> three <em>kos</em> from this place, and will arrive to-morrow. Will at once house them, and then seek for means to convey them to Surat. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp. Received August 26.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham and Robert Hutchinson at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> 17, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 137).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Mr. Parker arrived on the first with the jewels, leaving his goods about twelve <em>kos</em> behind. Their own departure has been delayed by the difficulty of obtaining the necessary papers in the case of Soares [<em>see the preceding volume</em>, p. 89]; but they hope to start shortly for Māndū and thence proceed to Surat ‘by the wayes of Awas’ [<em>ibid.</em>, p. 210 <em>note</em>], leaving Parker to look after his goods. The latter brought ‘Rampaxad’s’ bills, but they <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-151" data-page="151" data-folio="112" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n150_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 112">[p. 112]</button> cannot now find that individual. 'Cann Channa [the Khānkhānān] hath withdrawne from him his barratts [<em>see</em> p. 67] for the mony thatt hee should have paid him, which maketh him playe least in sight.' He is said to have gone with the Khānkhānān to Māndū. The matter is left in Parker's hands pending orders from Surat. 'Heere is newes thatt the Princes journey to Agemere is stayd by order from the K[ing], and reporte sayeth hee will returne for [Burhānpur?] and thatt hee will send his sonn, a childe [     ] yeares of age, in the conductt of Drabb<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-114" href="#sec-04-fn-114" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-114" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and divers others of his one sarvantts to the quantetye of five thousand horse to guarde Tutta and to take with them in the waye Biker Mageets [Vikramājīt], the Raja in Amadavaz, and the Raja Janne [Rājā Jahān?], Chann Channa beinge gonn to Mandoe to consulte uppon this busines; wheare itt may bee doubted thatt our names will bee questioned for assisting the Persian, and (as some heere sayeth) for givinge him transportt of men and munision in our shippes to Tutta;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-115" href="#sec-04-fn-115" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-115" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> which wee should bee unwillinge to heare of. Yf soe, wee must pleade innosence, and abide the bruntt of an unseasonable conditioned man.' (<em>Copy. 1½ pp. Received August 26.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Fursland and Council at Batavia to the Surat Factory, August</span> 18, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 197).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last on May 12 [<em>not extant</em>]. On May 21 received their letter of January 24, with additions dated March 7. 'The riches taken by the <em>Weson</em> [<em>Wesp</em>] falls much shorte of thatt which you write. Reportt there affermeth the Dutch heere have receaved butt 27,000 rials in vallue, butt itt maye verey well bee thatt the purchace taken was worth a greate deale more, for the saylers att there aryvall heere had good store of gould, which since they have dispercedd in the tavarnes and whorehouses, which commonly is the end of such gotten goods. Wee should much wonder att the unadvised proceedinge of the commaunder of the fleetes sentt to Persia contrary to order, yf our selves <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-152" data-page="152" data-folio="113" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n151_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 113">[p. 113]</button> weare nott accostomed to the licke proceedinges by the masters imployed in the Companys shipps in this places, whoe are noe sooner outt of this roade butt they doe whatt they list, whatt commission soeever wee give them, presuminge uppon the slight authoretye which our masters gives us for the punnishinge of them; yett we never found any to transgresse in soe high a degree as those under your command have nowe donne, whose rash proceedinges will cause such intollorable losse unto our imployers thatt, yf they weare nott compelled thereunto by the Kinge for the safetye of the trade, they deserve to bee punished with the greatest severetye of justice, thatt other maye take exsample and take heede howe they transgres commission in soe waighty matters. As wee are sorry to heare of the confusion and distruction which those proceedinges have caused in your affayres, soe wee cannott butt lamentt the greate losse which our masters are lickely to sustayne by your diverting of the Sumatra cargasonn for the Red Sea, and wee wish thatt the <em>Lyonn</em> and the small pinnace allone might have supplyed thatt busines; for although the Hollanders are insolentt enough, yett wee are perswaded thatt they dare nott bee soe bould as to overthrough our masters affayres in those places of Surratt &amp;c. by surprysinge the junckes of thatt place. By meanes of this unlicked [unlooked for?] accident wee shall loose this whole yeares trade in Achine.’</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cannot themselves send a ship thither, and the want of a supply from thence will prevent them from dispatching one home this year. Loss of the <em>Trial</em> coming from England. Two factors killed at Sukadana. Fear that the factory there has been sacked by the natives. News of ships homeward and outward bound. Spices expected from the eastwards. Will shortly send fresh instructions to the Fleet of Defence. ‘Thus farr is coppye of our last sentt you per waye Messulapatam. Since which tyme wee have had divers conferences with the Dutch aboutt the Goga [Goa] fleete, and after seryoues consideracionns itt is found requisite thatt the whole fleete shall remaine uppon the cost of Goa and Mallabar untell the last of March, or soe longe as the monsoone will permitt, to keepe in the carracke from returninge to Portinggall, according to our former commissionn given them. And seeinge this exploytte is begonne by us, yf itt should nowe be given over <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-153" data-page="153" data-folio="114" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n152_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 114">[p. 114]</button> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">consent</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">......</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">intend</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">to</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">see</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">our</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">things</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">that</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">we</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">cannot</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">otherwise</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">mean</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">to</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">serve</span> trade <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">to</span> have <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">seen</span> the <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">men</span> <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">of</span> all our masters afayres both att Mocha and Surratt toarynge the Generall and his Counsell to take notice thatt wee had declared soe much unto them, thatt wee heare after they may nott pleade ignorance yf any disastur comes by there occasionn. The disposinge any shippes for the Red Sea wee have wholy defferred unto you and your Counsell, as maye appeare by your letters sentt by Captain <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-154" data-page="154" data-folio="115" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n153_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 115">[p. 115]</button> Fitcharbartt.’ Are greatly in need both of money and victuals. Trust that they will send a ship to Achin and thence to Batavia. Piece-goods suitable. Intended dispatch of the <em>Lesser James</em> for England. Butter and meal should be forwarded yearly to Batavia. The former must be put into casks, not sent in ‘dubbers’ [<em>see</em> p. 8]. <em>P.S.</em>—Have sent some goods for sale. (<em>Copy.</em> 7 <em>pp. Received December</em> 4.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, August 21, 1622</span> (<em>Brit. Mus.· Egerton MS. 2123, f. 138</em>).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They are keeping ‘Surge Ingedas’ prisoner in their house till they hear that satisfaction has been received at Surat for the bills he accepted and then refused to pay. Details of receipts of cotton cloth. The Dutch are buying little, and the purchasers for Achin have almost finished their investment; so prices are now reasonable. Request a supply of money. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received August 23.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham and Robert Hutchinson at Burhānpur to the Surat Factory, August 23, 1622</span> (<em>Ibid., f. 138</em>).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Parker’s carts have arrived and the goods have been housed; but ‘cartage is hard to bee procured, by reasonn of many greate mens occasionn for cartes and cammells to travaile towards Mandoe and soe for the Kinges laskar, the Kinge havinge called from hence all his one sarvantts, leavinge this cuntry wholy to his sonns proteccionn.’ On the 18th received letters from Hughes at Agra, forwarding bills for Rs. 12,000; have exchanged these for bills on Surat, which they now send, having turned the rupees into ‘mamothes’ at the rate of Rs. 452 or 453 per thousand mahmūdīs. As Bangham is still awaiting his discharge in the case of Soares, it has been decided to send Hutchinson alone to Māndū to see what he can do in the recovery of that money in the Prince’s sarkār [<em>see</em> p. 38]. The Prince knows all about the business of Soares, having been informed by the Khānkhānān and ‘Abotta Canne’ [Ābid Khān]. Bangham will await Hutchinson’s return, and hopes that by that time the weather will permit them to make a start for Surat. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp. Received August</em> 30.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-155"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="155" data-folio="116" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n154_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 116">[p. 116]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the Surat Factory, August</span> 24, 1622 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, f. 139).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Were glad to hear that satisfaction had been received at Surat for the bills which the shroff refused to pay. They kept him prisoner a day and a night longer, in hopes of forcing him to make some amends; but finding him resolute, have now released him on the understanding that if called upon by the President he is to pay 35 mahmūdīs, which will be one per cent on the amount of the bills. Have taken up 4,000 mahmūdīs from ‘Roopegee Surgee’ [Rūpjī Sūrjī] and given him a bill on Surat, payable to ‘Cawarge Herydas’ [Kuvarjī Hari Dās]. This cost one-half per cent commission. As ordered, will continue to buy baftas, to be sent ‘browne’, as time will not permit their being ‘whited’. Narrow baftas are easily obtainable; but for broad, ‘wee must give outt our mony beforehand, and receave the proceedes of itt att the weavers and brokers pleasuer.’ So, unless otherwise instructed, they will merely buy those that come into the market, and not order any specially. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp. Received August</em> 25.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, August</span> 27, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, f. 139).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received the bills for 3,000 mahmūdīs; also a further sum of 10,000 mahmūdīs ‘in mamothes and rupes’, brought by Thomas Hawkridge. Return some as not passable except at a loss, and request that they may be exchanged. ‘Callyange’ [Kalyānji] who has been furnishing them with goods from Baroda, is said to have been put in prison for doing so. Nothing yet received from Dabhoi. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received August</em> 28.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Fursland and Council at Batavia to the Company, August</span> 27, 1622 (<em>O.C.</em> 1076).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-116" href="#sec-04-fn-116" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-116" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Money sent to Pulicat in the <em>Unity</em>. At that place ‘the Duch doe us all the wrong they can’, insisting that the cloth received should be divided strictly in accordance with the proportions of the two Companies’ investments, though they make the English pay their full share of the expenses. It is a pity that they ever had anything to do with <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-156" data-page="156" data-folio="117" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n155_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 117">[p. 117]</button> the Dutch, there or elsewhere. Little expected from Masulipatam this year. ‘For the Goa Fleete, haveinge a little hope that it may in some measure bee contynnued to proceede the next yeare, wee have thought itt better to make faier promises unto the Duch to perfourme, then utterly to dissable ourselves and soe give them full advauntage to protest against you for not joyninge with them accordinge to the contract.
