(<em>Copies.</em> 4 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Young and Justinian Offley at Broach to the Surat Factory, December 29, 1622</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 228).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Money received and delivered to the brokers. A further remittance necessary ; also a list of the goods to be provided. ‘As yett wee have nott gotten a barratt [<em>see</em> p. 67] of the Governor for our monyes, butt are in hope to have itt to morrowe. Hee goes for Mando within two or three dayes, and leaves his brother in his place. The Governor this daye receaved letters from the Princes courte, the contents as followeth : the Raja of Amadavaz is made 5,000 horse, and sentt to Agra to keepe itt for the Prince ; Rustamchon 5,000 horse ; and soe the rest of his sarvantts all are intrested and many desperced towards Agra and Agemeere to keepe the wayes. Hee hath lickewaies desposed [deposed] Pelwansaffeed<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-153" href="#sec-04-fn-153" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-153" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and taken his masters jaggeere from him, and all other thatt [is ?] belonginge to the Kinge or any nobellman of this contrary [country] of Guzeratt. Whatt the issue of this busines will come unto God knowes. To prevent the worst itt weare good to free ourselves of as much estate as maye possible bee invested [on ?] returne of the fyrst shipp.’ Request an invoice for the goods sent to them. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p. Received December 31.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-216"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="216" data-folio="177" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n215_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 177">[p. 177]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Joseph Salbank and Nicholas Crispe at Cambay to the Surat Factory, December 30, 1622</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 229).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Refer to previous correspondence. The prices of goods received from Jī Rām Shāh are net, as he refused to deal on any other condition. It is useless to appeal to the Governor against him, ‘they goinge in hand and hand in all there villanyes, and Jeramshawe the cheeffe plotter of them all.’ They must seek to Khidr Khān for redress. Note their instructions, and in future will give a longer time for their bills. Will also observe their directions as to packing and making the bales. Thank them for a further remittance. ‘Six dayes since the Governor gave lysence for the marchaunts and brokers of this place to buy and sell accordinge to there accustomed order, withoutt any unusuall duties to bee paid him, dareing nott to persist any further in his dishonest projectt, fearinge hee should incur the displeasuer of the Kinges merchantt aryved heere, as formerly advised in our last, doubtinge his advisinge his master, which would worke the Governor a greater inconvenency then the taxacion hee layd uppon the brokers would beniffitt him, which yf hee persisted in his tyraninge might have bine to the endangeringe of his life, or att the least to the loosinge of his jageere yf the Kinge should have had notice thereof (which threats this merchantt used). Soe thatt now this tyrannous tigar pursueth noe further after his praye butt is returned to his denn in peace.’ Yesterday the time fixed by Jī Rām Shāh expired, but he has only delivered a few more goods, ‘hee still feedinge us with shabash<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-154" href="#sec-04-fn-154" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-154" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, according to the base and wicked custome of this cuntrye.’ He now demands higher rates for future deliveries, on the ground that prices have risen; but they stand on their contract and demand either the goods or a money equivalent. He still promises satisfaction, but they doubt his performance. Last night a broker told them that he had orders from Surat to deliver them goods to the value of 8,000 mahmūdīs. He promises delivery within fifteen days, but they know not whether he is to be trusted. Having received the cloth and satin sent from Surat as a present to the Governor, they sought <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-217" data-page="217" data-folio="178" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n216_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 178">[p. 178]</button> an interview and offered the parcel to him. He refused, however, to take anything until their business were ended. They related to him their grievances against Jī Rām Shāh, and he promised to urge the latter to give them satisfaction. On the question of raising the prices, he sided with him, urging that his demand was reasonable. They beg that a limit may be fixed for their stay here, and that answers may be sent to the inquiries in their last letter. (<em>Copy. 5 pp. Received January 2.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same</span>, December 30, 1622 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 234).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Advise having drawn upon them for Rs. 4,926⅓. Refer to their general letter for other particulars. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received January 5.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Matthew Duke, Francis Futter, and John Dod at Masulipatam to the Surat Factory</span>, December 31, 1622 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 268).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Not having received any reply to their letters, they are afraid that ‘yours and ours are both cutt of’. Recapitulate therefore the chief contents of theirs of October 26 [<em>see</em> p. 134]. Sailing of Dutch ships. Are now expecting the <em>Schiedam</em>, ‘with purchace from the coast of Bengala.’ She is to be laden for Holland. <em>P.S.</em>—Agreed with the pattamar for 3½ pagodas, of which he has received 2¼. (<em>Copy. 1¼ pp. Received February 1.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Jewels sold on account of Morris Abbot</span>, 1622–25 (<em>O.C.</em> 1092).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Five emeralds received by Weddell’s fleet. Two sold for 2,910 rupees, of which 5,396 mahmūdīs 30 pice have been paid into the Company’s cash, to be delivered to Mr. Abbot at the rate of 6<em>s.</em> the rial of eight. The latter now bears a constant price of five mahmūdīs, less 2¾ pice for the old stamp and 4¾ pice for the new. (Letter from Surat dated December 22, 1622.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Note on interest to be charged. One per cent per month is the usual ‘bezar’ rate. (Ditto, January 9, 1624.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The 1,000<em>l.</em> jewel sold for 5,280 rupees; and a pair of pendant pearls for 2,300 rupees. The jewel of balass rubies was finally disposed of for 12,000 rupees. Three emeralds realized 3,000 rupees gross; deducting four per cent ‘desturi’ [<em>see the preceding volume</em>, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-218" data-page="218" data-folio="179" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/tdl.46404-the-english-factories-in-india-1622-1623-a-calendar-of-documents-in-th/page/n217_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 179">[p. 179]</button> p. 193 <em>n.</em>] and one per cent brokerage, the net sum is 2,850 rupees, which should be paid to Mr. Abbot at 6<em>s.</em> the ‘dollour’. (Ditto, February 14 and 15, 1625.)</p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-04-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-001" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">He appears to have been a master’s mate, first in the <em>Diamond</em> and then in the <em>Exchange</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Off the west coast of Sumatra, between lat. 2° 40′ and 3° 30′ S.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Wapen van Zeeland</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-004" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-004" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arabic <em>sharīf</em>, 'noble,' a title used by those Muhammadans who claim descent from the Prophet.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-005" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-005" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">San Jago, at the entrance to Mozambique harbour.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-006" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-006" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the Viceroy's letter (January 5, 1623, N. S.) in <em>Lisbon Transcripts, Books of the Monsoons</em>, vol. v.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-007" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-007" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">But see note on p. 7.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-008" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-008" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Honāwar, at the mouth of the Gersoppa river. See the description by Della Valle (Hakl. Soc. edn., p. 202), who was there in October, 1623.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-009" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-009" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">From a journal kept on board the <em>Royal Exchange</em>. After September 24, 1622, the entries are in the handwriting of Richard Swanley, who was first the mate and then the master of that vessel. He must be carefully distinguished from the Richard Swanley who was in the <em>Jonas</em> (see p. 7) at this time. It will be noticed that the dates given in this journal differ at times from those in the preceding account.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-010" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-010" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Réunion. The name of England's Forest was given to it by Captain Castleton, commander of the <em>Pearl</em>, which was the first English ship to visit the island (1613).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-011" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-011" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Kisima-Julu Harbour, about twenty-two miles north of Mozambique.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-012" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-012" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Master or master's mate in the <em>Jonas</em>, of Captain Weddell's fleet (<em>see</em> previous volume, p. 271).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-013" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-013" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Johan van der Burch: see his narrative in <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i, vol. vi (No. 217). He and his companions were taken to Diu and thence to Goa. There they were thrown into prison and miserably treated. The arrival of Dedel's fleet brought hopes of an exchange of prisoners but the negotiations came to nothing (<em>see</em> p. 4). At last, in February, 1624, Van der Burch and others were shipped to S. Thomé, whence in April they were taken to Pulicat and exchanged for some Portuguese prisoners.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-014" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-014" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Described as being sixteen <em>kos</em> from Burhānpur. Possibly it is the village of Navī, about twenty-six miles south-west of that city.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-015" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-015" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>dabbah</em>, a jar made of hides, usually employed for storing <em>ghee</em> or oil.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-016" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-016" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Iskander (Alexander), the interpreter and broker of the Surat Factory. Della Valle (vol. i. p. 29) says that he was an Armenian Catholic.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-017" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-017" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Gujarātī <em>gadiānun</em>, a goldsmith’s weight equivalent to about 52 grains</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-018" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-018" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Rustam Khān had been appointed by Prince Shāh Jahān to the government of Gujarāt in November, 1621 (see the previous volume). According to the <em>Tūzak</em> he was originally a Badakhshī named Yusuf Beg, who distinguished himself in the campaign against Udaipur.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-019" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-019" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">These places may be identified with the Watuwa and Kanij of the Indian Atlas, the former being four and the latter twelve miles from Ahmadābād on the way to Baroda.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-020" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-020" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arabic <em>Khalīfa</em>, 'one who succeeds to sovereign power,' a title given to the successors of Muhammad. It is also used (as here) to mean a deputy or lieutenant.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-021" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-021" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>lashkar</em>, 'army' or 'camp,' the usual term for the Mogul Emperor's encampment.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-022" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-022" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">From a copy forwarded by the Surat Council to their colleagues at Batavia. What became of the original articles is not known. In a letter to the Company, dated October 15, 1623 (<em>O. C.</em> 1120) the Persian factors declared that they were taken away by the commanders of the ships, and asked that they might be returned. About 1642 the Agent in Persia applied to Surat for the document, but the President and Council answered that it was not in their possession and that there was no record of its having been forwarded to them (<em>O. C.</em> 1808). Forty years later a committee of inquiry at home reported that according to a letter from Persia, dated February 29, 1680 [not extant], the agreement was sent home in the <em>Great James</em> about 1630 in the charge of Captain Weddell, but that it could not be found at the East India House (<em>Court Minutes</em>, April 7, 1682).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-023" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-023" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. January 11, 1622 (O. S.). But according to Monnox (<em>Purchas</em>, vol. i. p. 1794) the agreement was signed on the 9th.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-024" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-024" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Jarūn, the Persian name for the island of Ormus.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-025" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-025" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the other version on p. 16.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-026" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-026" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Capitão Môr</em>, or commander-in-chief, i. e. Ruy Freire. The Captain of Ormus at the beginning of the year was Francisco de Sousa; but he died shortly after and was succeeded (January 17) by Simão de Mello.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-027" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-027" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Suhār, on the coast of Omān.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-028" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-028" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The only other contemporary version at the India Office is one entered among the records of the Ispahān factory (<em>Persia</em>, vol. i. p. 104). As this is from an independent translation, we append it for reference :— ‘ Betweene us and the English was this agreement, noe otherwaies then upon our words. First, that, God givinge us the victory over our enemies that wee shall overcome this contrye and citty of Ormuz, the halfe of all that shall bee found within the cittye, within the houses, and within the castell shall remaine, the one halfe to us, the other to the English. If any shall privatly imbeazle any goods, that the one shall suspect in the other, that for clearinge they shall either of them take theire oathes on theire lawes. The gaine of the Alfandicae [Port. (from Arabic) <em>alfandega</em>, ‘ a custom-house’] etc. shalbe halfe the English and half the Persians. 2. God granting that wee may overcome the castell, that halfe English, halfe Persians shall remain therin; the one to doe nothing without mutuall consent of the other, till such time as I shall wright unto the Kinge to deliver it into the hands of the English. 3. That the shipps of the Kinge or Chaune coming with goods from India shalbe free from paying any fraight or custome, and likewise the shipps and goods of the English ; but if any marchants shall come upon the said shipps they shall not bee freed therof. And whatsoeever shipps of India or other places cominge to this side Ormus shall paye the foresaid duties, the one halfe of the which gaines to remaine to us and the other to the English. 4. If any our slaves or boyes shall flye unto the English to become Christians, they shall not entertaine them ; and if the Christians shall flye unto us, that wee shall not cause them to become Moors. 5. That all the portes to India, as Muscate and the rest upon the sea coast, if wee shall overcome them, the halfe shalbe ours and the other halfe the Englishes. 6. Off those castells which wee shall overcome, if the Captins therof shalbe Frankes, the English shall enjoye the one halfe and wee the other. And such mint houses as shalbe taken shall have the mony there coyned stamped with the name of the Kinge of Persia. 7. The shipps of the English, soe longe as they shall remaine in warr, that the expense of the shipps, powder, shott, etc. shalbe halfe upon the English and the other halfe upon us. 8. Soe longe as they shalbe in warr, that for cattell, sheep, henns, butter, etc. shalbe provided, halfe at the charge of the English and the other halfe at our charge. 9. That when the warrs shalbe fynished, yf there shalbe any necessetye of service for defence of the castell, that som of the English shipps shall remaine heere, and that that charge alsoe shalbe borne halfe by us and halfe by the English. 10. That the English shall make noe avanias [Ital. <em>avania</em>, ‘an exaction’] upon the Mores; and that if any greate menn shall flye unto the English to</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-029" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-029" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is an abstract in <em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. i. p. 103.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-030" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-030" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Important matters were decided by a council of eight, of whom four were Dutch and four English. In the case of the council being equally divided in opinion, the Admiral had a casting vote.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-031" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-031" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Now in the Dutch service. She had been captured in July, 1619, and retained in compensation for the <em>Black Lion</em>, which had been burnt while in the hands of the English.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-032" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-032" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly the copyist's perversion of some name intended for Sirhind.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-033" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-033" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Kuhistak, a small port on the Persian coast, about forty miles south-east of Ormus. There is another copy of this letter among the <em>Persia Records</em> (vol. i. p. 94); also an abstract in <em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. i. p. 119.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-034" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-034" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">On December 1 (<em>Persia</em>, vol. i. p. 6).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-035" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-035" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">An infant named Ummīd Bakhsh.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-036" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-036" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Here and elsewhere the copyist appears to have confused Āsaf Khān with Afzal Khān, the Dīwān of Prince Shāh Jahān (<em>see previous volume</em>, p. 36). The latter is of course meant.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-037" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-037" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Perhaps Francisco Pelsart, of whom see later.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-038" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-038" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">An abstract, wrongly dated Feb. 17, will be found in <em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. i. p. 128.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-039" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-039" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Called in other letters ‘Sabanary’, ‘Sabander’, ‘Savendak’, and ‘Sanduck’. Della Valle gives the name as ‘Sevenduk Sultan’. Probably it should be ‘Sandūk Sultān’.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-040" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-040" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>tūmān</em>. On p. 65 this amount is treated as equivalent to 2,000<em>l.</em>, which would make the <em>tūmān</em> 3<em>l.</em> 6<em>s.</em> 8<em>d.</em>—the value assigned a little later by Thomas Herbert.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-041" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-041" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Purchas</em>, vol. i. p. 1793; also <em>O.C.</em> 1032.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-042" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-042" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Nau-rōz</em> or 'New (Year's) Day' festival, held on the first day of the solar year.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-043" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-043" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This date does not agree with Crosby's log (p. 1).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-044" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-044" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mīrzā Ghiāsuddīn Muhammad, Itimād-uddaula, father of Nūr Mahal and Āsaf Khān.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-045" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-045" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Shāh Jahān reported to his father that Khusrū had died of colic. ‘Hackam Alamandine’ is probably Hakīm (the physician) Alimuddīn. ‘Bickermazete’ is the Rājā Vikramājīt. The report of their being summoned to court was evidently untrue (cf. p. 54).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-046" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-046" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Evidently a bribe or commission of some sort is intended. The word may be the Hind. <em>lālach</em>, which primarily means greed or avarice.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-047" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-047" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Kāngra or Nagarkot, in the Punjab. It had been captured shortly before by the imperial forces under Shāh Jahān, and Jahāngīr had gone there to view his new possession.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-048" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-048" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently the Portuguese <em>cavallaria</em>, in the sense of an establishment of horses or other animals. It is used elsewhere to include coach-oxen.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-049" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-049" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">An error for ‘ruslack’, i. e. crude (Hindī <em>ras</em>) lac.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-050" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-050" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A pamphlet by 'J. R.', published in 1615, in which the East India trade was attacked and defendyd versus</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-051" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-051" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The copyist’s error for ‘Saccadanna’, i.e. Sukadana, the head quarters of the trade in Borneo diamonds.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-052" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-052" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Wrongly entered among the 1623 letters.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-053" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-053" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘I thinck this John Johnson went out with the Danes’ (<em>endorsement</em>). Possibly he was the man of that name who was at one time master of the <em>Globe</em> in the Ninth Voyage (with Brockedon). On the Danish expedition see the introduction to the previous volume.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-054" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-054" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Brockedon was then at Batavia, but the letter was probably addressed to him at Masulipatam, under the impression that he was still there.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-055" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-055" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The name of one of these fugitives appears to have been Richard Hastfield or Hatfield (<em>see</em> p. 82).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-056" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-056" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Tranquebar (see the previous volume, p. xlv; also a work just issued at Copenhagen by Kay Larsen, entitled <em>Dansk-Ostindiske Koloniers Historie: Trankebar</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-057" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-057" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is an abstract of this letter in <em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. i. p. 105.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-058" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-058" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Swanley was master’s mate in the <em>Lion</em> (see p. 49), which was accompanied by the <em>Rose</em> and <em>Richard</em>. The merchants employed were Messrs. Salbank, Heynes, Barber, Hoare and Crispe.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-059" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-059" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Bābu-l-mandab [Gate of Tears], which forms the entrance to the Red Sea.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-060" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-060" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See a letter from the Dutch factor Albert Becker in <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i, vol. vi. (no. 209). He was on board the <em>Wapen van Zeeland</em>, one of the two vessels detached by Dedel from the Fleet of Defence in Feb. 1622 for a voyage to the Red Sea. They reached Mokha on March 13 (N.S.), and ten days later Becker and twenty-seven others who were ashore were seized by the natives and rigorously imprisoned. On August 16, after two of their number had died, they were 'half miraculously' released, and on the 20th both ships sailed for India. They reached Swally on September 30, with a hundred men sick, having lost over fifty on the way. Cf. also a letter from Batavia in the same series, vol. v., no. 186, which says that the cause of the imprisonment was the piracies committed by the <em>Sampson</em> and the <em>Wesp</em>, but that peace was made and the factory continued.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-061" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-061" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The rest of the journal is occupied by an account of Swanley’s homeward voyage in the <em>Jonas</em>, in company with the <em>Lion</em> and the <em>London</em>. They sailed December 30, 1622, and reached the Thames July 22, 1623.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-062" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-062" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Della Valle says that Rastell spoke Italian very well.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-063" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-063" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is another copy among the India Office Records (<em>Java</em>, vol. iii. part i. p. 299).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-064" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-064" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The present Rānder, two miles above Surat, on the other side of the river. 'Ranel' appears to have been the Portuguese form.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-065" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-065" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Payment? This unusual word appears to be a form of 'inchest', i.e. to put into a chest or box.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-066" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-066" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. and Gujarātī <em>bohrā</em>, a merchant or trader. On the two sections of <em>Bohrās</em> in Gujarāt see <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, 2nd edn., p. 105.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-067" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-067" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Thus in the MS. The claim was previously stated to be 10,000 rials of eight (p. 68).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-068" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-068" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the Port. <em>arrasador</em>, 'a destroyer,' is meant.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-069" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-069" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Kotwāl</em>, the official responsible for policing the city.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">i.e. a gratuity. The custom of chewing betel-leaf and areca-nut is well known.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See pp. 18, 26; also the previous volume, p. 325.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind <em>pān-supārī</em>, the combination of betel, &amp;c., offered to guests at the termination of a visit.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Holī</em> festival, held at the approach of the vernal equinox.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is probably the Surat copyist’s misreading of ‘festival’.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">A noble. The word is really the plural of <em>Amīr</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-076" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-076" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pioneers, in the military sense of men employed to dig trenches and otherwise prepare the way for an army.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In Monnox's narrative the date is given as the 23rd, and this agrees with other accounts.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Cash. The Portuguese <em>dinheiro de contado</em> (ready money) seems to be intended.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is an abstract of this letter in <em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. i. p. 132.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">On his return to England Beversham, the master of the <em>Lion</em>, was interrogated by the Court regarding the escape of Ruy Freire. He ‘made answere that it was through the negligence of the watch; that he had sett a guard of six men with rapiers and pistolls, and had given commaundment that the skiff should be moord asterne, but contrary to his direccion it was left by the shippes side and he gott awaie’ (<em>Court Minutes</em>, July 25, 1623).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is an abstract in <em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. i. p. 131.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Itibār Khān, whom Jahāngīr had now placed in charge of the <em>sūbah</em> of Agra, together with the fort and treasury.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Rājā Vikramājīt, who had been appointed to succeed Rustam Khān as the Prince’s deputy at Ahmadābād.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The bonds given when the English were released (see p. 73).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-085" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-085" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Mahal</em> (lit. ‘a place of alighting’) is used generally of a house or abode, but also specially of the inner sanctum, i.e. the women’s apartments. From this we get by transfer the meaning (as here) of the wives (or even wife) of a great man. The Khān-i-Azam had several sons. Perhaps the eldest (Shamsī, better known as Jahāngīr Qulī Khān) is here intended.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-086" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-086" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Jats are well known for their bravery.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i.e. ‘a receipt (<em>qabz</em>) attested by the stamp (<em>chhāp</em>) of the judge (<em>Qāzī</em>).’</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Id</em>, a festival—possibly the <em>Shab-i-barāt</em>. This is held on the eve of the 14th Shaban, which that year fell on June 14.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Cloth from Dabhoi, a town fifteen miles south-east of Baroda.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>mansab</em>, the military rank of a Mogul officer, as indicated by the number of horse he was supposed to be able to bring into the field.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>tābūt</em>, a ‘coffin’ or ‘bier’.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Allāhābād is thus spelt in Baffin’s map. Khusrū was first buried at Burhānpur, but by the order of Jahāngīr the corpse was disinterred and taken to Allāhābād, where a handsome mausoleum was erected over it. The garden in which the tomb stands is still known as the Khusrū-bāgh.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Described in the letter as ‘20 courses [<em>kos</em>] past Surunge [Sironj]’.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Handiā is on the south side of the Narbadā, in the Hoshangābād district of the Central Provinces. The usual route between Agra and Burhānpur passed through this town.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bāreja, twelve miles north-east of Dholka.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For the dispatch of ‘Huybert Visnicht’ or Visnich to open up Dutch trade with <span class="folio-turn">[p. 98]</span> Persia see Valentyn's <em>Oud en Nieuw Oost-Indien</em>, vol. v. p. 232; Heeres' <em>Corpus Diplomaticum Neerlando-Indicum</em>, p. 183; and <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. vi. nos. 194, 198.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is an abstract of this letter in <em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. i. p. 132.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-098" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-098" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Sultan of Mataram, the principal native ruler in Java.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-099" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-099" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the introduction to the previous volume, pp. <span class="sc">xxx–xxxii</span>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-100" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-100" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Portuguese <em>cartas</em>, a pass.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-101" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-101" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Wrongly written 'Amadz' (Ahmadābād) in the MS.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-102" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-102" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">According to Balfour's <em>Cyclopaedia</em>, <em>moories</em> are blue cloths principally made now in Nellore and Chingleput.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-103" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-103" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A white muslin made at Masulipatam. The name is supposed to be connected with Portuguese <em>beatilha</em>, 'a veil.'</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-104" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-104" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">A similar cloth, mostly white. The derivation is said to be from Sālepura, 'Weaver-town.'</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-105" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-105" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">See p. 253 of the previous volume. The reference seems to be rather to the second article.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-106" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-106" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mr. Donald Ferguson identifies this word with the Portuguese <em>risca</em>, 'a stripe' (in cotton goods).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-107" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-107" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">In his instructions to his successor (January, 1623), Governor-General Coen laid stress on the desirability of obtaining as many slaves as possible from the coast of Coromandel, China, Madagascar, &amp;c., for the purpose of peopling the Dutch settlements in the Moluccas; he also refers to the action of Governor Van Uffelen in this direction during the previous year (<em>O.C.</em> 1093 ii).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-108" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-108" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Valentyn mentions ‘Ramboetyns’ among the cloths procured at Negapatam, Porto Novo, &amp;c. The name may be due to some resemblance in the pattern to the red hairy fruit of the Malay ‘rambutan’ tree.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-109" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-109" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">For a fragment of the Pulicat journal, containing entries for April-June, 1622, see p. 70.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-110" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-110" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Hind. <em>pakhālī</em>, ‘a water-carrier.’</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-111" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-111" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Being a Hindu.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-112" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-112" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably a nickname—either <em>Barā</em>, ‘big,’ or <em>Burhā</em>, ‘old.’</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-113" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-113" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly the ‘Jhirree’ of the Indian Atlas, a village about six miles north of Burhānpur.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-114" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-114" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Dārāb Khān, second son of the Khānkhānān. He was at this time an active partisan of Shāh Jahān.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-115" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-115" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A baseless rumour.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-116" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-116" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Abstracted in <em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. i. p. 133.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-117" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-117" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Wrongly dated 1623 in the MS.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-118" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-118" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Abraham van Uffelen succeeding Andries Souri.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-119" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-119" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Souri and Van Uffelen.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-120" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-120" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This appears to be intended for a nickname of Van Uffelen, implying that he aped the proud and arbitrary bearing of Governor-General Coen.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-121" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-121" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Maarten Isbrandszoon.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-122" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-122" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Torture applied by tying the offender to the end of a rope, hoisting him up, and then letting him fall a considerable distance, thus jerking him violently. The name is probably from the Italian <em>strappada</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-123" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-123" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In a subsequent letter (<em>see</em> p. 147) the penalties are given as 50 and 100 rials respectively.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-124" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-124" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">From the next letter it appears probable that the first part of this was written on the 19th.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-125" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-125" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the next letter.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-126" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-126" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on p. 106. Perhaps we should read ‘neare expired’.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-127" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-127" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Also called the <em>Maagd van Dort</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-128" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-128" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">They were no doubt relying on Linschoten, who, in describing the route taken by Portuguese vessels leaving Cochin for Europe, says that if the season be late they should ‘passe betwene the iland Brandaon and the iland of Lopo Soares’ (English edition of 1595, p. 318). His map shows ‘S. Brandaon’ as situated to the north-east of Rodriguez, but no such island exists. There seems to be some confusion with the fabled island of St. Brandan, usually placed in the North Atlantic.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-129" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-129" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The modern Pondicherri, the Tamil name of which is Puthuchēri and the Hindustānī Pulcharī.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-130" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-130" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably <em>kōmatis</em>, a trading caste. Methwold (<em>Purchas His Pilgrimage</em>, ed. 1626, p. 997) says: ‘The next tribe is there tearmed a Committy, and these are generally the merchants of this place, who, by themselves or their servants, travell into the countrey, gathering up callicoes from the weavers and other commodities, which they sell againe in greater parcels in the port townes to merchant strangers.’ This corrects the note on p. 47 of the previous volume.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-131" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-131" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This story was incorrect.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-132" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-132" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is given both as the date of dispatch and the date of receipt. Probably it should be the latter.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-133" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-133" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A safe-conduct or amnesty. The real meaning is ‘a written agreement’ (Arabic <em>qaul</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-134" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-134" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mr. Swinton suggests that this may be Chennapa Nāyak, the father of the chief from whom the English obtained the site of Fort St. George. Another surmise is that he was the ‘Echama Naique’, who in 1614–16 was fighting with Jaga Rājā on behalf of King Ranga IV (see Mr. Sewell’s <em>A Forgotten Empire</em>, pp. 226–31).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-135" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-135" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A letter from the Dutch chief at Masulipatam to Batavia, dated January 15, 1623, N. S. (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series 1. vol. vi) mentions the arrival at Pulicat of 'Iteragie', to whom the Dutch gave sixty or seventy rials, with a promise to help him with cannon in his campaign against the invaders.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-136" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-136" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably we should read 'at Salure and Mayer'. The former place may be Salūrpet, on the Pulicat Lake, about 30 miles N. W. of Pulicat (<em>cf.</em> p. 104); the latter may possibly be Medur, on the road to Ponnairy.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-137" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><strong class="fn-backlink fn-unlinked">1 · [Unlinked note, p. 142]</strong> <span class="fn-body">There is a second copy in <em>Factory Records, Masulipatam</em>, vol. ix. p. 8.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-138" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-138" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arrived?</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-139" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-139" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Kolis of Gujarāt, a predatory tribe who were the terror of travellers in those parts. It is generally supposed that our word ‘coolie’ (<em>cf.</em> pp. 66, 139) is derived from this name, as members of the tribe were largely employed in low-class work.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-140" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-140" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Ibrāhīmīs (see note on p. 351 of the previous volume).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-141" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-141" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sanskrit <em>valla</em>, Marāthī <em>vāla</em>: a goldsmith’s weight, of which twenty went to the <em>gadiānun</em> and forty to the <em>tolā</em>. Fryer, however, divides the <em>tolā</em> into two ‘gudjanas’ or thirty-two ‘valls’.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-142" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-142" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Gingelly Coast extended from the Godāvarī Delta to Jagannāth.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-143" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-143" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Della Valle says that in this fight the Dutch captured three Portuguese maidens, one of whom became the wife of a Dutch factor at Surat. On the Court Minutes of December 15, 1624, is noted an accusation that Captain Green kept the two Portuguese women a whole year, refused 600 rials for their ransom at Mozambique, gave them costly clothing, and finally left them at Jakatra.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-144" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><strong class="fn-backlink fn-unlinked">1 · [Unlinked note, p. 154]</strong> <span class="fn-body">S. <span class="unclear" title="Uncertain reading in source scan">radi</span></span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-145" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-145" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The date in the original is simply 'the fine [i. e. end] 1622'.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-146" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-146" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The MS. is dated '5th November', which is probably an error for 'fine [of] November'.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-147" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-147" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Wrongly dated the 9th in the MS.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-148" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-148" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Khān-i-Azam and Prince Dāwar Bakhsh, son of Khusrū.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-149" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-149" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the town of Sojitra, 16 miles north-east of Cambay. Della Valle calls it ‘Sozintra’.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-150" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-150" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">As prize money, in the event of any vessels being captured.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-151" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-151" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mahī Dās. The broker employed by the English at Surat.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-152" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-152" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For this and other events of the blockade see Crosby’s narrative (p. 3).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-153" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-153" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pahlawān Safīd, Governor of Olpād, near Surat. The <em>jāgīr</em> belonged to Khwāja Abūl Hasan (see the previous volume, pp. 282, 318), who was a faithful adherent of Jahāngīr and held a high command in the operations against the Prince.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-154" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-154" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Shābāsh (Pers. <em>shādbāsh</em>, ‘be joyful!’) is in India a common exclamation of approval. It may generally be translated by ‘Bravo!’ or ‘Well done!’ but it is also used (as here) to imply assent to something proposed.</span></li></ol></section>
